       The Alpöge–Claude proof of the Riemann hypothesis
            explained for the Riemann zeta function
                 Claude Fable 5.2, Codex Astra 6.2, and Gemini Carbon
                         at the behest of Michael O. Rubinstein

                               Working draft, 21 November 2026
   Abstract. We explain the argument of Alpöge and Claude for the Riemann hypothesis in
   the case of the Riemann zeta function. The analytic step shows that a one-sided bound for
   a smoothed sum over prime powers, valid throughout intervals [Y, Y 3/2 ] with Y arbitrarily
   large, excludes zeros to the right of the critical line. We derive this bound from one inequality
   between weighted sums over primes, the main inequality (M), and two estimates for Dirichlet
   characters. We prove the two estimates from theorems in the literature, and explain how the
   divisibility and the size of theta lifts enter (M). The proof of (M) is taken from the Lean
   development. This is a work in progress: Part III writes up parts of that proof and states
   exactly the inputs it takes from the Lean development; (M) itself is not proved here.



Note. This is a working guide to Alpöge’s Lean-verified proof of the Riemann hypothesis, specialised
to the Riemann zeta function. Its goal is a human-readable exposition of the proof in the style of
classical analytic number theory. It is a work in progress, written as an aid to studying the proof.
Written up so far: the deduction of the Riemann hypothesis from one inequality, called (M)
throughout, and two estimates for Dirichlet characters (Parts I and II); proofs of those two estimates
and the construction of two auxiliary sets (§III.1–III.3); the analytic part of the bound for the size of
the theta kernels (§III.4; four passages there are conditional on inputs from the Lean development);
a local divisibility estimate for power series at inert primes, under one hypothesis from the Lean
development, proved for orbits of length two and with a further duality input for longer orbits
(§III.5); the passage from that estimate to lattices: the lemmas on lattices are proved, the prime sum
is split and several of its terms are bounded (the bounds for the remaining loss sums are quoted), and
the theorem on the glued lattice is conditional on two statements of the Lean development (§III.6);
the deduction of the size inequality from eleven named inputs, and the value of an index-corrected
mean of periods under stated hypotheses (§III.7); the deduction of the dimension bound that is
used from three statements of the Lean development (on traces, ranks and pairings) and stated
conditions on sizes and subspaces, with a finite model of the trace estimates (§III.8); and the step
from the bound for Galois cohomology to a bound for denominators, with an account of how the
Lean development proves that bound (§III.9).
Not proved here, all of it inside (M), and taken from the Lean development: (1) the integrality of the
theta kernels in the coordinates in which the divisibility is proved, and the statement on valuations
built on it; (2) the arithmetic inputs of the size bound: the inner-product formula for the theta
lifts and its local factors, the covolumes of the lattices, the local index bounds and the passage
between levels; (3) the trace-formula estimates themselves; (4) the bound for Galois cohomology
itself; (5) the construction of the maps and their weights, and the assembly of (M), which in the
Lean development is a mean over a family of maps (§III.6 treats a model case only).
The table at the start of Part III indicates which statements are proved in these pages and which
are still taken without proof from the Lean development.

What this is. In November 2026 Levent Alpöge announced a proof, found with the AI system
Claude, of the Riemann hypothesis. It exists as a formal proof in the Lean proof assistant, about 17
million lines long. This paper explains how the argument works for the Riemann zeta function, for
a reader who knows a first course in analytic number theory: the prime number theorem, Dirichlet
characters, and Mellin transforms.
How it is organised. Part I is an exposition: the strategy first, then each step with its motivation
and with examples computed on real primes. Part II gives the details and proofs to which Part
I refers. A reader can stop after Part I and know how the proof goes; Part II is there to check
each step. Part III, which is being written, is to prove the statements that Parts I and II quote.
It proves Estimates B and C and constructs the two auxiliary sets of residue classes (the “sets of
columns” of §I.5). For the main inequality (M) it proves some steps, proves others conditionally on
statements of the Lean development which it names, and takes the rest from that development; the
table at the start of Part III says which.
What is proved here and what is not. The argument rests on three statements. One of
them, the main inequality (M), is the arithmetic heart of the matter. Two further estimates (B
and C) concern auxiliary Dirichlet characters. We state B and C exactly, and (M) in the form
in which it is used, and explain why one should expect them. That the three together imply the
Riemann hypothesis is proved in Part II. Estimates B and C are proved in Part III, from theorems
on Dirichlet L-functions that are cited there. But (M) is not yet proved here: we are working
through its proof in the Lean development and adding the details to Part III section by section. Of
what it needs, Part III so far constructs the two sets of columns (§III.3) and proves: the analytic
part of the bound for the size of the theta kernels, with four conditional passages (§III.4); a local
divisibility estimate for power series at inert primes, under the integrality of the theta kernels in the
coordinates of the height-two logarithm (hypothesis (B)), for orbits of length two and with a further

                                                          2
duality input for longer orbits (§III.5); the lemmas on lattices that carry this estimate to lattices
and part of the sum over primes, the main theorem there being conditional on two statements of the
Lean development (§III.6); that the size inequality follows from eleven named inputs (§III.7); that
the dimension bound follows from three statements of the Lean development on traces, ranks and
pairings (§III.8); and the step from the bound for Galois cohomology to a bound for denominators
(§III.9). Part II proves the elementary lemmas. The rest of (M) is for now quoted: hypothesis
(B) and the statement on valuations built on it, the arithmetic inputs of the size estimate, the
trace-formula estimates themselves, the bound for Galois cohomology itself, the construction of the
maps and their weights, and the assembly of (M) from these. Statements marked [quoted] are taken
from the Lean development without proof. The full proof of (M) is where almost all of the formal
development goes, and writing it up is the work in progress. Every numerical example, except
where stated, can be reproduced with the scripts listed under Computations, after Part III.



                             Part I. How the proof works


I.0    The strategy in one page
Chebyshev’s idea about counting primes. Chebyshev [Che52] bounded the number of primes
using the prime factorization of factorials. In the simplest     form of the argument, due to Erdős
[Erd32], one considers the middle binomial coefficient 2n   . It  is divisible by every prime between n
                                                          
                                                        n
and 2n, and it is at most 4 . So the product of those primes is at most 4n . That is an upper bound
                           n

for a count of primes, obtained from two facts about one integer: what divides it, and how big it is.
   One piece of notation. For a real number v we write v+ for its positive part: v+ = v if v > 0,
and v+ = 0 otherwise. For example 5+ = 5 and (−3)+ = 0. A sum of terms of the form (· · · )+ is
automatically cut off where the terms would become negative.
   The plan. The proof of the Riemann hypothesis described here is Chebyshev’s argument, run
with a far better “integer”. There are five steps.

 1. Zeros to primes. The Riemann hypothesis is equivalent to a bound |G(x)| ≤ Cx1/2+ε for
    a certain smoothed count of primes G(x), for every ε > 0, with C = C(ε). In fact an upper
    bound G(x) ≤ x1/2+ε is enough, and it is enough to have it on a sparse sequence of long
    intervals. (§I.1.)
 2. Why Chebyshev’s method stalls. For n! the divisibility is known exactly: the exponent of
    p is h ⌊n/ph ⌋. The floor functions are the obstacle; they are why the classical method gives
        P

    constants but not asymptotics. (§I.2.)
 3. A divisible object with no floor functions. Derivatives of functions on an elliptic
    curve with complex multiplication are divisible by primes to exponents of the smooth form
    a/p + a/p3 + · · · , but only at the primes that stay prime in the associated imaginary quadratic
    field. Subtracting a large weight w turns each term a/ph into (a/ph − w)+ = w (x/ph − 1)+
    with x = a/w: exactly the smooth kernel that G uses, magnified by w. (§I.3 and §I.4.)
 4. Size, to the precision needed. “Divisibility ≤ size” now gives an upper bound for a sum
    over primes. For it to be useful the size must be known not just to leading order a log a
    but through the term linear in a. This forces one to replace a single number by a linear
    map between lattices, built from theta series, whose size is governed by classical formulas

                                                  3
      (Chowla–Selberg, the mass formula). The resulting comparison of divisibility with size is the
      main inequality (M). (§I.5 and §I.7.)
 5. Clean up and balance. The sum in (M) runs over primes in certain residue classes. Averaging
    over the classes leaves G(x) plus three sums twisted by quadratic characters, which are small
    for well-chosen auxiliary primes. Repeating the construction m times leaves two competing
                                      √
    errors, x/m and m. Taking m ≈ x gives G(x) ≤ x1/2+ε . (§I.6 and §I.8.)

Logical skeleton.
                         §I.6                      §I.6                                          §I.1
      (M) , B, C −−−→ Estimate A −−−→ G(x) ≤ x1/2+ε on long intervals −−−→ RH.                                (I.1)
      |{z}    | {z }
   [quoted]   Part III


Three statements enter: the main inequality (M), which is quoted, and two estimates for auxiliary
Dirichlet characters, one at the scale x (Estimate B) and one at a much larger scale (Estimate C),
which are proved in Part III from cited theorems. The order of choices is this. For each small ε
there are arbitrarily large Y for which a single pair of auxiliary primes q, d serves for every x in
[Y, Y 3/2 ]; the rest of the construction is then made for each x separately.


I.1     From zeros to a sum over primes
The statistic. Let Λ(n) = log p if n = ph is a prime power and 0 otherwise. Define

where γ is Euler’s constant. Equivalently S(x) = n Λ(n)(x/n − 1)+ , where v+ = max(v, 0): with

the positive part, the condition n ≤ x under the sum can be omitted. Compared with Chebyshev’s
ψ(x) = n≤x Λ(n), the sum S weights n by the ramp (x/n − 1)+ : as a function    of n it is large for

small n, decreases, reaches 0 at n = x, and is 0 for n > x. Also S(x) = x 1x ψ(t) dt/t2 . The two

subtracted terms are the main term of S, so G is a remainder.

n−s /(s(s − 1)) for Re s > 1. Summing over n with weights Λ(n),

The last two terms cancel the pole at s = 1 (both terms of its principal part). What remains are
simple poles of F at the zeros ρ of ζ, with residue −1/(ρ(ρ − 1)) at a simple zero, and a pole at

the sum being over the nontrivial zeros. It converges absolutely, because of the factor ρ(ρ − 1);

                                                                                                  √
has real part 2 then |ρ(ρ − 1)| = ρ(1 − ρ), and therefore, for large x, |G(x) − log 2π| ≤ 0.0462 x.
              1

A zero with real part β > 12 would contribute a term of size xβ .


                                                              4
    A sanity check on real primes. Summing over the prime powers up to 2·106 gives G(104 ) = 1.749,
G(105 )√= 3.671, G(106 ) = 11.040, G(2 · 106 ) = −12.268, to be compared with log 2π = 1.838 and
0.0462 106 = 46.2.
    One side is enough. The terms of a zero off the line and of its complex conjugate together
form a real function of x that oscillates and takes both signs. So one expects a zero with real part β
to make G(x) exceed +cxβ for some arbitrarily large x. Thus an upper bound for G should already
exclude it. That an upper bound suffices follows from a theorem of Landau on Mellin transforms of
functions of one sign [Lan05]; see [MV07, §15.1].

Lemma (one-sided criterion). Let 0 < θ < 1. If G(x) ≤ xθ for all large x, then ζ(s) ̸= 0 for
Re s > θ.

Proof. The integral H(s) = 1∞ xθ − G(x) x−s−1 dx equals 1/(s − θ) − F (s), and its integrand is
                              R                 

nonnegative for large x. Landau’s theorem says that a Mellin transform of an eventually nonnegative
function has a singularity at the real point of its abscissa of convergence. But by (I.3) the function
H is regular at every real s > θ: the only real singularities of F in s > 0 would come from real
zeros of ζ in (0, 1), and there are none. Hence the abscissa is at most θ, the integral converges for
Re s > θ, and F is holomorphic there. A zero of ζ in that half-plane would be a pole of F .

   The arithmetic argument below produces the upper bound only for x in certain long intervals.
The lemma survives this weakening.

Lemma (the same, on intervals). Let 0 < θ < 1. If there are arbitrarily large Y such that G(x) ≤ xθ
for all x ∈ [Y, Y 3/2 ], then ζ(s) ̸= 0 for Re s > θ.

The proof replaces Landau’s theorem by an estimate of high derivatives of F , which see only one
interval at a time; it is one page and is given in full in Part II, §1.
    Conclusion of step 1. To prove the Riemann hypothesis it suffices to show: for every ε > 0
there are arbitrarily large Y with G(x) ≤ x1/2+ε throughout [Y, Y 3/2 ]. (Zeros come in pairs ρ, 1 − ρ,
so no zeros to the right of the line means none to the left.) From now on the goal is an upper bound
for a sum over primes.


I.2    Chebyshev’s method, and where it stalls
The method. Let N = 2n     n . Every prime p with n < p ≤ 2n divides (2n)! and not (n!) , so it
                                                                                               2
                              

divides N . Also N ≤ (1 + 1)2n = 4n . A positive integer divisible by distinct primes is at least their
product, so
                                       log p ≤ log N ≤ 2n log 2.                                   (I.5)
                                 X

                                  n<p≤2n

In general: if ∆ is a nonzero integer and pep divides ∆ for each prime p in some set, then

                                               ep log p ≤ log |∆|.                                 (I.6)
                                        X

                                           p

Divisibility is a statement about each prime separately; size is a statement about the real numbers.
The inequality (I.6) is the bridge, and it always points the same way: it bounds primes from above.
By step 1 that is the direction we need. This is the arithmetic analogue of Stepanov’s count of
zeros with multiplicity; see Part II, §2.


                                                      5
   The best classical integer. For ∆ = n! both sides are known exactly. Legendre’s formula
[Leg08] says the exponent of p in n! is h≥1 ⌊n/ph ⌋. Hence
                                       P

                                                              jnk
                                         log n! =          Λ(r)                                  (I.7)
                                                    X
                                                                      .
                                                    r≤n
                                                                  r

The left side is n log n − n + O(log n) by Stirling. If the floor were not there, the right side would
be n r≤n Λ(r)/r, which is S(n) + ψ(n): essentially our statistic.
    P

   Where it stalls. The floor is there. Writing ⌊n/r⌋ = n/r − {n/r}, the fractional parts
contribute r≤n Λ(r){n/r}, which is of size n: as large as the term −n on the left. All information
          P

about ψ(n) − n is hidden in the fractional parts, and (I.7) cannot separate it. This is why
Chebyshev’s method yields c1 n ≤ ψ(n) ≤ c2 n and no more.
   What would be needed. A nonzero “integer” ∆ such that:
(W1) the exponent of ph in ∆ is a smooth function of ph , like the ramp (x/ph − 1)+ , with no floor
     function, or with rounding that is negligible;
(W2) log |∆| is known with an error much smaller than its linear term, in fact smaller by a factor
     of about x−1/2 , where x is the scale at which the primes are tested (§I.1);
(W3) the primes that occur are all primes, or a set of primes we can relate to all primes.
The rest of the proof constructs such an object. No single integer does all three; the final object is
a linear map between lattices, and (I.6) becomes an inequality between the index of a lattice and
the norm of a map.

    Why. Near the origin the addition law of an elliptic curve is a formal group: a power series
X ⊕ Y R in two√variables. Its logarithm l(X) is the power series with l(X ⊕ Y ) = l(X) + l(Y ); here
l(t) = 0t du/ 1 − u4 and sl = l−1 . For a curve with complex multiplication and an inert prime p,
                                                            2
multiplication by p on the formal group looks like X 7→ X p modulo p, and the logarithm behaves
like the model series                              2     4
                                                Xp    Xp
                                   l(X) = X +        + 2 + ··· .                              (I.10)
                                                 p     p
(From here on we write a for the order of the derivative, as in the rest of the paper.) For this model
one proves by induction that the coefficient of z a in the inverse series has p-adic valuation at least
−(a − 1)/(p2 − 1) (Part II, §2). An ath derivative multiplies that coefficient by a!, whose valuation
is a/(p − 1) − O(log a). The difference is
                                 1     1        p    1 1      
                         a          − 2     =a· 2   = a + 3 + ··· .                                 (I.11)
                                 p−1 p −1      p −1     p p
Only odd powers of p appear. That is the signature of an inert prime. At a split prime the formal
group is of a different type (height one): its logarithm has the denominator pj already at degree pj ,
the inverse series loses about a/(p − 1), and this cancels the whole valuation of a!. There is no gain.
That an elliptic curve with complex multiplication has the first type at inert primes and the second
at split primes is a theorem of Deuring [Deu41]; for the shape of the logarithm see Honda [Hon70]
and Lubin–Tate [LT65].
    What this achieves, and what it does not. We have integers whose exponents are smooth
in p: wish (W1), up to a small amount of rounding. Three defects remain, and each drives a part
of the real proof.
 (D1) Rounding. An error of size about 1 in each exponent costs p≤n log p ≈ n in (I.6): again as
                                                                      P

      large as the linear term. This is the floor-function problem of §I.2 in another guise.
 (D2) Only half the primes. Only inert primes appear, weighted by 1 − χ(p), that is, 2 for inert p
      and 0 for split p, where χ is the quadratic character of the field (χ(p) = −1 for inert p).
 (D3) Size. |An | is about n!/Rn with R = 1.854 . . ., so log |An | ≈ n log n. But the inert primes
      account only for p≡3(4), p≤n np log p = 12 n log n + O(n). The inequality (I.6) is true and
                         P

      useless: it is off by a factor 2 in the leading term. (At n = 101: inert primes up to n
      contribute 167.7 of log |An | = 306.8.)
For the objects of the real proof, the divisibility at inert primes is written up in §III.5, conditionally
on the integrality of the theta kernels in the coordinates of the height-two logarithm (hypothesis
(B)), which is taken from the Lean development.


I.4     Repairing the three defects
The lemniscate numbers An were derivatives of a function attached to an elliptic curve. The real
proof keeps the idea differentiate a function of arithmetic origin and look at what divides the result,
but changes the function. This section says which functions are differentiated, what “differentiating”


                                                    7
means for them, and what each choice achieves. It has four parts: the functions (§I.4.1), then the
repair of (D3), (D1) and (D2) in that order (§I.4.2–I.4.4), and finally a list of what the reader can
and cannot check directly (§I.4.5).


I.4.1   What is differentiated: theta series with a variable

The simplest case. Let Λ = Z[i] ⊂ C, the lattice of Gaussian integers, and put

                              Θ(z) =         exp − π|λ|2 + λz           (z ∈ C).                        (I.12)
                                       X                        

                                       λ∈Λ

The Gaussian factor makes the sum converge for every z, so Θ is an entire function. Its nth
derivative at the origin is
                                                          2
                               Tn = Θ(n) (0) =   λn e−π|λ| .                          (I.13)
                                               X

                                                          λ∈Λ

So differentiating n times at the origin means inserting the weight λn into the sum over the lattice.
                                                                            2
More generally, for a polynomial P one has P ( dz d
                                                    )Θ(0) = λ P (λ)e−π|λ| : a weighted count of
                                                              P

lattice points. Because Λ is preserved by multiplication by i, the sum Tn vanishes unless 4 | n.
    Three things visible in this example. They can all be checked by direct computation
(script e6_hecke_theta.py).
                                     √                                                  √
  1. A period. T0 = 1.18034 . . . = 2 ϖ/π (a classical evaluation), where ϖ = 2 01 dt/ 1 − t4 =
                                                                                   R

     2.62205 . . . is the lemniscate constant: the period of the curve y 2 = 1 − x4 . And T4 /T0 =
     0.12131 . . . = 14 (ϖ/π)4 .
 2. Rationality. After division by T0 and by (ϖ/π)n , the values are rational numbers:

                   T4k
        rk =              :     r1 = 14 ,     r2 = 17

    The rk were identified numerically: for k ≤ 30 the value agrees with a rational number of small
    denominator to 250 digits. The reason is the identity Θ(z)/T0 = σ(u)2 ℘(u) with u = ϖz/2π,
    where σ and ℘ are the Weierstrass functions of the lattice with g2 = 4, g3 = 0, whose Taylor
    coefficients are rational; this identity was observed numerically, to more than 400 digits, and
    we have not written out a proof of it. It gives rk with denominator 4k . That lattice sums
    of this kind are algebraic multiples of powers of a period is a classical theorem; compare
    Damerell [Dam70]. So derivatives of Θ are arithmetic objects, once the period is removed.
    Each derivative costs one factor ϖ/π; this is why a term linear in the order of differentiation,
    −a log Ω, appears in the size estimate of §I.7.
                                                                                        2

    root of a factorial, not a factorial. Numerically, log |T120 | = 119.0, while 12 log(120!/π 120 ) =

   The general case. In the proof, Λ is replaced by a lattice of the following kind. Let E be a
number field with complex multiplication (it is specified in §I.4.4 and in Part II, §6.1), and let L,
L′ be lattices over its ring of integers, of ranks r and r′ , each with a positive definite Hermitian
form. The role of Λ is played by the lattice of linear maps from L to L′ . The variable z becomes a
complex r × r′ matrix, one for each embedding of E in C from a fixed set of 2n embeddings (a
CM type; 2n is half the degree of E, and n is the number of columns of the table of §I.5). The

                                                      8
theta series ΘL,L′ (z) is a holomorphic function of all these matrix entries, and one applies to it a
differential operator P (∂), where P is a polynomial in the entries:

             P (∂) ΘL,L′ (0) =                         ΘL,L′ (z) =                       P (z) =                  (I.15)
                                 X                                        X                        X
                                     Pν ν! cν ,                               cν z ν ,                 Pν z ν .
                                 ν                                        ν                        ν

Here ν runs over exponent vectors and ν! is the product of the factorials of its entries. As in item 2
above, these numbers become algebraic only after the period has been removed; in the proof this is
done by writing the theta series in algebraic coordinates from the start. The numbers (I.15), so
normalised, are the divisible objects of the proof [quoted].
   Why differentiate at all? There are three separate reasons.
   • It is forced by the construction. The numbers (I.15), for suitable P , are exactly the entries of
     the matrix of a theta lift (§I.7) between spaces of forms of prescribed weights. The degree of
     P at a given embedding is one of the parameters (weights) of these spaces. We call it the
     order a, and reserve the word weight for the parameters w of §I.4.3. It can be chosen freely,
     and it will be chosen huge.
   • Differentiation creates divisibility. This is the mechanism of §I.3: in a suitable algebraic
     coordinate the function has p-integral Taylor coefficients, an ath derivative multiplies a
     coefficient by a!, and at an inert prime the net gain is a (1/p + 1/p3 + · · · ).
   • Differentiation costs little in size. By the Gaussian computation in item 3 above, an ath
     derivative has squared size about a!.
    A warning, with numbers. The simplest theta function above does not have the full
divisibility. At n = 120 the exponent of 3 in r30 , as identified above, is 14, whereas n · 83 = 45. (For
the lemniscate sine of §I.3 it was the full amount; for that function the size was too large.) Having
both properties at once, full divisibility at inert primes and Gaussian size, is a theorem about the
particular theta series used in the proof, written in particular coordinates. It is the deepest local
statement in the argument, and it is quoted, not reproved, here [quoted]. (See §III.5 for what is
proved: the divisibility at inert primes is written up in §III.5, conditionally on the integrality of the
theta kernels in the coordinates of the height-two logarithm (hypothesis (B)), which is taken from
the Lean development.)

prime, d ≡ 3 mod 4, and the inert primes are those inert in Q( −d); see §I.4.4. √    After the period
normalisation the entries (I.15) are algebraic numbers, in a field containing Q( −d). If such a
number β is a nonzero algebraic integer divisible by pe , for any prime p, then the average of
log |σ(β)|2 over the complex embeddings σ of the field is at least 2e log p. This is the product
formula. (At a single embedding it can fail, because of units; only the average is controlled.) In an
imaginary quadratic field there is just one pair of embeddings and the statement is elementary: if
β = pe β0 with β0 a nonzero integer of that field, then |β|2 = p2e |β0 |2 ≥ p2e . So divisibility by pe
contributes 2e log p to the logarithm of the squared size.

a being computed exactly in §I.7. The leading terms now agree, and the comparison has become
a statement about the terms of order a. Compare (D3): there the size was log |An |2 ≈ 2n log n
against the same n log n from divisibility.


I.4.3   Repair of (D1): a huge order, and a weight

What the exponent really is. For the entries (I.15) the exponent of an inert prime is not
a (1/p + 1/p3 + · · · ) but a modification of it. Fix one of the 2n embeddings; call it the place. Let a
be the order (the degree of P ) at that place. Then, schematically,
                                                        X a                
              exponent of an inert prime p ≥                       h
                                                                     − wh           − (small losses),   (I.19)
                                                       h odd
                                                               p                +

where wh > 0 is a weight attached to another place: the one reached from the given place by the
Frobenius automorphism of ph followed by complex conjugation. The display keeps only the main
term. The statement proved in the source is an inequality for sums of such exponents over the m
lifts and over the places, with several loss terms: from the digits of the integers involved, from
repeated visits of a Frobenius orbit to the same place, and from indices of lattices (Part II, §6.4)
[quoted] (see §III.5 for what is proved).
    The Frobenius and the places. The field E lies inside a field generated by roots of unity.
The Frobenius of p is the automorphism of E induced by raising those roots of unity to the pth
power. It permutes the 4n embeddings of E, and it depends only on residue classes of p. The
2n places are
           √ chosen embeddings, one from each pair of complex conjugates. At a prime that is
inert in Q( −d) an odd power of the Frobenius carries each chosen embedding to one that is not
chosen; composing with complex conjugation brings it back. This is the reason for the conjugation
above. Since the Frobenius depends only on a residue class of ph , residue classes of prime powers
will appear in §I.5.
    Where the subtraction of w comes from. The polynomial P is a product, over the 2n
places, of minors (determinants of sub-matrices) of the matrix variable at each place. Its shape at

                                                       10
each place is described by a diagram of boxes. At the chosen place the diagram has a first row
of length about a. At another place τ ′ the polynomial contains about wτ ′ minors, where wτ ′ is
the weight parameter at τ ′ . At the scale ph the Frobenius links the chosen place to the place τh ,
and each minor there can absorb only one derivative in a linked direction: in det(X + sb, Y + tb)
there is no term in st, s2 or t2 . Each derivative absorbed at τh costs one unit of the gain at the
scale ph . There are about wh minors at τh , each absorbing at most one, and at most a/ph units
are available; so the loss at that scale is at most min(a/ph , wh ), and what survives is (a/ph − wh )+
(Part II, (6.1)). In the simplest case (a single index; Part II, (6.1a)) the loss comes from the digits
of the integers involved, by Legendre’s formula, and is of size O((log a)2 ) for each prime (Part II,
§6.4). The losses in the statement the source proves are larger and of several kinds, as said above.
    Example. Take a = 81, p = 3, and wh = 2 for all h.

                                     h                 1        3           5         7
                                     p   h
                                                       3    27            243      2187
                                    a/ph              27     3           0.33      0.04
                                  a/ph − w            25     1          −1.67     −1.96
                                (a/ph − w)+           25     1              0         0

The exponent is at least 25 + 1 = 26, up to the losses. Every term with ph > a/w = 40.5 is zero.
    The choice of a and w, and what it achieves. Let x be the scale at which we want
information about primes. Put

                                b = exp(xε ),               w ≈ b,              a ≈ xb.          (I.20)

Then a/w ≈ x, and the elementary identity
                             a                      x            
                                    −w           =w         −1                  (x = a/w)        (I.21)
                               ph            +         ph               +

shows what has been gained. (In the example, x = 40.5 and for ph = 3 the right side is
2 · (40.5/3 − 1) = 25.)
   • Only prime powers up to x are involved, at the places that carry the weight b. The right
     side of (I.21) vanishes for ph ≥ x. Although we differentiate about xb times, the primes that
     matter at those places are those below x. (At the places with small weights the cut-off is
     much larger; see below.)
   • The kernel is the ramp of §I.1. The factor (x/ph − 1)+ is exactly the weight with which prime
     powers enter G(x). This is where the smoothing in the definition of G comes from.
   • Rounding becomes invisible. Each prime power below x now contributes about b times its
     ramp value. The bound (I.19) is used only where its main term is positive; elsewhere one
     uses only that the exponent is not negative, which is the integrality of the entries. The loss
     terms are of lower order, and after dividing the whole inequality by b they are absorbed in
     the error of (M). The precise accounting is part of the quoted statement. This removes defect
     (D1), and with it the floor-function problem of §I.2.
    Why b must be exponentially large. To defeat rounding, a power of x would do. One
other step needs more. There are 2n places and each needs a weight, but only some of them can be
given the weight b (the columns that carry it form the set T2 constructed in §III.3). The others
are given very small weights w′ , so that they test primes at a scale a/w′ ≥ 13 exp(xε/3 ); only at

                                                           11
such a scale is the distribution of primes in the relevant progressions known unconditionally with a
good error term (§I.6(b)). The weights in the construction are positive integers, so w′ ≥ 1; this
forces a ≥ 13 exp(xε/3 ), and hence b exponential in a power of x. The size of b also enters the
control of denominators (§I.7). What is bounded there is a weighted sum of logarithms of orders,
  ℓ log√ℓ  a hℓ,a in the notation of Part II, §6.8, and b is chosen so that the available bound is at
P         P

most b. After division by b this is negligible.


I.4.4   Repair of (D2): characters, and a table

Only inert primes: use a character. Let d be    √ a prime with d ≡ 3 mod 4 and let χd (r) = (r/d)
be the Legendre symbol. The primes inert in Q( −d) are those with χd (p) = −1. A sum over odd
powers of inert primes is a sum over all prime powers with the coefficient 1 − χd (r):

treated separately, with coefficient log d (Part II, §4.1). So the places can be arranged in a table,
with two rows (the value of χq ) and n columns (the residue class), and each prime power is assigned
to a cell of the table. The inequality that comes out of the construction, stated in §I.5, is a bound
for a sum over prime powers in which each prime power carries the weight of its cell. §I.6 then
removes the dependence on the cell by averaging.

I.5     The inequality that comes out
Here is the statement. It has the form: there exist data (weights, two sets of columns, integers m,
k) with the properties listed, such that the inequality (M) holds. The properties are given here in
outline and exactly in Part II, §4.2–4.3. The statement looks elaborate; the example after it shows
that it is elementary to state.
    Data. Fix a small ε > 0 and a scale x. Let q ≡ 3 mod 8 and d ≡ 7 mod 16 be primes with
q | d + 1 (how they are chosen: §I.6). They are very large: if x lies in the interval [Y, Y 3/2 ], then
log q is about Y ε/5 and log d about Y ε/5 (log Y )2 . Let n be a power of 2 with x2+8ε ≤ n ≤ 2x2+8ε ,
and N = 4n. Let H be the group of odd residue classes modulo N with r and −r identified; it has
n elements, written [r]. Let b = exp(xε ).
   The table. Picture a table with two rows v ∈ {0, 1} and n columns indexed by H. An odd
prime power r = ph , p ̸= q, is assigned to the cell

                               row v(r) = 12 (1 + χq (r)),         column [r].                               (I.24)

The 2n cells are the 2n places of §I.4.3. Each cell i carries a positive weight wi . A cell of row 1 is
distinguished when its column lies in a set of columns of density δ; its weight is then (very nearly)
b. All other weights are tiny by comparison: so small that a/wi ≥ 13 exp(xε/3 ).
   The prime sum and its main term. For a derivative order a with |a/b − x| ≤ 1 (it depends
on the label t introduced below, and is written at in Part II), put
                                                                  a                   
                          P =                1 − χd (ph ) log p                                              (I.25)

                         M = a log a − 1 − γ +           (1, χd ) − ⟨log w⟩ ,                                (I.26)
                                                      L
where ⟨log w⟩ is the average of log wi over the 2n cells. P is the divisibility side: by (I.21) it is a
sum of ramps, one for each cell, at the scale a/wi . M is its expected value if primes were perfectly
distributed among the cells (§I.6(a); proved in Part II, §5.1). So P − M measures the irregularity
of primes in the cells of the table, at the scales a/wi .

                                                      13
   The primes 2 and q can be omitted without changing anything: d ≡ 7 mod 8 gives χd (2) = 1,
and q | d + 1 gives χd (q) = 1 by quadratic reciprocity, so the coefficient 1 − χd vanishes on their
powers. This is what the congruence conditions on q and d are for (Part II, §4.1). (The powers of d
do occur, with coefficient log d, but only at the large scale, since d > x.)
    The inequality. The construction depends on three further choices: a translate u ∈ H (which
moves the distinguished cells to other columns), a label t in a set T ⊂ H (which shifts the column
of every prime power), and an index j = 1, . . . , m numbering the m repetitions. Write Pu,t,j , Mu,t,j
accordingly.

Here B = |T | m b δ/2 is the natural normalisation (δ is the proportion of distinguished columns), k
is a further parameter of the construction (a rank), and C is an absolute constant. The inequality
is asserted for x ≥ x0 (ε) [quoted]. In words: on average over translates, the primes are not more
numerous than expected in the table, up to the four errors shown. It is an upper bound, as every
instance of (I.6) is. The set T and the set of distinguished columns (called T2 in Parts II and III)
are constructed in Part III, §III.3; how the construction uses them is part of the quoted statement.
     More precisely: let Y be large, let q, d be the primes of Estimate B for Y (§I.6(e)), and let
Y ≤ x ≤ Y 3/2 . Then the construction provides m = x1/2+O(ε) , k = x9/2+O(ε) , sets T , T2 with
|T2 |/n = δ ≥ x−ε and |T |/n ≥ 21 x−ε/8 , orders at and weights as above, for which (M) holds. The
constants C and x0 (ε) do not depend on Y , q, d.
     A table small enough to write out. Take q = 3, d = 23, N = 16, so n = 4 and
H = {[1], [3], [5], [7]}. The prime 7 is a square modulo 3 and 7 ≡ 7 mod 16: it goes in row 1, column
[7], with coefficient 2 log 7 because 7 is a non-square modulo 23. The primes up to 61 with non-zero
coefficient fall as follows.
                               v\column [1] [3]     [5]    [7]

With x = 1000, b = 10, a = 104 , weight 10 in cell (1, [1]) and 0.05 elsewhere, summing over the
prime powers gives P = 95461.616 and M = 95444.464: the excess P − M is 17.152, against a main
term of nearly 105 . Moving the distinguished cell to the other three columns gives excesses 368.21,
−129.25, −204.65. The mean of the four excesses is 12.86. (The example ignores one feature of
the construction, that the weights are integers. The identities it illustrates hold for any positive
weights.)


I.6    From the table to G(x)
This step is classical analytic number theory, and it is proved in full, from (M), B and C, in Part II,
§4–5. Here are the ideas.
    (a) Each cell is an arithmetic progression. The prime powers in a given cell are those
in one pair of residue classes ±c modulo N with a√prescribed value of χq ; the coefficient 1 − χd
then selects the odd powers of primes inert in Q( −d). For fixed moduli and scale tending to
infinity, the prime number theorem in progressions gives each cell the fraction 1/(2n) of the primes,
half of them inert, and M is defined as the resulting main term, with its constants. The identity

                                                  14
P − M = i wi Ri (a/wi ), where Ri (y) is the remainder of cell i at scale y (Part II, (5.2)), is then
           P

exact (Part II, (5.3)). No uniformity is claimed at this point. At the scale x there are far more
cells than primes, and a single Ri (x) need not be small; the argument uses only the average over
translates in (c). At the large scale the uniformity is Estimate C.
   (b) Two scales. A cell with weight wi tests primes up to a/wi . For a distinguished cell this is
a/b ≈ x: the scale we care about. For every other cell it is at least 13 exp(xε/3 ), an enormous scale.
Why arrange this? The construction needs weights on all 2n cells, but we want information at scale
x only. At the enormous scale the distribution of primes in these progressions can be controlled
with a saving better than any power of x. This is Estimate C below. It rests on the classical
zero-free region for Dirichlet L-functions; it is available because log q and log d are much smaller
than xε/3 , and because q and d are chosen so that no exceptional real zero interferes. So those cells
contribute a negligible amount. In the example they account for 4.18 of the mean excess 12.86.
   (c) Averaging over translates. As u runs over H, the distinguished cells visit every column
equally often. So the average over u no longer sees the residue class modulo N . It sees only the
two quadratic conditions: row 1 means χq (r) = 1, and the coefficient is 1 − χd (r). The average of
the distinguished part is therefore
                 δb X                             x    
                       Λ(r) 1 + χq (r) 1 − χd (r)     −1        minus its main term.             (I.28)
                                      
                 2 r≤x                              r

In the example this accounts for the other 8.68 of the 12.86.
    (d) Expanding the product. Since (1 + χq )(1 − χd ) = 1 + χq − χd − χq χd , the last expression
   2 Rq,d (x) with
is δb
                      Rq,d (x) = G(x) + Gχq (x) − Gχd (x) − Gχq χd (x),                      (I.29)
                                                                                ′
where, for a primitive character χ =
                                   ̸ 1, Gχ (x) = r≤x Λ(r)χ(r)(x/r − 1) + x LL (1, χ) is the analogue
                                                 P

of G for L(s, χ). The function G appears with coefficient +1. (The construction gives this at the
scale a/b, with |a/b − x| ≤ 1; the passage to x costs O(1 + log x), see Part II, §5.4.) Dividing (M)
by B, and using Estimates B and C below to bound the error terms (Part II, §5.4), gives:

Estimate A. Let Y be large, q, d the primes of Estimate B, and Y ≤ x ≤ Y 3/2 . For the parameters
of the construction, with m ≈ x1/2 , k ≈ x9/2 , n ≈ x2+8ε ,
                                 x       xm       k        ′
                Rq,d (x) ≤ xCε        +      + m + 2 ≤ x1/2+C ε           (x ≥ x0 (ε)).          (I.30)
                                  m        k      n

    (e) The three twisted sums. To pass from Rq,d to G we need:

Estimate B. Let 0 < ε < 52    1
                                . For all large Y there are primes q ≡ 3 mod 8, d ≡ 7 mod 16 with
q | d + 1, log q ≍ Y , log d ≍ Y ε/5 (log Y )2 , such that |Gχ (x)| ≤ x1/2+ε for χ = χq , χd , χq χd
                      ε/5

and all x in the interval [Y, Y 3/2 ]. (Proved in Part III as Theorem III.1.1(d), in a stronger form:
exponent 12 + 2ε , and any interval [Y, Y A ].)

Estimate C. For the same primes q, d and every x ∈ [Y, Y 3/2 ], Y large, every one of the 4n
primitive Dirichlet characters χ that occur when the cells of the table are expanded in characters
(their conductors divide 4nqd) satisfies |Gχ (y)| ≤ y exp(−κxε/8 ) for all y ≥ 13 exp(xε/3 ), with a
constant κ > 0; here Gχ = G for the trivial character. (Proved in Part III as Theorem III.2.1,
where κ is called c′ and may be any positive number once x is large. The proof uses a further
property of q and d, part (e) of Theorem III.1.1.)


                                                  15
    Is this circular? It looks like assuming the Riemann hypothesis for three L-functions. It is not,
for a reason of scale. The moduli q and d are astronomically larger than x: their logarithms are
                                                                         ′
powers of x. The quantity Gχ (x) has two parts. One is the constant LL (1, χ), an infinite sum over
prime powers. The other is the finite sum over r ≤ x. The tail of the constant beyond Z = Y 600 is
shown to be small using a zero-density estimate for zeros close to the line Re s = 1; this discards a
small proportion of the moduli, and uses nothing about zeros near Re s = 12 . What remains is a
sum over r ≤ Z, which is still very short compared with q and d. Mean-square estimates over the
                                                                                            √
modulus show that for most pairs (q, d) such a sum is as small as a random sum, about x. One
only needs one good pair. For C see (b) above. Estimates B and C are proved in Part III, from
standard theorems on Dirichlet L-functions that are cited there.
                                                                                                        ′′
   Conclusion. From A and B (A itself uses (M), B and C): G(x) ≤ Rq,d (x) + 3x1/2+ε ≤ x1/2+C ε
on the interval. By §I.1 this proves the Riemann hypothesis.


I.7     Where the main inequality (M) comes from
This is the part quoted from the Lean development; Part III has begun its proof: §III.4 (the
analytic part of item 2 below) and §III.5 (item 1 below, conditionally on one hypothesis from the
Lean development). Sections III.6–III.9 prove further steps, each from named statements of the
Lean development, which are quoted: the passage from derivatives to lattices, in part (§III.6); the
deduction of the size inequality from eleven inputs (§III.7); the deduction of the dimension bound
of item 3 from three statements (§III.8); and the step from the bound for Galois cohomology to the
bound for denominators (§III.9). The table at the start of Part III says what each section proves,
proves conditionally, or quotes. The construction of the maps and weights and the assembly of (M)
are not begun, and (M) is still quoted. We describe the objects and the three things that have to
be proved about them.
   Theta series. A theta series counts representations by a quadratic form. For the form a2 + b2 ,
                                   2   2
                Θ(z) =           sa +b = 1 + 4s + 4s2 + 4s4 + 8s5 + · · · ,    s = e2πiz .          (I.31)
                          X

                         a,b∈Z

Theta series have two properties at once: they are sums over a lattice, so their coefficients are
integers with arithmetic meaning; and they are Gaussian in nature (the terms have absolute value
       2   2
e−2πy(a +b ) , where y is the imaginary part of z), so their sizes are governed by Gaussian integrals.
These are the two properties needed in §I.4: divisibility of derivatives (§I.4.1) and Gaussian size
(the repair of (D3), §I.4.2).
    A theta lift is a map   defined by a theta series in two sets of variables, used as the kernel of
an integral: (T f )(h) = Θ(g, h)f (g) dg. (It is conjugate-linear in f .) In the case used here both
                         R

groups are definite unitary groups, for which everything is finite: a “function on the group” is a
function on a finite set of lattices L1 , . . . , Lc (the classes in a genus), the integral is a finite sum
weighted by 1/|Aut Li |, and T is a matrix. Its entries are derivatives, at the origin, of the theta
series attached to pairs of lattices. So a theta lift is a matrix of derivatives of theta functions,
between two spaces that carry natural integer lattices (functions with integer values) and natural
norms.
   The role of (I.6). Let T : L → M be an injective linear map between lattices in Euclidean
spaces. If T (L) ⊂ pe M for some prime power, then, comparing volumes,

                                   e log p ≤ log ∥T ∥ + µmax (M ) − µ(L),                           (I.32)


                                                     16
where ∥T ∥ is the operator norm and µ = − rank 1
                                                  log(covolume) is the slope of a lattice (µmax : the
largest slope of a sublattice). For L = M = Z this is (I.6). In the construction the lattices are
modules over the integers of a field with complex multiplication, and one works with squared norms:
(I.32) is doubled, so that 2e log p is compared with log ∥T ∥2 , as in §I.4. Three things must be
established.
     1. Divisibility. The matrix of the lift is divisible by pe with e of the form h odd (a/ph − w)+
                                                                                    P

at inert primes. The mechanism is that of §I.3, applied to theta functions of lattices over a field
with complex multiplication: derivatives of order a gain a(1/p + 1/p3 + · · · ). The subtraction of w
has an elementary source, explained in §I.4.3: each minor can absorb only one derivative in a given
direction. Composing m lifts multiplies the matrices, so the exponents add: this is the analogue of
a function vanishing to order m in Stepanov’s proof of the Riemann hypothesis for curves over finite
fields. The divisibility at inert primes is written up in §III.5, conditionally on the integrality of the
theta kernels in the coordinates of the height-two logarithm (hypothesis (B)), which is taken from
the Lean development. The passage from this estimate to lattices is the subject of §III.6. Proved
there: the lemmas on slopes of lattices, the form of the prime sum and its splitting by classes of
primes, and bounds for several of its terms. The theorem on the glued lattice is conditional on two
statements of the Lean development. The bounds for the eleven loss sums and for their total are
quoted, and so is the statement on valuations itself.
    2. Size. The squared norm of the lift has logarithm at most a(log a − 1 − log ΩE ) + · · · , with
the linear term known exactly. The leading term is the Gaussian integral ∥z a ∥2 = a!. The constants
Ω and ΩE are periods: Ω belongs to an elliptic curve, and ΩE , defined below, to the field E. A
derivative with respect to the algebraic invariant differential and one with respect to the analytic
variable differ by a constant factor, the period; an ath derivative acquires its ath power, and Ω is
the square of that factor. Its value is
                                                   2π L′
                                   log Ω = γ + log √ − (1, χd ).                                   (I.33)
                                                    d L
In this form √
             the identity is Kronecker’s limit formula; Ω is then a geometric mean, over the ideal
classes of Q( −d), of squared periods of the corresponding elliptic curves. Expressing it through
values of the Gamma function is the formula of Lerch and of Chowla and Selberg [CS67], whose
name the source uses. For the field Q(i), with d replaced by 4 and χd by the character modulo
4, exponentiating this formula gives Ω = 2π(ϖ/π)2 = Γ( 14 )4 /(4π 2 ) exactly: that is 2π times the
square of the factor ϖ/π met in §I.4.1. The constant Ω above is the period for the elliptic curve.
The constant that enters the size bound for the field E is ΩE , with
                                             rdE          2π  L′
                        log ΩE = log Ω − log √ = γ + log     − (1, χd ),                           (I.34)
                                               d         rdE  L
where rdE is the root discriminant of E. Part II, §6.6 gives both normalisations; the cancellation of
the linear terms is the same. This is how γ and L′ /L(1, χd ) enter the main term M . They are the
same constants that appear in the prime sum, by §I.6(a); that the two sides produce the same linear
term is the reason the method can work at all. The remaining linear terms come from the slopes in
(I.32): the number of lattice classes counted with weights 1/|Aut| (the Siegel mass formula) and the
dimensions of the spaces (the Weyl dimension formula). After an exact cancellation of factorials,
the total is M .
   3. Denominators and rank. Two things can spoil (I.32).
   • Denominators. The lift need not map integer-valued functions to integer-valued functions; one
     must find the right lattices. They are assembled prime by prime, using the freedom to twist

                                                   17
      the lift by characters, and what cannot be removed is measured by√certain Galois cohomology
      groups of the field (generalisations of class groups). A bound, by b, for the logarithm of the
      product of the orders of these groups (over all primes ℓ and all twists up to x400 ) is proved
      separately in the Lean development (it is quoted here); it uses the theory of automorphic
      forms and Galois representations, and is a large part of the formal development. Part III,
      §III.9 proves the step from this bound to the bound for denominators, and the vanishing of
      the groups in some cases over Q from cited theorems; the bound itself is quoted, with an
      account of its proof in the Lean development.
  • Rank. A single map of small rank controls only the maximal slope of its target, which can be
    far from the average and would lose the main term. One therefore uses many maps. Their
    images cannot all be perpendicular to one another: the dimensions of the images add up to
    more than the dimension of the target. What is shown is a bound for their total overlap (a
    second moment; a trace formula gives it). It says that the images are spread evenly: the
    weighted sum, over the maps, of the adjoint of the map followed by the map is close in mean
    square to a multiple of the identity of the target. A lemma of linear algebra then says the
    images fill almost the whole target, so that the mean slope is controlled. Part III, §III.8
    proves that the dimension bound of this lemma follows from three statements of the Lean
    development (on traces, ranks and pairings) and stated size conditions; the three statements,
    and the theorems behind them, are quoted.
    The elementary parts. Several steps in this section are elementary and are proved, with
worked examples, in Part II, §6: the persistence of integrality through a chain of maps; the minor
congruence; the factorial count (from Legendre’s formula) giving the gain a p/(p2 − 1); the Gaussian
norm; the cancellation that leaves M ; the elementary slope inequality; and the linear-algebra lemma
on images that are spread evenly in this sense. The analytic part of the size estimate is proved
in part in Part III, §III.4: the Gaussian bound for one block, for one map and for a chain, with
four passages conditional on inputs from the Lean development. Part III, §III.7 proves that the
size inequality (III.75) follows from eleven named inputs, and that an index-corrected mean of
periods equals log ΩE under hypotheses stated there. The eleven inputs are quoted, among them
the inner-product formula, the covolumes and the local index bounds. So the size bound itself (Part
II, (6.5), first line) remains quoted.


I.8    Why the exponent is one half
After division by B, the errors in (M) are, up to factors xCε ,

                                     x      xm              k
                                                      m        .                              (I.35)
                                     m       k              n2
  • x/m: errors made once, in the sizes of the lattices at the two ends of the chain of maps.
    The gain from divisibility is collected m times, so these are divided by m. This is why one
    composes many lifts.
  • m: errors accumulated over the m steps. The budget of each step (ranks, weights, denomina-
    tors) itself grows in proportion to m, so the total is of order m2 before normalisation; the
    factor m contained in B leaves m.
  • xm/k and k/n2 : costs of the auxiliary parameters k (the rank) and n (the size of the table).
                                                                                            √
    With k ≈ x9/2 and n ≈ x2 the first is negligible (x−3 ) and the second is again of order x.


                                                 18
                                               √                   √
The terms x/m and m are balanced when m ≈ x, and both equal x. That is where the exponent
2 comes from. If the second cost were m instead of m after normalisation, one would have
1                                            2

x/m + m2 , balanced at m = x1/3 , and the same analytic argument would give only that ζ(s) ̸= 0
for Re s > 23 , provided the corresponding inequality could be proved with the other errors below
x2/3+ε . (Whether such an inequality can be proved without the estimate for Galois cohomology is
a separate question, which we do not address.) So the precision of the comparison of lattices is
decisive.


I.9       Dictionary with Alpöge’s announcement
The announcement1 lists twenty steps, in the order in which they were found between 6 October
and 16 November 2026. It is a record of discovery, so it contains earlier formulations and motivating
ideas as well as steps of the final argument. This section says where each step appears in this paper.
The correspondence is our reading. For steps 1, 2, 5 and 7–20, and for the Poincaré bundle in step
3, it rests on statements of the Lean development that were read in the course of this work. Steps 4
and 6, and the rest of step 3, were not found in the Lean development and are matched from the
description in the announcement.

Steps that appear here in another form, or only in part.

• Step 2 (the invariant derivation D on a curve with complex multiplication satisfies Dp ≡ 0
  at an inert prime; divisibility of Da Ω · θ(0)). This is the idea of §I.3 and §I.4.1: derivatives
  on such a curve, the period (ϖ in §I.4.1, Ω in §I.7), and exponents involving odd powers of
  p only. We could not confirm the stated exponent on the simplest example: for the theta
  function of Z[i] the exponent we computed is smaller (§I.4.1). The object or its normalisation
  in the announcement must differ from that model. For what is proved in this paper about
  the divisibility at an inert prime, see Part III, §III.5.
• Steps 4 and 6 (positivity W (f ) ≥ 0 in Weil’s explicit formula, obtained from a determinant;
  a zero-free region from such a positivity). These are an earlier form of our first step. In the
  final argument the statement “an upper bound excludes zeros” is the Landau-type lemma of
  §I.1.
• Step 7 (determinants containing Gan–Gross–Prasad periods; interlacing weights). In the
  Lean development the period pairing at the end of the chain of lifts is identified with a Bessel
  period (§I.7; Part II, §6.2 and §6.8; source file [19]), and a condition of interlacing on the
  weights governs the passage between consecutive unitary groups (source file [23]). We do not
  use the Gan–Gross–Prasad terminology in the text.

                                                     20
  • Steps 11 and 19 (an Iwasawa tower; a construction of Behrend). The tower is the field E
    with n a large power of 2, which gives the columns of the table (§I.4.4, §I.5). Behrend’s
    construction is used to choose the set T2 of distinguished columns (Part II, §4.2). The set is
    constructed in Part III, §III.3, following source file [27], which uses the sphere construction
    of Behrend as formalised in Mathlib. (The definitions in [17] are an earlier formulation; the
    final theorem does not use them.) The announcement itself expects Behrend’s construction
    to be unnecessary.
  • Step 18 (covolumes; the index of theta series; congruences between lifts and forms). These
    are the slopes of the lattices in §I.7. The elementary inequality for slopes is proved in Part II,
    §6.8; the form with minimal slopes, the covolumes and the indices are quoted.

    A step largely absent from the final proof. Step 3 (an identity qN = M k + R; a quadric;
automorphy through a theta function on a sixfold cover of GL5 ; the Poincaré bundle). We found
no trace of the identity or of a sixfold cover in the Lean development (quadrics occur in some file
names, in what appear to be unrelated lemmas on orbit measures); step 16 of the announcement
refers to them as “a different half-plane argument”. A Poincaré bundle does occur in the archive,
on an auxiliary abelian scheme, in a construction of the line bundle whose section gives the theta
kernel in algebraic coordinates (§I.4.1; Part II, §6.5). The declaration we tested from that file is
not among those the final theorem depends on, so we cannot say that the final proof uses it.
    Steps of the proof that the announcement does not list. The choice of the auxiliary
primes q and d, with the three character sums and the zero-density and mean-square estimates that
control them (§I.6(e)); the two scales, and the size of b (§I.4.3, §I.6(b)); and the form of Landau’s
lemma on intervals (§I.1; Part II, §1). These are in the Lean development; file names are given
in Part II. The first is proved in Part III, §III.1, the estimate at the large scale in §III.2, and the
lemma in Part II, §1.


I.10     The same proof for Dirichlet L-functions
The Lean proof treats all Dirichlet L-functions at once, and ζ is the case of the trivial character.
This section says what changes for another character. It is described from the statements of the
Lean development and is not worked out in this paper.
    Let ψ be a primitive Dirichlet character of conductor M . Its powers 1, ψ, ψ 2 , . . . form a finite
cyclic group; let o be the number of distinct powers, that is, the order of ψ. For 1 ≤ j < o let ψj
be the primitive character that induces ψ j (the power ψ j itself need not be primitive), and let Gψj
be the function of §I.6 attached to it:
                                                          y          L′
                           Gψj (y) =         Λ(n)ψj (n)        −1 +y      (1, ψj ).               (I.36)
                                       X

                                       n≤y
                                                           n           L

The statistic G is replaced by the combination

                                                       1 o−1
                               Gψ (y) = G(y) +                 Re Gψj (y).                        (I.37)
                                                           X

For n prime to M , the term Λ(n)(y/n − 1) occurs in it with the coefficient 1 + 3o+1
                                                                                  1

                                                                                     o−1     j

which equals 3o+1 if ψ(n) = 1 and 3o+1 otherwise. For n not prime to M the coefficient is
               4o                     3o

1 + 3o+1  j=1 Re ψj (n), which can differ from this formula, since ψj (n) may be nonzero where
      1 Po−1



                                                     21
ψ j (n) = 0; it is still at least 3o+1
                                   3o
                                       . So every coefficient is positive. Multiplied by 3o + 1, the
combination is the statistic attached to the product of ζ(s)3o+1 and the functions L(s, ψj ), 1 ≤ j < o;
at a zero of this product the multiplicities add, so every zero of ζ or of an L(s, ψj ) gives a pole of
the transform. The lemma of §I.1 then applies with one addition. The proof for ζ uses that there is
no pole on the real segment 0 < s < 1; for L-functions a real zero β cannot be excluded in advance.
At such a zero the residue of the transform is a positive multiple of 1/(β(1 − β)). If it is the only
pole nearest to the real point σ, the derivatives at σ have size about (σ − β)−j−1 , and the fixed
sign of the residue makes them positive; this contradicts the bound of Part II, §1 when β > θ; the
Lean proof of the lemma has this case as a separate branch. The conclusion is: if for every ε > 0
the bound Gψ (y) ≤ y 1/2+ε holds on intervals [Y, Y 3/2 ] with Y arbitrarily large, then ζ and every
L(s, ψj ) have no zero with real part greater than 21 . (For o = 1 this is G and ζ.)
    The rest changes as follows. The field E is enlarged so that ψ becomes one of its characters.
The columns of the table are then indexed by a larger group: it consists of o cosets of the subgroup
on which the label coming from ψ is trivial, and the common size of these cosets depends on how
much ψ shares with the residue classes modulo 4n (for the character ( 2· ) modulo 8, for instance,
the number of columns does not grow). The set T is chosen with slightly different densities on
different groups of columns; this is where the weight 3o+11
                                                              comes from. Estimate B is needed for the
             j
twists ξχq χd of all characters ξ modulo M , and Estimate C for the characters of the larger field;
          i

the theorems of §III.1 and §III.2 are stated in that generality. Everything else is the same: the
main inequality (M), with its theta lifts, divisibility, size, trace formula and Galois cohomology, is
the same construction for the larger field, and the passage from the table to one prime sum is the
same computation with more characters.


I.11     Notes on the literature
This paper explains an argument due to Alpöge and Claude [Alp26]. Apart from that argument it
uses, or quotes, results of many mathematicians. This section says which, and where. Two kinds of
use should be distinguished.
   (a) Published results used in the proofs given in this paper.
   • Chebyshev’s method (§I.0, §I.2): Chebyshev [Che52]; the form with the middle binomial
     coefficient is due to Erdős [Erd32]. The exponent of a prime in n! is due to Legendre [Leg08].
     Textbook accounts: [Apo76, Ch. 4], [Dav00, Ch. 7].
   • Landau’s theorem on Mellin transforms (or Dirichlet series) of functions of constant sign
     (§I.1): Landau [Lan05]; see [MV07, §15.1]. The version on intervals (Part II, §1) is proved in
     full in this paper, following the Lean development.
   • The explicit formula and the value ρ ρ(1−ρ) 1
                                                      = 2 + γ − log 4π (§I.1): von Mangoldt; see
                                             P

     [Dav00, Ch. 12 and 17]. The use of positivity in an explicit formula goes back to Weil [Wei52].
   • The prime number theorem in arithmetic progressions, for a fixed modulus, and Mertens’
     theorem in progressions (§I.4.2, §I.6; Part II, §5): de la Vallée Poussin; see [Dav00, Ch. 20
     and 22], [MV07, Ch. 11].
   • Theorems on Dirichlet L-functions used in Part III. For Estimate B (§III.1): the zero-free
     region with at most one exceptional zero, Landau and Page [Pag35], [Dav00, Ch. 14]; primes
     in progressions to large moduli, Thorner and Zaman [TZ21], after Linnik and Gallagher
     [Gal70], [Bom87]; a zero-density estimate, Montgomery [Mon71]; Landau’s bound for L′ /L in


                                                  22
     a zero-free region, [Tit86], [MV07]. For Estimate C (§III.2): the zero-free region, the count of
     zeros in a window and the truncated explicit formula, [Dav00, Ch. 14, 16, 19], [MV07, Ch. 11,
     12]. These are cited, not proved. The mean value over the auxiliary primes is obtained from
     primes in progressions and not from a large sieve; compare [Bom65], [DH66], [HB95].
  • Sets without three-term progressions (§I.9, steps 11 and 19; Part III, §III.3): Behrend [Beh46].
    The construction is carried out, with proof, in §III.3.
  • Stepanov’s method (§I.2, §I.7; Part II, §2), as the model for counting with multiplicity:
    Stepanov [Ste69]; see Bombieri [Bom74].
  • The lemniscate functions and their arithmetic (§I.3): Hurwitz [Hur99]. Formal groups with
    complex multiplication, which explain the exponent a p/(p2 − 1) at inert primes: Lubin and
    Tate [LT65], Honda [Hon70]. That the reduction is of this type exactly at the inert primes:
    Deuring [Deu41]. Algebraicity of the lattice sums of §I.4.1: compare Damerell [Dam70].
  • The Gaussian inner product on polynomials, ∥z a ∥2 = a! (§I.4.2; Part II, §6.5): the Fock, or
    Bargmann, space [Bar61].
  • Hadamard’s inequality, the Chinese remainder theorem and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality
    are used in Part II, §6 without further reference.
   (b) Results behind the quoted statements. The statements marked [quoted] are proved in
the Lean development, which contains its own proofs of the theorems it needs. The underlying
mathematics has the following sources. We cite them for the classical statements; we have not
compared the formalised statements with the published ones in detail.
  • Theta series as kernels and the representation that governs them (§I.4.1, §I.7): Weil [Wei64];
    the correspondence between forms on a pair of groups: Howe [How79]. Seesaw identities:
    Kudla [Kud84]. The inner product formula for theta lifts: Rallis [Ral87]. Bessel periods and
    the restriction problems in which they occur: Gan, Gross and Prasad [GGP12].
  • The period formula (§I.7; Part II, §6.6). In the form used, it is Kronecker’s limit formula (see
    [Wei76]); the evaluation through Gamma values is due to Lerch, and to Chowla and Selberg
    [CS67].
  • The mass formula for definite lattices (§I.7; Part II, §6.6): Siegel [Sie35]; for Hermitian lattices,
    Shimura, see Gan, Hanke and Yu [GHY01]. The dimension formula: Weyl [Wey25].
  • Slopes of Euclidean lattices and the maximal slope (§I.7; Part II, §6.8, §6.10): Stuhler [Stu76],
    Grayson [Gra84]. The slope inequality for maps of lattices: Bost [Bos96].
  • The relative trace formula (§I.7, “Rank”): Jacquet [Jac86].
  • The bound for Galois cohomology (§I.7, “Denominators”; Part II, §6.8). The announcement
    describes it as a weakening of Vandiver’s conjecture. According to our reading of the Lean
    development its proof uses the vanishing of the Iwasawa µ-invariant (Ferrero and Washington
    [FW79]): a statement under that name is proved in the development and the final theorem
    depends on it. The development also contains statements named after Kato’s Euler systems
    [Kat04]; some of these are among the declarations the final theorem depends on and one
    that we tested is not. The proof also uses modularity lifting theorems. We did not find the
    main conjecture of Iwasawa theory (Mazur and Wiles [MW84]) stated under that name. An
    upper bound for a cohomology length in terms of the valuation of a Bernoulli number with
    explicit integral factors is present; it corresponds to a consequence of the main conjecture,
    and we have not compared the two. A bound for the whole group H 2 at every prime is also

                                                  23
      present. Finiteness of these groups is due to Soulé ([Sou79], from memory). Soulé [Sou03,
      Thms. 2 and 3] gives a quantitative bound away from small primes: for the primes p ≥ 4n′ − 1,
      p ≥ [L : Q] + 2, where L is the field and n′ is the twist (Soulé writes n; it is not the n of this
      paper). This was compared with the article by a reviewer, not by us. We have not identified a
      published estimate in the full small-prime range. Part III, §III.9 describes the statement and
      the structure of its proof in the development, and says which published theorems the cases
      correspond to; we have not checked how closely the formal proofs follow the published ones.
    What is new. The combination described in this paper, namely iterated theta lifts as a source
of multiplicity, the comparison of divisibility with size through slopes of lattices carried to the term
linear in the order, and the resulting one-sided bound for G(x), is the contribution of [Alp26]. The
exposition, the worked examples and the computations in this paper are ours.

The formal proof in Lean establishes the Riemann hypothesis for all Dirichlet L-functions at once,
and with it the statements quoted here, in its own formulation. The case of ζ described here is the
case of the trivial character; it uses the same construction.

Sources (the Lean files) are at the end of Part II; the list of scripts follows Part III.



                                                     25
                                  Part II. Details and proofs

Part II can be read after §I.1 and §I.5: it uses G(x) from §I.1 and otherwise repeats its definitions.
It is numbered separately. Its sections are 1–6. Its displays are numbered (II.n); those referred to
often carry section tags instead: (1.1), (4.1), (5.1), . . . , (6.7), and (A), (M). The main inequality is
called (M) in all three parts, and Estimate A of Part I is formula (A) of Part II. Section 3 of Part
II states the reduction to one prime sum again, with all definitions, so that sections 4–6 can be read
on their own. References in square brackets with numbers are to the list of source files at the end of
Part II. Some letters are used with more than one meaning in Part II (for instance N , L, T , h and
a); each is defined where it is used. Formula (5.6) of Part II is Estimate C of Part I. Where sections
3–5 attribute a statement to “the source”, a proof is now in Part III for the following: the choice of
the primes q, d and the bound for the three twists (section 3; Part III, §III.1); formula (5.6) (Part
III, §III.2); the existence of the sets T , T2 with the stated densities (section 4; Part III, §III.3).
The divisibility at inert primes of sections 6.3–6.4 is written up in Part III, §III.5, conditionally
on the integrality of the theta kernels in the coordinates of the height-two logarithm (hypothesis
(B)), which is taken from the Lean development. Part III, §III.6–III.9 prove further steps towards
(M), each from named statements of the Lean development: the passage to lattices in part (§III.6),
the size inequality from eleven quoted inputs (§III.7), the dimension bound of section 6.9 from
three quoted statements (§III.8), and the step from the bound for Galois cohomology to the bound
for denominators (§III.9); the table at the start of Part III gives the status of each. The main
inequality (M) is still quoted.


1. What a zero forces
For Re s > 1, termwise integration gives

                                                          ζ ′ (s)     1       1+γ
                            Z ∞
                  F (s) =         G(x)x−s−1 dx = −                −         +     .                 (II.1)
                            1                         s(s − 1)ζ(s) (s − 1)2   s−1

Indeed, the transform of (x/n − 1)+ is n−s /[s(s − 1)], where v+ = max(v, 0). The Laurent expansion
of ζ at 1 cancels both terms of the principal part there. A zero ρ of multiplicity ν in the critical
strip gives F a simple pole of residue −ν/[ρ(ρ − 1)]. The poles at s = 0 and at the trivial zeros
−2, −4, . . . lie outside the half-plane Re s > θ used below. The poles in the critical strip are nonreal,
since the alternating zeta series gives ζ(σ) < 0 for 0 < σ < 1.
A bound valid for every large x would allow the usual oscillation argument of Landau. We know
the bound only on certain intervals. To use one such interval, we differentiate the transform many
times. Its new weight is concentrated on that interval, and a nearby pole forces large derivatives.
Lemma (the one-sided criterion on intervals; §I.1). Let 0 < θ < 1. If G(x) ≤ xθ throughout
[Y, Y 3/2 ] for arbitrarily large Y , then ζ has no zero in Re s > θ.
Write u = log x, g(u) = G(eu ) and, for each integer j ≥ 0, let Aj (s) = (−1)j F (j) (s)/j! be the
signed jth Taylor coefficient. Let η = max(0.8e0.2 , 1.2e−0.2 ) = 0.9825 . . . and c = 0.008, which is
less than log((1 + η)/(2η)) = 0.00887 . . .; these are absolute constants. Fix σ > 2 so large that
(σ − θ)/(σ − 2) ≤ ec . With the constants above this holds for σ ≥ 252 and every θ in (0, 1).
Differentiating the Mellin integral gives
                                    Z ∞
                    Aj (σ + it) =         g(u)e−itu Kj (u) du,   Kj (u) = uj e−σu /j!.              (II.2)
                                     0

Claim. For U large and j nearest to 54 (σ − 2)U , the part of 0∞ uj e−(σ−2)u du outside [U, 3U/2]
