tail is therefore O(e−cj (σ − 2)−j−1 ) = O((σ − θ)−j ), by the choice of σ. This proves (1.1) along a
sequence j → ∞.
Suppose now there is a pole to the right of θ. Let R be the smaller of σ − θ and the distance from
σ to the nearest pole in Re s > 0. Some pole has horizontal distance from the line Re s = σ strictly
less than R. If R = σ − θ, this follows from the supposition. Otherwise take a nearest pole to σ and
use its nonzero imaginary part. Take t0 = Im ρ for that pole ρ; then the pole nearest to σ + it0 is
at distance less than R. Only finitely many poles lie within R + 1 of it, and each pair is equidistant
from at most one point of the line Re s = σ (both lie to its left). So t near t0 can be chosen so that
z = σ + it has a unique nearest pole, at distance d < R.
Choose d < r < R so that the disk of radius r about z contains just that pole. Subtracting its
principal part and applying Cauchy’s estimate gives |Aj (z)| ≥ c1 d−j − O(r−j ) with c1 > 0 (the
principal part c/(s − ρ) contributes exactly c (z − ρ)−j−1 to Aj (z)). At the real centre σ the same
estimate gives |Aj (σ)| ≪ r−j . This contradicts (1.1) for large j, since d < r < σ − θ. This proof
uses only the poles in two bounded disks. The Lean proof [1] reaches the same statement by a
slightly different route.




                                                    27
2. How divisibility counts primes
Start with a polynomial P (X) of degree D. If P has distinct roots α1 , . . . , αs , each of multiplicity
at least m ≥ 1, then sj=1 (X − αj )m divides P . Comparing degrees gives ms ≤ D.
                    Q

Stepanov uses the same count for a nonzero rational function on a smooth projective curve
over a finite field. Count points over the algebraic closure of that field (the curve is assumed
geometrically irreducible), and include the points at infinity. For a nonconstant function the zeros
and poles are the inverse images of 0 and of infinity under its map to the projective line. Each
inverse image, counted with multiplicity, has the degree of that map. Thus the total multiplicity
of the zeros equals the total order D of the poles. Hence at most ⌊D/m⌋ points can be zeros of
order at least m. On the projective line the function is a quotient of polynomials. A polynomial of
positive degree D has just one pole, of order D at infinity.
Over the integers, write vp (c) for the exponent of a prime p in a nonzero rational number c
(numerator exponent minus denominator exponent), and put vp (0) = +∞. The corresponding
count is
                       ep log p ≤ log |∆|,   ∆ ∈ Z \ {0}, 0 ≤ ep ≤ vp (∆).             (II.3)
                   X

                       p

To bound the left side one needs an independent upper bound for |∆|. For a square integer matrix
of nonzero determinant ∆, for example, divisibility gives a lower bound, while the lengths of its
columns give an upper bound by Hadamard’s inequality. Section 6 explains the corresponding
comparison for lattices. (The file [2] states such an inequality for an earlier form of the argument.
That form is stated in the archive but, according to our dependency check, is not used by the final
theorem.)
Why odd powers of a prime occur. Here is the local model in rational arithmetic. For a prime
p form the series
                                                 2j
                             l(X) = X +                                                (II.4)
                                          X
                                              X p /pj .
                                                     j≥1
                                                                                                     2j
It has a unique inverse under substitution, U (z) = z+· · · , given recursively by U = z− j≥1 U p /pj .
                                                                                              P

Put L = p2 − 1. We claim that the coefficient of z a in U has valuation at least −(a − 1)/L for
each integer a ≥ 1. This holds for a = 1. In a product of r = p2j copies of U , the degrees i1 , . . . , ir
contributing to z a are positive and sum to a. Each is less than a. By induction their coefficients
have product of valuation at least −(a − r)/L. Division by pj costs j ≤ (r − 1)/L, proving the
claim.
For a series f with integer coefficients, the same product calculation gives the bound −(a − 1)/L
for the coefficient of z a in f (U (z)). Its ath derivative at zero is a! times this coefficient. Counting
multiples of p, p2 , . . . in a! gives vp (a!) = h≥1 ⌊a/ph ⌋ = a/(p − 1) + O(1 + logp a), where ⌊y⌋ is the
                                                P

greatest integer at most y and logp a = log a/ log p. The derivative therefore has valuation at least

                   1     1                                  1   1   1
                                                                           
          a          − 2          − O(1 + logp a) = a         + 3 + 5 + ···       − O(1 + logp a).   (II.5)
                  p−1 p −1                                  p p    p

The series models differentiation on an elliptic curve with complex multiplication (extra endomor-
phisms) at primes remaining prime in its imaginary quadratic field. The source proves a weighted
version for its arithmetic maps. At odd powers ph it yields the term (a/ph − w)+ for a positive
weight w (quoted; see section 6.4). When w = b and a = bx, this is precisely b(x/ph − 1)+ . [3]

3. The reduction to one prime sum
For an odd prime D let χD (r) equal 0 if D | r, 1 if r is a nonzero square modulo D, and −1
otherwise. For a nonprincipal Dirichlet character χ put
                            L′
                     cχ =      (1, χ),      Gχ (x) =            Λ(r)χ(r)(x/r − 1) + cχ x.        (II.6)
                                                          X
                            L                             r≤x

A Dirichlet character is a homomorphism from the invertible classes modulo an integer to the
nonzero complex numbers, extended by zero to other classes. It is nonprincipal if it takes a value
different from 1 on the invertible classes. The function L(s, χ) is the continuation of r≥1 χ(r)r−s
                                                                                       P

from Re s > 1, and the prime denotes differentiation in s.
For distinct odd primes q, d write η = χq χd for the pointwise product. Consider the single prime
sum
                       Sq,d (x) =     Λ(r)(1 + χq (r))(1 − χd (r))(x/r − 1).                 (II.7)
                                  X

                                     r≤x
Away from q, d, the coefficient of Λ(r) before smoothing is 4 if r is a square modulo q and a
nonsquare modulo d, and zero otherwise. The exact identity obtained by expanding the two factors
is
                     Rq,d (x) = Sq,d (x) − x log x + (1 + γ + cχq − cχd − cη )x
                                                                                           (II.8)
                              = G(x) + Gχq (x) − Gχd (x) − Gη (x).
Thus a one-sided bound for Rq,d and absolute bounds for three quadratic character sums suffice.
The reduction. Fix 0 < ε < 1/52. By Theorems III.1.1 and III.2.1 of Part III, for every large Y
there are primes q ≡ 3 mod 8, d ≡ 7 mod 16 with q | d + 1 for which statement 2 below (Estimate
B) and formula (5.6) (Estimate C) hold for Y ≤ x ≤ Y 3/2 . Statement 1 (Estimate A) is then
deduced in sections 4 and 5 from the main inequality (M), which is quoted, together with Estimates
B and C.
1. The residue sum. There are positive integers m, k, n depending on x such that
                                                          x   xm     k

Here C is absolute and Cε depends only on ε. The orders are m = x1/2+O(ε) , k = x9/2+O(ε) ,
n = x2+O(ε) . Sections 4 and 5 derive this from (M).

The convergence here follows from the prime number theorem for a fixed nonprincipal character.
The proof bounds the proportion of bad choices by mean-square estimates, first in q, then in d for

the exponent 12 + 2ε ; the Lean proof is in [9, 10, 29].

G(x) ≤ xθ . Section 1 excludes every zero to the right of θ. Letting θ decrease to 1/2 and using the
symmetry ρ 7→ 1 − ρ from the functional equation proves RH from (M) and Estimates B and C. [1,

4. The main inequality (M)

4.1 Which prime powers are counted?

Fix an interval and primes q, d as in section 3. Fix x in the interval. For an odd prime power r = ph
with p =
       ̸ q, attach two labels. The first is the row v(r) = (1 + χq (r))/2, equal to 1 if r is a nonzero
square modulo q, and 0 otherwise. For the second, choose n = 2e at the scale x2+8ε ≤ n ≤ 2x2+8ε
and put N = 4n. Write [r] = {r, −r} mod N . The n pairs of odd residues form a group H under
multiplication. Its elements will label columns. Choosing a fixed t ∈ H puts r in column t[r].
The coefficient at r = ph , before inserting a weight, is

                                        c(r) = (1 − χd (r)) log p.                                 (II.10)

If p =
     ̸ d it is 2 log p for an odd power of a nonsquare modulo d and zero otherwise. At p = d it is
log d for every power. The two omitted primes 2, q have χd (2) = χd (q) = 1. Indeed d ≡ 7 mod 8
gives the first identity. For the second, q, d ≡ 3 mod 4 and d ≡ −1 mod q, so quadratic reciprocity
gives (q/d) = −(d/q) = −(−1/q) = 1. So c(r) vanishes at every power of 2 and of q; omitting these
primes changes nothing.
A table small enough to write out. Take q = 3, d = 23, N = 16 and t = [1]. The four columns
are [1], [3], [5], [7]. For example [3]2 = [9] = [7] and [3]3 = [5]. The nonzero squares modulo 23 are
1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 9, 12, 13, 16, 18. In each cell below are the primes at most 61 with nonzero coefficient.
A dash denotes an empty cell in this range.

                                    v\s [1] [3]      [5]    [7]
                                     0 17    −    5, 11, 53 23                                     (II.11)
                                     1   − 19, 61 37, 43     7

Among primes the density in each cell is 1/16, as shown in section 5. To place 11, observe that
11 ≡ 2 mod 3 and [11] = [5]. To place a higher power one repeats both tests on that power. The
prime 7 goes in (1, [7]) with coefficient 2 log 7. Its square disappears because χ23 (72 ) = 1. Its cube
has the same row and column as 7. At the exceptional prime 23, its first power lands in (0, [7]),
its square in (1, [1]), and each has coefficient log 23. The example only illustrates the identities of
section 5, which hold for any positive weights; its parameters do not satisfy the conditions of (M).

4.2 How a weight determines a cutoff

Let b = exp(xε ). The construction supplies nonempty subsets T, T2 of H, an integer at > 0 for
each t ∈ T with |at /b − x| ≤ 1, and m maps of initial rank k (the chain of theta lifts of section 6.2;
m and k are the parameters in (A)). Write |S| for the cardinality of a finite set S and δ = |T2 |/n.
For each translate u ∈ H and map 1 ≤ j ≤ m there are 2n positive weights wu,j (v, s). In the
construction they are integers. The distinguished cells are (1, s) with s ∈ uT2 . Their weights satisfy
b ≤ wu,j (1, s) ≤ b + O(b/(nx2 )). At every other cell the ratio at /wu,j (v, s) is at least exp(xε/3 )/3.
Thus the same table tests primes at two markedly different scales.
Fix u, t, j, write a = at and w = wu,j . Each power in cell i = (v, s) contributes

                   c(r)(a/r − wi )+ = c(r)wi a/(rwi ) − 1 + ,           z+ = max(z, 0).            (II.12)
                                                               


The contribution ends at r = a/wi . Adding all cells gives the finite sum

                              p̸=q

In the running example take T = T2 = {[1]}, one table, x = 1000, b = 10, a = 10000 and u = [1].
Here δ = 1/4. The weights and cutoffs, in the same column order as above, are

         wi  [1]  [3]  [5]  [7]                a/wi     [1]    [3]    [5]    [7]
         0 0.05 0.05 0.05 0.05                  0   200000 200000 200000 200000                    (II.13)

For example 7 contributes 2 log 7 (10000/7−0.05), whereas 232 = 529 contributes log 23 (10000/529−

at 7 is 10, and at 529 it is 0.05.

5.1 Subtracting the main term cell by cell

For fixed moduli, among primes not dividing 2qd the fraction contributing to a given cell is 1/(4n).
One half are nonsquares modulo d, one half have the specified sign modulo q, and 1/n have the
specified pair modulo N . The Chinese remainder theorem makes these conditions independent
among the invertible residue classes. The prime number theorem in progressions gives their density
among primes. The coefficient 2 log p doubles this density in Ψi . Thus Ψi (z) has main term µz,
where µ = 1/(2n).
The fixed-modulus prime number theorem gives an error OA (z/(log z)A ) as z → ∞ for each fixed
A > 0. Higher prime powers and the one exceptional prime fit within this bound. The constants
may depend on the moduli.
Set Ci = 1∞ (Ψi (z) − µz) dz/z 2 , which converges by that estimate. Subtract the integral from y to
         R

In particular Ri (y) = o(y) for fixed moduli, meaning Ri (y)/y → 0. The uniform estimate needed
when the moduli vary with x comes later.

5.4 Discarding the other scale with a uniform estimate

Divisibility uses all the cells, but only the distinguished cells should measure primes near x. Small
weights at the other cells push their cutoffs far enough out for the prime number theorem to control
them. The fixed-modulus limit Ri (y)/y → 0 gives no rate uniform in q, d, N . For the moduli of
section 3 one has, for each primitive character in the cell expansion and for y ≥ exp(xε/3 )/3,

                          |Gχ (y)| ≤ y ϑ,      ϑ = exp(−κxε/8 ),         κ > 0,                   (5.6)

with G for the trivial character. The constant κ does not depend on x, q or d, and the bound is
for x ≥ x0 (ε, κ). This is Estimate C. It is proved in Part III, §III.2 (Theorem III.2.1), where κ is
called c′ . At any nondistinguished cell i use (5.6) at y = a/wi . Each remainder is a combination
Ri = χ fχ Gχ of the functions Gχ for the characters of the table, with χ |fχ | = 2: the sum of

Indeed there are at most 2n cells per table, at /b ≤ x + 1, and nx/δ grows polynomially in x.
Exponential decay in (5.6) absorbs this factor.

6. Where the main inequality (M) comes from
Sections 6.1–6.2 construct the maps from theta series. Sections 6.3–6.4 explain why their derivatives
are divisible by powers of inert primes. Sections 6.5–6.7 bound the size of the same derivatives and
identify the main term. Sections 6.8–6.10 control denominators and show that enough independent
images survive. We prove the elementary calculations in each step and say which arithmetic
statements are quoted.


6.1 A theta lift in classical terms

A theta series records representations of integers by a quadratic form. For instance put L = Z[i],
Q(a + bi) = a2 + b2 and z in the complex upper half-plane. With s = exp(2πiz), its theta series is

                        ΘL (z) =             sQ(λ) = 1 + 4s + 4s2 + 4s4 + 8s5 + · · · .                           (II.27)
                                         X

                                     λ∈L

The coefficient of s5 is 8 because the solutions are (±1, ±2) and (±2, ±1). Absolute convergence
follows from a,b exp(−2π ℑz (a2 + b2 )) < ∞. Fourier orthogonality recovers a coefficient by pairing:
             P

for each y > 0,                       Z            1
                                     e10πy             ΘL (u + iy)e−2πi5u du = 8.                                 (II.28)
                                               0
         R 1 2πi(k−5)u
Indeed    0 e          du is one for k = 5 and zero for every other integer k.
To make this a lift, allow the lattice to vary within a finite collection of isometry classes Li . Attach
to each class a value f (i). Weight it by hi = 1/| Aut Li |, the reciprocal of the number of its
symmetries in the chosen group. The theta series are now a kernel depending on both i and z. The
lifted function is
                                      Θ(f )(z) =    hi f (i) ΘLi (z).                              (II.29)
                                                 X

                                                             i

In the rank-one Hermitian example, unitary symmetries of Z[i] are multiplication by 1, −1, i, −i. On
its single class with f = 1 this normalization gives ΘL /4, so the same Fourier pairing gives 8/4 = 2.
This example shows what integration against a theta kernel does. The high-rank construction has
different groups and test functions.
                                    −1 √
                                              √
For the source let E = Q(ζN + ζN       , −q, −d), with ζN = exp(2πi/N ), and let F denote its
maximal real subfield. Write for complex conjugation on E. A Hermitian matrix J satisfies
  t
J = J. Its unitary group consists of matrices g with g t Jg = J. It is definite if its form at every
real embedding of F is definite. At a fixed integral level and scalar weight, forms on a definite
group can be viewed as functions on finitely many lattice classes. At higher weight the values are
vectors and must transform under the finite stabilizers.
For a pair of unitary groups, the general lift has the same two operations:
                                                                                   Z
                Θφ (g, h) =          (ω(g, h)φ)(λ),                   Tφ f (h) =             Θφ (g, h)f (g) dg.   (II.30)
                                X
                                                                                   [U (J)]
                              λ∈V (F )

Here V is a finite-dimensional coordinate space for the theta sum; V (F ) denotes its rational points
over F . The notation [U (J)] denotes integration over the group at all places modulo its rational
points, with its invariant measure. The test function φ decays rapidly at infinity and is locally
constant of compact support at finite primes. The Weil representation ω is the action of the two
groups on these test functions. Lattice indicators at the finite primes restrict the sum to lattices.

                                                                 37
In the displayed convention the lift is linear in φ and conjugate-linear in f . [13] (The linearity
statement of [13] is a statement in the archive, not used by the final theorem.)




                                                38
6.2 Composing maps and clearing their denominators

For a finite form of the calculation let Ij be the finite set of classes at stage j, hj (i) its weights,
and Kj (i, i′ ) the matrix of the jth map. In the linear convention write

                                  (Tj f )(i′ ) =          hj (i)Kj (i, i′ )f (i).                (II.31)
                                                   X

                                                   i∈Ij

Complex conjugates from a pairing are absorbed by taking the conjugate source space. Expanding
two such sums gives the composite kernel i′ Kj (i, i′ )hj+1 (i′ )Kj+1 (i′ , i′′ ). Thus successive maps
                                           P

really do use the same intermediate index.
In the source the definite ranks are Nj = k + j(τ0 + 1) for an odd integer τ0 . The rank conditions
[26] include m even and m ≥ 2, k even and k ≥ m + 3, τ0 ≥ k + m + 4, and τ0 > 3m. For instance
m = 2, k = 6, τ0 = 13 gives ranks 6, 20, 34 and satisfies these numerical constraints. The full chain
also needs field, level, and lattice data. Since m is even and m ≥ 2, the chain has at least two steps.
At the end of the chain the complex pairing of the entire chain can be expressed as a single period
integral over a subgroup. The source proves this identity with prescribed normalization factors
and coordinate changes. Its use is that the final period can be compared at different choices of the
intermediate character twists. [7, 14]
The integrality assertion for a finite chain. Let S be a subring of a commutative ring R.
Suppose all Kj (i, i′ ) and starting values belong to S. If scalars Dj ∈ R satisfy Dj hj (i) ∈ S for
every i, then every final value multiplied by 1≤j≤m Dj belongs to S. For one map this follows by
                                                 Q

moving D1 into its finite sum. Applying the same argument to the already cleared values proves
the statement by induction. For vector values, replace S by a lattice at each stage and require each
Kj (i, i′ ) to carry the preceding lattice into the next. This is the assertion stated as ChainInt, with
its vector version, in [14] (a statement in the archive, not used by the final theorem).
For example, on one class let the successive weights be 1/2, 1/3, the kernel entries 5, 7, and the
starting value 1. The output is 35/6, and clearing the two weights gives 35. At the prime 5, two
entries divisible respectively by 52 and 53 would give a cleared output divisible by 55 . In a sum the
exponent can increase through cancellation. It is always at least the common lower bound on the
summands. This elementary induction explains how divisibility persists. It requires separate local
estimates to put significant prime powers into each kernel entry.
Where a period enters. A kernel evaluated in complex coordinates need not be integral. An
algebraic local coordinate and a complex analytic coordinate on the same elliptic curve differ at
the tangent space by a nonzero scale. An ath derivative acquires its ath power. Taking logarithms
gives a term linear in a. In the global comparison the local gains, the period, and the lattice indices
must all pertain to the same normalized maps. We next give a local example in which one can
track every derivative, and then the estimates that take its place in the chain.




                                                          39

6.4 How the weight cuts down the local exponent

Here is a calculation which accounts for the positive part in (4.1). A directional Hasse derivative of
a polynomial Q is the coefficient of Z β in Q(X + j bj Zj ), where bj are vectors, Zj indeterminates,
                                                  P

and β a list of nonnegative exponents. It has no factorial denominator. For a product c Qc the
                                                                                           Q

coefficient is the sum of products over decompositions β = c βc . If for a given set S of directions
                                                             P

the coefficient for Qc vanishes whenever j∈S (βc )j > mc , then the coefficient for the product
                                             P

vanishes whenever j∈S βj > c mc : each term exceeds the allowance of some factor. This is the
                     P         P

product-vanishing lemma in [21].
For a determinant the allowance has a simple origin. In det(X + sb, Y + tb), where X, Y, b are
columns of length two, the coefficient of st is det(b, b) = 0, and there is no term in s2 or t2 . Each
minor can take at most one such hit. Products of C minors can take at most C. With several
directions confined to specified columns, the number of available columns provides another upper
bound. These are constraints on nonvanishing terms before any prime estimate is made.
For an inert prime p, write γp = p/(p2 − 1) = h≥1, h odd p−h . Suppose a local expansion of
                                                              P

derivative order a supplies a valuation lower bound γp a − h ih − D0 , where D0 is a loss, and the
                                                          P

nonvanishing terms have
                                0 ≤ ih ≤ min(a/ph , wh )  (h odd).                          (II.36)
Then the gain after charging these terms is at least
                       X                               
                             a/ph − min(a/ph , wh ) − D0 =                    (a/ph − wh )+ − D0 .                  (6.1)
                                                                       X

                     h odd                                            h odd

The first bound ph ih ≤ a is a degree bound. The second is supplied by column and minor capacities
in the translated weight data. For example p = 3, a = 81, wh = 2 gives 25 at h = 1, 1 at h = 3,
and zero thereafter. Obtaining all these bounds together is the arithmetic content. In the source
the factorial valuations in the polynomial substitution give the initial lower bound, other terms
have to be paid for, and a local lattice index must also be subtracted. [19, 21]
The factorial count, with proof. The initial lower bound in the last paragraph comes from
valuations of factorials. Here is the source’s statement in full, with an elementary proof. Let p be
prime and put ρp = 1/(p2 − 1), so that γp + ρp = 1/(p − 1). For an integer n ≥ 0 let λ(n) be the
number of digits of n in base p, with λ(0) = 0. Let S be a finite set and S0 ⊂ S. Suppose given
nonnegative integers as follows:
  (i) αs and ℓs for s ∈ S, with αs = 0 outside S0 ;
 (ii) lists es,1 , . . . , es,r1 with sum αs ;
 (iii) lists as,1 , . . . , as,r0 r1 with sum at most ℓs ;
 (iv) is,h for h in a finite set of nonnegative integers, zero outside S0 , with p2h+1 is,h ≤ αs ;
  (v) with A =        s αs and Λ =       s ℓs , one has            j as,j +     s,h is,h = Λ.
                   P                  P                   P P                 P
                                                              s

Let V be the sum of vp (n!) over all the es,j , as,j , is,h . Then for every integer i2 with pi2 ≤ Λ,

     V ≥ γp A + ρp (A + Λ) + i2 − ∆,                 ∆=            r1 λ(αs ) + r0 r1 λ(ℓs ) +          λ(αs )2 .   (6.1a)
                                                             X                                 X

                                                             s∈S                                s∈S0

Proof. Legendre’s formula is vp (n!) = (n − σp (n))/(p − 1), where σp (n) is the sum of the digits of
n in base p. Each digit is at most p − 1, so vp (n!) ≥ n/(p − 1) − λ(n). The integers in the three
families add up to A + Λ, by the two hypotheses on sums. Hence V ≥ (A + Λ)/(p − 1) − λ(n).
                                                                                             P



                                                             41
Now count digits. Each es,j is at most αs , and there are r1 of them: at most r1 λ(αs ). Each as,j is
at most ℓs , and there are r0 r1 of them: at most r0 r1 λ(ℓs ). If is,h ≥ 1 then p2h+1 ≤ αs , so h < λ(αs )
and at most λ(αs ) values of h occur; each such is,h is at most αs . These contribute at most λ(αs )2 ,
and only for s ∈ S0 . Thus λ(n) ≤ ∆. Finally (A + Λ)/(p − 1) = γp A + γp Λ + ρp (A + Λ), and
                              P

γp Λ ≥ Λ/p ≥ i2 . □
In (6.1a) the term γp A is the model gain A(1/p + 1/p3 + · · · ) of section 2. The term ρp (A + Λ)
pays exactly for the denominators 1/(p2 − 1) per unit of degree met there in the inverse series, and
i2 pays for one further division. The loss ∆ is small: per index s it is at most (r1 + r0 r1 + λ)λ,
where λ is the larger of the digit counts λ(αs ), λ(ℓs ). For example, with p = 3, one index s,
r0 = r1 = 1, α = 81, ℓ = 0 and no is,h : V = v3 (81!) = 40, while γ3 A + ρ3 A = 81/2 = 40.5 and
∆ = λ(81) + λ(81)2 = 30. The inequality 40 ≥ 40.5 − 30 holds with room to spare; the main terms
agree to within one. This lemma supplies valuations of individual factorials. It does not identify
which factorials occur in the theta coefficients, which is part of the quoted local theorem. [21]
The coefficient 2 log p. For the coefficient field L0 and a prime ideal p over p, write ep , fp for the
ramification index and residue degree. The normalized local weight is λp = log Norm(p)/[L0 : Q].
The source measures a local gain y in units 2λp ep y. Since Norm(p) = pfp and p|p ep fp = [L0 : Q],
                                                                                P

one unit at every place over p sums to 2 log p. For example in Q(i) above 3, e = 1, f = 2 and
λ = (log 9)/2 = log 3. The factor 2 is the squared-norm normalisation of §I.4.2(ii). At the ramified
prime d the splitting condition has half weight and gives log d. These are the coefficients c(r) of
section 4.1; section 5.2 checked that the character expansion reproduces them. [3, 22]
The concrete minor, the Hasse product rule and (6.1) are three elementary ingredients of the
local argument. Applying them to all the normalized theta kernels, choosing the usable places,
and bounding the losses after summation requires the source’s local divisibility theorem. In its
statement the left side is the sum of the odd-prime-power expressions minus the orbit and rounding
losses. The right side is the sum of actual local gains over the m maps minus the local lattice
correction. [19] The divisibility at inert primes is written up in Part III, §III.5, conditionally on
the integrality of the theta kernels in the coordinates of the height-two logarithm (hypothesis (B)),
which is taken from the Lean development; under that hypothesis §III.5 proves the lower bound for
the valuation of a product of minors applied to one such function (for Frobenius orbits of length
two; for longer orbits it also uses a duality statement cited from the Lean), with the positive parts
of (6.1), and it proves without hypothesis the estimate of section 2 for the inverse of the logarithm
in several variables; it does not prove the passage to lattices, and the congruence of section 6.3 is
not used there. The passage to lattices is treated in Part III, §III.6: the lemmas on lattices and the
splitting of the prime sum are proved there, the theorem on the glued lattice is conditional on two
statements of the source, and the bounds for the loss sums are quoted.




                                                    42
6.5 Which theta function is differentiated?

At step j let Nj = k + j(τ0 + 1) be the rank of the definite unitary group, where τ0 is the odd rank
parameter. The coordinates of the holomorphic theta function, for a pair of lattice classes L, L′ at
consecutive stages and a complex embedding of the coefficient field, are the entries of a rectangular
matrix at each weight place:

                            z = (zτ,α,β ),            1 ≤ α ≤ Nj ,       1 ≤ β ≤ Nj+1 .            (II.37)

The analytic estimates bound the coefficient factor using the Gaussian-weighted norm of the theta
function on a fundamental domain. The polynomial factor is bounded by a product of hook factors
of the weights times the squared norms of the two input vectors. In one row of length a the hook
factor is exactly a!. The full operator bound also contains the period, level, multiplicities and theta
mass. [23, 24] The estimates for the two factors are proved in Part III, §III.4, apart from the four
conditional passages listed at the start of that section.
Comparison with section 6.3. In the lattice comparison the archimedean map height is the
average over infinite places v of log ∥Tv ∥2op , where the operator norm uses the two chosen Hermitian

                                                           43
metrics. Divisibility by pe thus contributes 2e log p when measured at all places above p, and a degree-
a monomial contributes log(a!) = a log a − a + O(log(a + 1)) in the squared Gaussian norm. The
difference from section 6.3 is visible in Aa = a![z a ]s(z), where [z a ] means the coefficient of z a . The
nearest pole of s is at finite positive distance R; the radius formula gives lim supa |[z a ]s|1/a = 1/R.
Along a subsequence, therefore, log |Aa |2 = 2 log(a!) + O(a), while the squared Gaussian norm
contributes just one factorial. [24, 25]




                                                    44
A natural question. Why not differentiate a classical entire theta function, which already
has Gaussian size? For the lemniscatic lattice the Weierstrass function σ of y 2 = 4x3 − 4x
has integer derivatives σ (n) (0); they vanish unless n ≡ 1 mod 4, and the nonzero ones satisfy
log |σ (n) (0)| ≤ 12 n log n + O(n), by the Gaussian growth of σ and Cauchy’s formula. Exact
computation for n ≤ 241 shows that their divisibility at inert primes falls well short of the model:
the exponent of 3 is 13 at n = 101 and 28 at n = 241, against 3n/8 = 37.9 and 90.4. So Gaussian
size does not by itself bring the divisibility with it. The source’s local theorem establishes both
properties together for its theta kernels, in coordinates chosen for that purpose, and it is quoted
here. The computation is in the file e4_sigma.py listed under Computations. [21, 23]


6.6 Size of a derivative, its period, and the count of forms
                                                                                                    2
The model for the size of a derivative is this. Let f be an entire function with |f (z)| ≤ S e|z| /2 for
                                                                √
all z, where S > 0. Cauchy’s formula on the circle |z| = a gives |f (a) (0)| ≤ S a! (e/a)a/2 for a ≥ 1.
By Stirling’s formula, if f (a) (0) ̸= 0, then log |f (a) (0)|2 ≤ a log a − a + 2 log S + O(log(a + 1)): one
factorial for the square. Rescaling the variable by the period adds −a log ΩE , with ΩE as defined
below. This is the model at the start of Part III, §III.4. It identifies the factorial and scale terms of
(6.5). The bound for the norm of an actual composed map needs additional estimates.
Here is the exact period normalization appearing in the comparison. It is the normalization for the
field E, which is the one of the source. Let rdE = |disc E|1/[E:Q] be the root discriminant of E, and
put v = rdE /2. The normalized period ΩE is the positive number with

                      log ΩE = γ + log π − log v − cχd = γ + log(2π/rdE ) − cχd .                       (6.3)

This is the Chowla–Selberg identity in the form used. The period ΩE measures squared size.
In [16] the period is not constructed from a specific curve: the statement is that a curve and a
normalisation exist for which (6.3) holds. It is important here which period normalization is used.
Rescaling the differential changes the linear term. [16]
                                                                                       √
Remark. For the elliptic√        curve  itself, with  complex   multiplication   by Q(   −d),√the same
identity holds with v = d/2, the imaginary part of √   the quadratic lattice parameter (1+i d)/2, and
with the period Ω of the curve: log Ω = γ + log(2π/ d) − cχd = γ + log π − log v − cχd . In this√form
the identity is Kronecker’s limit formula, and Ω is a geometric mean over the ideal classes of Q( −d).
For Q(i), with d replaced by 4, exponentiating the same expression gives √   Ω = 2π(ϖ/π)2 , with ϖ the
lemniscate constant. For d = 23 the formula gives log Ω = γ√+ log(2π/ 23) + 0.422120948038 . . ..
The two normalizations differ by log Ω − log ΩE = log(rdE / d), and the sum       √ of the logarithm of
the period and log v is the same in both: log ΩE + log(rdE /2) = log Ω + log( d/2). Only this sum
enters (6.6). So (6.6) is the same in either normalization, and the numerical check in section 6.7
may use either. The two lines of (6.5), taken separately, hold only with ΩE and v = rdE /2.
The other source of linear terms is a count of forms. The mass of a definite genus is the sum of

The three quantities in hB = µCC + h − F0 are these. First, h is an upper bound for the height of
the pairing map, the average over the archimedean places of the logarithm of the squared operator
norm (section 6.5). Second, µCC is the mean slope of the common lattice in which all the pairing
maps take their values. Third, F0 is the part of the local divisibility that comes from the places
other than those of the top weights.
Neither offset survives. The term F0 also appears, with the opposite sign, in the slope of the source
lattice after gluing (section 6.8), and the two cancel. The term µCC is the slope of the target in the
inequality of section 6.10, where it appears on both sides. So one may read (6.5) as a bound for the
height h alone, as in Part I. An earlier formulation [18] writes the same pair of inequalities for a
single quantity.

What is quoted in this comparison. The period formula (6.3), the mass formula, the construction
and count of the auxiliary levels, and the local level correction are the arithmetic inputs; they are
quoted. The analytic norm bound is proved in part in Part III, §III.4. The factorial cancellation
(6.4) and all signs in (6.6) are algebraic consequences proved above. The next comparison explains
why the divisibility can be applied to enough independent vectors at once.


                                                      47
6.8 From one determinant to lattices

A lattice L of rank r in a Euclidean space is the integer span of r independent vectors. Its covolume,
measured in its span, is the volume of the parallelepiped on a basis. Put µ(L) = − log(covol L)/r.
Let T be an injective linear map with T (L) ⊂ M , and let ∥T ∥ be its Euclidean operator norm. (This
section uses the plain normalisation, µ and log ∥T ∥. In (6.5)–(6.6) the height h is the logarithm of
the squared norm, and µCC is normalised to match, so it is twice a slope in the present sense, up to
an additive constant depending only on the field. The two normalisations must not be mixed.) The
volume of T (L) is at most ∥T ∥r times that of L, as one sees using an orthonormal basis of the span
and Hadamard’s inequality. Taking logarithms,

                                     µ(L) ≤ µ(T (L)) + log ∥T ∥.                                (II.43)

Suppose first that M has the same rank r. Then T (L) has finite index in M and covol T (L) = [M :
T (L)] covol M , so
                                    1
                            µ(L) + log[M : T (L)] ≤ µ(M ) + log ∥T ∥.                          (II.44)
                                    r
This is the exact form of the principle that divisibility is bounded by size: the index is on the left
and the norm on the right. For T = diag(6, 10) and L = M = Z2 the index is 60, both slopes are
zero and ∥T ∥ = 10. The inequality reads 12 log 60 ≤ log 10, that is, 60 ≤ 100. The factors 2, 3, 5 of
the index and the two column lengths refer to the same map.
If L has smaller rank than M , the image T (L) is only a sublattice of smaller rank, and the first
inequality gives
                                  µ(L) ≤ µmax (M ) + log ∥T ∥,                             (II.45)
where µmax is the supremum of slopes of nonzero sublattices. A single map of small rank therefore
controls only the maximal slope of its target. Sections 6.9 and 6.10 use many maps together in
order to reach the mean slope µ(M ).
A rational matrix presents a further problem: local divisibility may apply at different primes in
different bases. The source uses equality of a period pairing under changes of intermediate twists to
place the local lattices in a common rational vector space. One can then choose a favorable lattice
at each prime and put these choices together.
Over Q the last operation has a short proof. Let Lp be a full lattice over Zp inside Qrp , equal to Zrp
outside a finite set. Here Qp is the completion of Q for the p-adic absolute value, and Zp its ring of
integers. Choose a positive integer D with DZrp ⊆ Lp ⊆ D−1 Zrp at all primes. Let L be the set of
x in D−1 Zr whose image in D−1 Zrp /DZrp lies in Lp /DZrp for every p | D. The Chinese remainder
theorem applied after multiplying by D identifies the ambient finite quotient with (Z/D2 Z)r , the
product of its prime parts. The resulting lattice has the prescribed localization at every prime. For
example allow a denominator 2 in the first coordinate only at 2, and a denominator 3 in the second
only at 3. The result is 12 Z ⊕ 31 Z. Its covolume is 1/6. This proves the gluing step over Q. The
source uses the corresponding construction over a number field, with its locally chosen character
twists.
The denominators that remain. For a prime ℓ and positive twist index a, the relevant finite
group consists of first Galois cohomology classes over E with values in Qℓ /Zℓ (−a), unramified away
from ℓ and zero locally above ℓ. The twist means that Galois acts by the inverse ath power of its
action on roots of unity of ℓ-power order. A finite group of order ℓh costs h log ℓ in a logarithmic
index. Write hℓ,a for the length of this group as a Zℓ -module. On the selected window the supplied
bound, after passage to the working scale, is hℓ,a < ∞ for 1 ≤ a < ⌊x400 ⌋ (the number of twists


                                                  48
used is at most x400 ; see Part III, §III.9) and
                                                                           √
                                          (log ℓ)                                          (II.46)
                                    X                  X
                                                                  hℓ,a ≤       b
                                    ℓ∈S             1≤a<⌊x400 ⌋

for every finite set S of primes. This is the uniform assertion needed here. (In [8] the bound is
stated for a < x401 at a slightly different scale; the form above is what is used.) Even for ζ the
bound is needed over the field E, not over Q. The statement on these extension groups, the local
estimates relating their lengths to actual lattice indices, and the independence of the normalized
global period are quoted. [8, 19] The slope inequalities proved in this section are the elementary
case; the form with minimal slopes used in the chain is quoted (section 6.10).




                                                        49
6.9 Why many independent images improve the estimate

A large norm for one map could be concentrated on one direction. A second moment distinguishes
this possibility. For a positive Hermitian matrix Q of size D, Cauchy–Schwarz on its nonzero
eigenvalues gives
                                rank Q ≥ (tr Q)2 / tr(Q2 ) (Q ̸= 0).                    (II.47)
Two maps with images the coordinate axes in C2 give Q = I when one adds their orthogonal
projections. The bound gives rank two. Two maps onto the same axis give Q = diag(2, 0), with the
same trace 2 but second moment 4, and the bound gives rank one.
Here is the version needed when some source directions have already been discarded. Let Bi :
Vi → H be maps of finite-dimensional complex inner product spaces, with D = dim H > 0,
S = i dim Vi > 0 and positive weights ci . A superscript † denotes the adjoint. Put Ki = ci Bi† Bi
    P

and Q = i ci Bi Bi† . Let Ui be subspaces of Vi with i (dim Vi − dim Ui ) ≤ αS, and suppose

                     ∥Ki − I∥2HS ≤ eS,
                 i

(The proof below gives the sharper bound with η in place of 2η. The form with η is the one that
is used: in section 6.10 for the chain of the source, and in Part III, §III.8, Lemma T1(d).) The
display, with 2η, is the finite-dimensional assertion FrameLA stated in [20] (a statement in the

Proof. We may take 0 ≤ α ≤ 1, since losing the entire space makes a larger bound automatic. Let
P project onto the missing target space, of dimension zD. Then P Bi vanishes on Ui . Hence tr(P Q)
is at most the sum of the traces of Ki over the omitted source directions, since ci Bi† P Bi ≤ Ki and

This proves even z ≤ η + 4α + 4 e. The specialization to the actual maps requires a lower first
moment and an upper second moment of their period pairings; the latter comes from a relative
trace formula. A trace formula equates a sum over a basis of functions with a sum of geometric
integrals. Here it controls correlations between the images of different maps. These automorphic
estimates are quoted. [7, 19, 20] In the source the index i is a word: a string of k − 2 binary letters
which chooses, at every weight place, one of the weights that interlace a fixed weight of the group
in k − 1 variables; each word also has its own level prime. The space Vi is attached to the word,
and H is common to all words. The character twists of section 6.8 are a different index (one twist
for each place) and do not enter this family. There is one such family for each translate u of section
4; the weights wu,j depend on the translate and not on the word. See Part III, §III.8.

6.10 From the rank bound to (M)

It remains to see why a count of directions can control a mean slope. In this section H is a bound
for heights and u is a spread of slopes; they are not the target space H of section 6.9 and the
translate u of section 4. Let d1 , . . . , dD be real slopes, a a reference value, and assume dj ≥ a − H
with H ≥ 0. Put N (t) = #{j : dj < a − t}. Integrating the indicator of each deficit gives

                         1 X          1 X                              N (t)
                                                                 Z H
                             dj ≥ a −     (a − dj )+ = a −                   dt.                   (6.7)
                         D j          D j                         0     D

For example, slopes 2, 3, 5 and a = 4 give N (t) = 2 on 0 < t < 1, 1 on 1 < t < 2, and 0 thereafter.
The mean deficit is (2 + 1)/3 = 1. The lower bound 3 is below the true mean 10/3 because the
largest slope exceeded 4. This is the layer-cake formula; the form used by the final theorem is in
[19].
Here is how an integrable count is obtained in [20]. If R slopes yj have mean at least s and all are
at most s + u, u > 0, the fraction below s − t is at most u/(u + t). Indeed, with fraction z below,
their mean is at most z(s − t) + (1 − z)(s + u). Apply the rank bound of section 6.9 after discarding
those directions; so α = u/(u + t) in the lemma of section 6.9, which gives the term 4u/(u + t)
below.
(Passing from the bound on the missing directions to a count of the slopes of the target below a
given level uses the inequality for the minimal slope of the image of a map, and properties of the
filtration of a lattice by slopes. These are in [25]; they are quoted here, and are not the inequality
proved in section 6.8.)
In the notation of [20], for numbers s, h, L, u, H, η, e with u > 0 and L, H, η, e ≥ 0, the hypotheses
take the form
                          #{j : dj < s − h − L − t}         4u   √

bracket is 4u log(1 + H/u) + (η + 4 e)H, with η and not 2η. It is multiplied by 1/(1 − δp ), where

lower bound also has − log |W| as a separate term, which this factor does not multiply. Here W is
the set of words and log |W| ≤ (k − 3) log 2, which is negligible; the sizes of H and of Ecnt are part

                                                  51
of the quoted statements and are not derived here. The bound for the spread u and the arithmetic
control of L are also needed. This argument uses a mean slope of the target, where the elementary
inequality of section 6.8 by itself would only control a maximal slope.
Assembly. The local derivative bounds place the prime sum in the integral lattices. The period
identity makes it possible to compare the different local choices in one space. The height and
counting comparisons (6.5)–(6.6) give the prime sum’s main term. Finally the mean-slope bound
accounts for losses from indices and directions which must be discarded. The exact output of the
source’s comparison is this: for each translate, the required divisibility sum is at most its main term
plus an aggregated error [28]. The hypotheses of that statement are defined in [7]. The archive
also has the form averaged over the translates, in [7]; the final theorem does not use it. The size
estimates bound the error by the unnormalized expression of section 4.3. These are the inputs [5, 7,
19, 28] used at (M).
Once the quoted estimates are granted, sections 4 and 5 give (A), and section 3 concludes by
balancing x/m and m. A cost of m2 would instead give m = x1/3 and exponent 2/3, if the
corresponding inequality were available with the other errors under control.

Proved in Part II, section 6. Finite-chain integrality, the minor congruence, the Hasse product
rule, the reduction (6.1), the factorial count (6.1a), the Gaussian norm and the Cauchy–Schwarz
bound of section 6.5, the count identity (6.4) and the cancellation (6.6), the elementary slope
inequality, rational lattice gluing, the rank bound and its integration. (Sections 1–5 are proved in
full, given (M) and Estimates B, C.)
Quoted from the Lean source. The normalized theta maps and period identity, their
simultaneous local divisibilities and losses, the period and mass formulas, the level bounds, and
the norm bounds (apart from the analytic estimates of Part III, §III.4); the denominator estimates;
and the two moments for this family. Part III, §III.6–III.9 deduce parts of these from named
statements of the source, which remain quoted; see the table at the start of Part III.

                    Part III. Proofs of the quoted statements

Parts I and II deduce the Riemann hypothesis from statements marked [quoted]. Part III is meant
to prove those statements, in the same classical language, so that the three parts together form a
complete proof. It is being written in stages, and the table below records what has been done.

So at present Parts I–III prove the Riemann hypothesis from the inequality (M) and from the cited
theorems, and (M) is quoted. Of what (M) needs, §III.4–III.9 prove the parts stated in the table,
conditionally as stated there.
The statements below are translated from the Lean development of the announcement. In Part III
“the source” always means this Lean development. For §III.1–III.3 each is a statement that the
final theorem actually depends on (for §III.4–III.9 see the remarks in those sections): we computed
the set of all declarations on which the Lean theorem riemannHypothesis depends, and checked
that these belong to it.
For §III.1–III.3 the check was also made for the lemmas of the Lean proofs that the sections
follow (for instance QH.thinH, twistK, meanSqK, bad_share, tailK, count_bad, hostThickExists,
topsChoiceH): all are among the declarations the final theorem depends on.
     A correction to the source references of Part II. The same computation shows that several
statements cited in Part II are present in the archive but are not among the 1,478,198 declarations
on which riemannHypothesis depends: the Fock norm and jet definitions of [23]; the statements
ChainInt of [14], FrameLA and FrameLast of [20], and CountIdentity of [17] (a statement of the
same name elsewhere is used); the definitions concerning Behrend sets in [17]; and the inequality of
[6] in the form quoted. These are parallel or earlier formulations. The same holds for the linearity
statement of [13]. The mathematics of Part II does not depend on them, since what Part II proves
it proves directly; but for these items the phrase “the source’s statement” should be read as “a
statement in the archive”. The statements that are used include: the Landau lemma on intervals
[1], the auxiliary primes [9, 29], the weighted sums ASK and MAIN [3], the height of a map [25],
the Chowla–Selberg period [16], the minors congruence [15], the digit inequality StarArith [21],
the layer-cake formula, the Bessel identity and the local comparisons of [19], the bound for each
translate each_unit_bound_statement of [28] with the hypotheses defined in [7] (the averaged
statement MeanBoundSecondForm of [7] is not used), and the bound for Galois cohomology [8].

Remarks. (1) For ζ one takes M = 1 and A ≥ 32 . Then (d) gives Estimate B of Part I, with x = y,
the interval [Y, Y 3/2 ] contained in [Y, Y A ], and the exponent 12 + 2ε in place of 12 + ε.
   (2) The conductors are far larger than y: log q is a power of Y . So (d) cannot be a statement
about a fixed character as y → ∞. It is a statement about well-chosen q and d.
   (3) Condition (c) has a consequence that is not visible in Part I. If p is an odd prime with p ≤ Y ε/5 ,
then d ≡ −1 mod p, and since d ≡ 3 mod 4 quadratic reciprocity gives χd (p) = −1       p (−1)
                                                                                              (p−1)/2 = 1.
                                                                                         
                                                            √
Also χd (2) = 1. So every prime up to Y ε/5 splits in Q( −d); the inert primes, which carry the
argument of Part I, all exceed Y ε/5 .
   (4) Part (e) is used for Estimate C: it excludes exceptional real zeros for the real characters
whose conductor is divisible by q or d.


Proof of Theorem III.1.1

The proof chooses q at random from a set of primes, then d at random from a second set, and
shows that most choices work. Two things must hold for the chosen primes: a sum over n ≤ Z
must be small (this comes from a mean square over the primes), and a tail n > Z must be small
(this comes from a zero-free box, which holds for all but few primes by a zero-density theorem).
Throughout, τ = ε/2,

                           Z = Y 400A ,      z = Y ε/5 ,     z ′ = Y ε/5 (log Y )2 ,                (III.4)

and Y is large in terms of M , ε, A. Constants C, c are absolute, or depend only on A, unless
indicated.
   Cited theorems.

     form [TZ21, §1]). Let c1 = 131

     m, have no zero with β ≥ 1 − c1 / log(m(|γ| + 3)), except possibly one simple real zero β1
     of one real nonprincipal character χ1 . Moreover β1 ≤ 1 − c/m; this follows from the bound
     β1 ≤ 1 − c′ m−1/2 recorded in [TZ21, §1]. This is the exceptional zero meant in (Q2).

parts of the line Re s = 2 contribute at most Kδ log Q · w2 /Q. Divide by h = wη.

                                                    60
   Remarks. (1) There is no circularity. Nothing of the strength of the Riemann hypothesis is
assumed for the characters χ. The bound y 1/2+τ is the mean-square size of a sum of fixed length
Z = Y 400A over a family of primes q with log q ≥ 12 Y ε/6 , far beyond that length. As q varies, the
symbols ( nq ), n ≤ Z, behave like independent signs; reciprocity turns the statement into one about
primes q in progressions to moduli m0 s much smaller than q, where (Q2) applies. The zero-free
box is a weak property of the same kind, and it is proved for all but few q, by (Q3).
   (2) Part (e) is proved for every primitive ρ of conductor dividing 8M , real or not.
   (3) The mean value (III.12) is obtained without a large sieve inequality. A reader who knows
Heath-Brown’s large sieve for real characters [HB95] will recognise the statement; the route above
uses primes in progressions.


III.2     The large scale: proof of Estimate C
This section proves Estimate C from three standard theorems on Dirichlet L-functions, which
we cite, and from part (e) of Theorem III.1.1. No zero-density estimate and no mean value over
characters is used here. In the Lean development this is the theorem QH.thinH with the lemmas it
calls.

    The three theorems cited. Proofs are in Davenport [Dav00, Ch. 14, 16, 19] and Montgomery
and Vaughan [MV07]: Theorem 11.3 for (Z1), Theorem 10.17 for (Z2), and Theorems 12.5 and

form. The Lean development proves all three.
(Z1) Zero-free region (de la Vallée Poussin, Gronwall, Landau, Titchmarsh). There is an absolute
     constant c1 > 0 such that, for every N ≥ 1 and every character χ modulo N , if L(ρ, χ) = 0
     and β ≥ 1 − c1 / log(N (|γ| + 2)), then χ is real and nonprincipal and ρ is real. (For N = 1
     this says that ζ has no zero in the region.)
(Z2) Zeros in a window. There is an absolute C1 such that for every primitive χ modulo N and
     every real t, the number of zeros with 0 < β < 1 and |γ − t| ≤ 1 is at most C1 log(N (|t| + 2)).

                                                    63
III.3      The sets of columns T2 and T
The table of §I.5 has n columns, indexed by the group H of §I.5. It is cyclic of order n; fix an
isomorphism with A = Z/nZ and write it additively, so that the translates uT2 of Part II become
u0 + T2 . The construction singles out two sets of columns. Both are needed only through the
combinatorial properties proved here; how (M) uses them belongs to the part not yet written.
Throughout, x is large, 0 < ε < 52
                                1
                                   , and x2+8ε ≤ n ≤ 2x2+8ε .


The set T2 : a construction of Behrend

Theorem III.3.1 (hostThickExists in [27]; hostThickOrbit, thickFrame2). Let L ≥ 2 be an
integer with L ≤ CL log x. For x large in terms of ε and CL there is a set T2 of positive integers
with the following properties.
 (a) 4Ls < n for every s ∈ T2 , and |T2 | ≥ x−ε n.
 (b) No relation. If s1 , s2 , s3 ∈ T2 are distinct and i, j are integers with 0 < |i|, |j| ≤ L, i =
                                                                                                   ̸ j, then
     j(s2 − s1 ) ̸= i(s3 − s1 ).
 (c) Arithmetic progressions meet translates of T2 rarely. Let u0 , t, h ∈ Z/nZ. If the additive order
     of h exceeds 4L, then among any L consecutive terms t + ih at most two lie in u0 + T2 . If the
     order of h is at most 4L, at most one element of Z/nZ on the progression lies in u0 + T2 .

For i = 1, j = 2 property (b) says that T2 contains no three-term arithmetic progression. The proof
is Behrend’s [Beh46]: points on a sphere, written in a large base.
      Proof. A sphere. Let S, D ≥ 2 be integers. The DS vectors v ∈ {0, . . . , D −1}S have |v|2 < SD2 ,
so some sphere |v|2 = k contains at least DS−2 /S of them. Call this set Σ. Three points of a
sphere are never collinear. More precisely: if v1 , v2 , v3 ∈ Σ and j(v2 − v1 ) = i(v3 − v1 ) with
i, j =
     ̸ 0, i ≠ j, then v2 = v1 . Indeed put b = v2 − v1 , c = v3 − v1 . Since |v1 + b|2 = |v1 |2 we have
2v1 · b + |b|2 = 0, and likewise for c. Substitute b = ji c in the first relation and multiply by j 2 /i:
this gives 2j v1 · c + i|c|2 = 0. Subtract j times the second relation: (i − j)|c|2 = 0. So c = 0 and
then b = 0.
    Base B = 4LD. Map v 7→ s(v) = m vm B m , and let T be the image of Σ. Suppose
                                                P

j(s(v2 ) − s(v1 )) = i(s(v3 ) − s(v1 )) with i, j as in (b). The digits cm = j(v2,m − v1,m ) − i(v3,m − v1,m )
satisfy |cm | ≤ 2L(D − 1) < B and m cm B m = 0; hence every cm = 0 (look at the lowest nonzero
                                         P

digit modulo B). By the statement about the sphere, v2 = v1 . This is (b) for T .
                              √
    Parameters. Put u = log n log L, S = ⌊u/ log L⌋, a = (log n−log L−3)/S and D = ⌊ea /(4L)⌋.
For large x one has S ≥ 32, a ≥ 7 + log L, hence D ≥ ea /(8L) ≥ 2, and a ≤ 2u. Then
B S ≤ eSa < n/(4L), so 4Ls < n on T , and

                                        DS−2  n
                                             ≥ exp − 32 log n log L .                                (III.30)
                                                       p           
                               |T | ≥
                                         S    L
Since log L ≤ log log x + O(1), the right side exceeds 4x−ε n for large x. Remove 0 from T to get T2 .
This proves (a) and (b).
    Property (c). Let the order of h exceed 4L, and suppose t + ah, t + bh, t + ch lie in u0 + T2
with a < b < c < a + L: they equal u0 + sa , u0 + sb , u0 + sc . The s’s are distinct because (b − a)h,
(c − a)h, (c − b)h ̸= 0. Eliminating h,

                          X = (c − a)(sb − sa ) − (b − a)(sc − sa ) ≡ 0 mod n.                       (III.31)

But |X| < n, because 4Ls < n for each s. So X = 0, against (b). If the order m of h is at most 4L
and two points of the progression are u0 + s1 , u0 + s2 , then n | m(s1 − s2 ) and |m(s1 − s2 )| < n, so
s1 = s2 .


The set T : a random choice

Let δ1 = x−ε/8 . The set T ⊂ A is chosen so that |T | is about δ1 n and so that, for most primes p
and most t ∈ T , the points of T on the orbit of t under the Frobenius of p are evenly spread. The
precise meaning is this.
    Good orbits. Let n′ ≥ 1 and W ⊂ {0, . . . , n′ − 1}, read cyclically. Fix integers L2 , K, r ≥ 1,
and put u = ⌈1/δ1 ⌉, ℓ = (6r + 1)u, M0 = ⌈(5 + 8ε) 106r+1 log x⌉, G′ = (M0 + 5)ℓ and N1 =
⌈max(6eG′ δ1 , (5 + 8ε) log2 x)⌉ + 1. Cut {0, . . . , n′ − 1} into ⌊n′ /ℓ⌋ consecutive tiles of length ℓ (a
remainder shorter than ℓ is ignored, and tiles are numbered cyclically), and each tile into 6r + 1
cells of length u, numbered 1, . . . , 6r + 1. A tile is marked if its odd-numbered cells contain no
point of W and each even-numbered cell contains exactly one point of W , at an even position. Call
W good if
(G1) every cyclic window of length L2 contains at most K points of W ;
(G2) among any M0 + 1 consecutive tiles (cyclically) at least one is marked;
(G3) every cyclic window of length G′ contains at most N1 points of W .

Theorem III.3.2 (topsGroup, topsChoiceH). Let Lb > 0, K = ⌈8(4 + 8ε)/ε⌉ and r = K + 1.
There are Cb and x0 such that for x ≥ x0 the following holds. Let L2 ≤ Lb . Let P be a finite set of
primes, and for p ∈ P let hp ∈ A have order np ≥ xε/4 . For each p and each coset of ⟨hp ⟩ fix a
base point b on it; for t ∈ A let Wp (t, T ) = {0 ≤ i < np : b + ihp ∈ T }, b being the base point of the
coset of t. Then there is T ⊂ A with
                                   X log p                                                         X log p
2 δ1 n ≤ |T | ≤ 2δ1 n

                                  p∈P

In the application P consists of the inert primes below exp(xε/3 ) whose Frobenius has order at least
xε/4 in A, and L2 is bounded by a constant depending only on ε (the source gives 2(620 Co /ε + 1)
for primes p > xε/(5Co ) , with a constant Co ≥ 2 that is defined in the source). That L2 is bounded
uses Remark (3) of §III.1: every inert prime exceeds Y ε/5 .
   Proof. Choose T at random: each element of A belongs to T independently with probability δ1 .
(For Dirichlet characters other than the trivial one the source uses two different probabilities, δ1
and 34 δ1 , on different cosets. For ζ there is one coset.)
    Size. |T | has mean δ1 n and variance at most δ1 n.
   One orbit. Fix p and t, write n′ = np , and condition on t ∈ T . The other points of the orbit
remain independent. We bound the probability that W = Wp (t, T ) is not good.
    (G1) fails only if some window  of length L2 contains K points other than t. For one window
this has probability at most LK2 δ1K ≤ LK    −Kε/8 ≤ LK x−(4+8ε) . There are n′ windows.

                                        3r+1  u                  3r

since uδ1 is between 1 and 1.01. Among M0 + 1 consecutive tiles at most one contains t; the

t ∈ T with Wp (t, T ) not good is at most Cb x−2 δ1 n, for every p.
    Choice. We may assume P is not empty. Let V = (|T | − δ1 n)2 /(2δ1 n) and let B be the left side
of the inequality of the theorem divided by four times its expected upper bound Cb x−2 δ1 n p logp p .
                                                                                           P

Then E(V ) ≤ 12 and E(B) ≤√ 14 . So some T has V + B ≤ 1. For it B ≤ 1, which is the second
assertion, and |T | − δ1 n ≤ 2δ1 n ≤ 12 δ1 n, which gives the first.


III.4      The size of the theta kernels (partly written)
This section concerns the upper bound for the size of the maps: inequality (6.5) of Part II, first
line. It is partly written. The following are proved below: the one-variable model; the coefficient
estimate in several variables and in several blocks, with the period; the bound for the polynomial
on the unit sphere; the bound for one map and for a chain of maps; the bound for a theta function
by its mean square; and the hook product. Four passages are conditional: the reference mean
square; the eigenvalues of a lift; the comparison, row by row, of the kernel degrees with the hook
product; and the norm of the pairing at the end of the chain. The last two are deductions from
inputs of the source that are named where they are used. The passage from these estimates to (6.5)
is not written. It needs the arithmetic statements listed at the end of the section, none of which
is proved in these pages. So the first line of (6.5) is still quoted. We begin
                                                                             √ with the one-variable
model. It shows where the two features of the bound come from: the size n! and the sign of the
term with the period.
                                                                        2
    The model. Let F be an entire function with |F (z)| ≤ S e|z| /2 for all z, where S > 0. Then
for n ≥ 1
                                                      e n/2
                                  |F (n) (0)| ≤ S n!          ,                           (III.34)
                                                       n
so that, if F (n) (0) ̸= 0,

                          log |F (n) (0)|2 ≤ 2 log S + n log n − n + O(log(n + 1)).              (III.35)
                                                                             2               √
Proof. By Cauchy’s formula on the circle |z| = r, |F (n) (0)| ≤ n! S er /2 r−n . Take r =        n. The
second form follows from Stirling’s formula, log n! = n log n − n + O(log(n + 1)).
So a Gaussian bound for the function gives, for the square of its nth derivative, the size n log n − n:
this is log n!. Compare a function with a pole at finite distance, such as 1/(1 − z): its nth derivative
at 0 is n!, and the logarithm of the square is 2 log n!. That was the situation of the lemniscate sine
in §I.3. Theta functions satisfy such a Gaussian bound. This is point (i) of §I.4.2.
    The period. If the coordinate is changed by a factor, z = λw with |λ|2 = 1/Ω, then the nth
derivative with respect to w is λn times that with respect to z, and the bound for the logarithm of


                                                     66
its square becomes
                              n log n − n − n log Ω + 2 log S + O(log(n + 1)).                              (III.36)
This is the shape a(log a − 1 − log Ω) of (6.5), with its sign.
                                                          √     The constant relevant there is ΩE , not
the elliptic Ω of §I.7; the two differ by the factor rdE / d.
    The paragraphs that follow carry the model over to the setting of the proof: several complex
variables, a polynomial with values in linear maps in place of a single derivative, several blocks
of variables (one for each place), and sums over classes of lattices. Those up to and including the
bound for a theta function by its mean square, and the hook product, are complete as stated; the
four passages named above are conditional on inputs from the source. The first of them assumes
a Gaussian bound S for the function and a bound for the polynomial on the unit sphere; both
assumptions are then proved in an abstract setting; for the first, the mean square of one reference
theta function is bounded under the inputs (I1)–(I5) listed at the head of that paragraph. That
the actual theta kernels and polynomials of the construction are instances of this setting is taken
from the source: the hypotheses of the majorant lemma with v = rdE , the form of the constant
S with its factors β and c, and the identification of the polynomial P of the construction with a
substitution of the kind treated below.

To see this, multiply the first k rows of X by an indeterminate t. On the dense set where the upper
left k by k minor is invertible, subtract combinations of the first k columns from each later column
to make its first k entries zero. These operations commute with the row scaling and preserve
f . Now t occurs only in the first k columns, so the resulting polynomial in t has degree at most
   i≤k νi . This holds for all X by polynomial identity. Equality for k = r is total homogeneity.

    Row substitutions by unitary matrices preserve both the Gaussian norm and the two defining
properties of Wν . Hence the singular value decomposition of A reduces the claim to diagonal row
scaling by the si , with the unused rows of Y integrated out. For positive si , partial summation
of the degree inequalities against the decreasing numbers log si gives i di log si ≤ i νi log si .
                                                                        P               P

Thus the multiplier of every monomial is at most i si . Distinct monomials are orthogonal in
the Gaussian norm by coordinate rotations. This proves the claim. Zero singular values follow by
continuity, and the sum of their squares is the trace of AA∗ .
   Why the norm factors cancel. In the application the input degree vector is µ, and the
polynomial is multiplied by det(X)e before substitution, for an integer e ≥ 0. This gives precisely

                                                             68
νi = µi + e. For a degree vector µ let pµ be the product of the leading i by i minors raised to
µi − µi+1 , where µr+1 = 0. The norm on Wµ is ∥f ∥G /∥pµ ∥G . In this norm multiplication by dete
is an isometry from Wµ to its image in Wν .
     Indeed, write a nonsingular Gaussian matrix as X = QR by Gram–Schmidt on its columns,
with Q unitary and R upper triangular with positive diagonal. The defining column operations
                       Q µi
give f (QR) = f (Q) i Rii    . In computing ∥f ∥2G , we may first average |f (U X)|2 over unitary U
with its invariant probability measure, since the Gaussian measure of X is unchanged. The average
of |f (U Q)|2 is the average of |f (U )|2 . Hence the squared Gaussian norm is the unitary average of
|f (U )|2 times a factor depending only on µ. Singular matrices have measure zero. Dividing by the
same formula for pµ cancels the factor. Since pν = dete pµ and | det U | = 1, the normalised norm
is unchanged by the shift. Padding ν with zeros to length b preserves the norm of pν , because it
then depends only on the upper left r by r variables. Thus the normalised substitution has norm
at most i sνi i . For independent matrix blocks the maps form a tensor product; its norm is the
           Q

product of the individual norms, as follows by singular value decomposition. On each unit sphere
   i si = 1. This supplies the polynomial bound used in the coefficient estimate.

The reference section of ranks N, N ′ has squared norm Θ0,ρ = Hρf N N , where f = 2n is the number
of weight places (n as in Part II). This last identity uses the tensor-power description of the

stated at the head of the paragraph.

    The hook product. The hook product enters as an exact Gaussian integral. Given integers
λ1 ≥ · · · ≥ λs ≥ 0, form a diagram with λi boxes in row i, left justified. The hook length of a box
is one plus the numbers of boxes to its right in the same row and below it in the same column. Let
H(λ) be the product of all hook lengths, with empty product one. For a square complex matrix X
of size s put

    The next identity is needed for the norm of C: C ∗ pσ′ = ξ/κ. The operator C commutes with
unitary substitutions on the first ψ rows, as does its adjoint. Such an intertwiner also commutes
with complex invertible row substitutions: differentiate on the unitary group and take complex
linear combinations, since skew-Hermitian matrices span all matrices over C. Thus C ∗ pσ′ has row
degrees σi′ in the first ψ rows and is invariant under adding an earlier row to a later one among them.
(Rows are meant here, where the definition of Wν used columns: this step uses that pσ′ has row
degrees σi′ and is unchanged when an earlier row is added to a later one, as well as the corresponding
properties for columns.) There is at most one such polynomial up to scalar in the weight space of
the domain. Indeed, where the leading minors are nonzero, these row operations and the allowed
column operations reduce the upper ψ by ψ matrix to a diagonal matrix. On this slice row and
                                                    σ′
column degrees force a single monomial, i Xiii zibi . Polynomial identity extends uniqueness to
                                               Q

all matrices. The polynomial ξ has these properties, and pairing with ξ gives the asserted scalar.
The unitary row translates of pσ′ span the target. To see this, any nonzero invariant orthogonal
complement would contain a vector of lexicographically largest row degree among its diagonal torus
weights. Adding an earlier row to a later one fixes this vector, since every nonconstant term of
the substitution raises that weight. Reduction to a diagonal matrix as above now identifies this
vector with a multiple of pσ′ . It could not lie in the complement. It follows by equivariance that
CC ∗ = I/κ on the target.
    The integral normalisation is an input from the source. There the map is ϑκC (Lean: brStd,
with the constant brConst), where ϑ is the following nonzero integer. For each i ≤ ψ take all
integers v from λi+1 to min(λi , λi+1 + d0 ), omitting σi . Multiply v − i − (σj − j) over these i, v and
over 1 ≤ j ≤ ψ. Interlacing gives v − i > σj − j for i < j and the reverse inequality for i > j. The
omission excludes zero when i = j. Put Γ = λ1 − λΨ + Ψ and d1 = ψ          i=1 min(λi − λi+1 , d0 ). Each
                                                                        P

factor has absolute value at most Γ and there are ψd1 of them. What the source shows to be integral
is the transposed map ϑκC ∗ (Lean: BranchMap); the map ϑκC itself can have denominators. A
map and its adjoint have the same norm, so the statements about norms here are not affected. By
CC ∗ = I/κ, the squared norm of either map is ϑ2 κ.
   One can also bound κ by the Gaussian calculation above. The two polynomials in its ratio,
ξ and pλ′ , have identical column degrees, and both are unchanged when a multiple of an earlier

                                                     79
III.5     Divisibility at inert primes
This section concerns the lower bound for the exponent of an inert prime: inequality (I.19) of §I.4.3.
It proves the local estimate behind that bound, for power series, under one hypothesis of integrality,
called (B) below. In this section the prime is called ℓ, as in the Lean files; it is the inert prime p of
Part I. Several letters used here have other meanings elsewhere in the paper, among them β, λ, ε,
a, p, d, x, N , D, q, k, m, n, f , c, σ, e and ψ; in this section each has the meaning given where it is
introduced.
    Proved outright. The valuation of the coefficients of the inverse of the height-two logarithm, in
several variables (Lemma 1); the integrality lemma (Lemma 2); the optimisation for the top row
(Lemma 6), which produces the sum h odd (A/ℓh − wh )+ ; weak duality for the linear program of
                                     P

§5; and the closed form of that program for orbits of length two.
    Proved conditionally on hypothesis (B). Lemmas 3 and 4: the passage from (B) to a bound for
a series β in the exponent, with the gain from 1/(ℓ2 − 1) to 1/(ℓ − 1). Proposition 5: the bound
for one Taylor coefficient of a function U · exp(±β), with weights on the variables. Theorem J, for
orbits of length two: the bound for a product of minors applied to such a function.
    Conditional on (B) and on strong duality, cited from the source. Theorem J for orbits of
length four or more. The cited statement is the equality of a linear program with its dual (Lean:
strongDuality). This case was not tested end to end.
    Taken from the source without proof. The transcription of the top-level inequality LinkG_at (§1
below); that the theta kernels of the construction have the form U · exp(±β) and satisfy (B); the
dictionary between sites and places; the frames and labels that Theorem J requires; the passage
from Taylor coefficients (jets) to lattices and to the inequality that the final theorem uses; and the
remaining primes. These are listed at the end.


1.   The statement in the source

The top-level inequality. The final theorem depends on the Lean statement LinkG, of the
file GlobalTheoremInputs.lean. It does so through the theorems closed_linkG_of_duty and
linkG_concrete5. Its third clause is the inequality of Part II, §6.4, last paragraph. We state it.
The scale is x > 1, 0 < ε < 1/52, b = exp(xε ), a = xb, X ′ = exp(xε/3 ), b′ = a/X ′ , w′′ = b′ /(xn),
X ′′ = a/w′′ , p0 = k 2 x26 , δ1 = x−ε/8 . Here m (the number of lifts), k and t0 are integer parameters
of the construction (the fields m, k, t of D.R); n is a field of the parent structure (ord); and f = 2n
and Nm = k + m(t0 + 1) are defined quantities, not fields. T is a finite index set (D.host.TT).
The following are given data (fields of the structure SizeData2 ): integers at for t ∈ T with
a ≤ at ≤ a + a/(kx13 ); real numbers gain(j, w) and τ (w) for 1 ≤ j ≤ m and w a place of the
coefficient field; real numbers wPart(j, t, p, i) ≥ 1; subsets Tw ⊂ T ; and real numbers Sc, η, ΛG .

The statement LinkG asserts this, with four further clauses, for all large x, with a constant C and

in the field Q( −d) of Part I (this d is the prime of Part I; everywhere else in this section d is
the length of an orbit), is of class signal or inertClass according to the value +1 or −1 of one
further sign; DataHost.lean.)
    What this statement is. The inequality (III.79) is an inequality between the numbers gain, τ ,
wPart. In the Lean it is not a statement about valuations. A separate field of the family (fills, of
type ParametersFill) identifies these numbers with explicit formulas (gainP, tauP), and the proof
of (III.79) (linkG_at_core) rewrites by ParametersFill and concludes by linear arithmetic from
a hypothesis AuxBlock8.Conclusion1, which already is the inequality for the formulas, together
with Conclusion0, lo_le_loG and fl_le_flG. The calculation with the formulas is in stmtF. The
statements which identify gain with a valuation of theta kernels are other hypotheses and other
theorems of the development. We do not claim more than this about (III.79). The Lean theorem in
which a valuation of Taylor coefficients is bounded below by a sum of the shape (III.76) is ThmAOp
(StatementI.lean, proved in ThmAOp.lean); the final theorem depends on its proof thmAOp: this
is confirmed by the dependency computation (see §6 (iii)). The rest of this section is about that
theorem.
    One function, several places. Let K be a field of characteristic zero with a non-archimedean

Frobenius followed by complex conjugation (this identification is made in the source and is assumed
here). Let N0 , N1 ≥ 1. The variables are Zs,q,c and Xs,i,c , for s a site, q, i < N0 , c < N1 . A frame is
a family ξ(s, i, q) ∈ K with v ≥ 0; it gives the substitution Zs,q,c = i ξ(s, i, q)Xs,i,c . The function
