2.   Weights, and the inverse of the logarithm

Weights. Let f = n fn X n be a power series in finitely many variables Xi , with coefficients in K.
                     P

Let r = (ri ) be real numbers ≥ 0 and w a real number. Write n · r = i ni ri and |n| = i ni . We
                                                                       P                   P

say that f has weight w at radii r, and write f ∈ W(r, w), if v(fn ) ≥ w − n · r for all n. This says
that the Gauss norm of f on the polydisc v(Xi ) ≥ ri is at most ℓ−w . (Lean: WtGe.) The following
rules are immediate from v(x + y) ≥ min(v(x), v(y)).
 (W1) If f ∈ W(r, w) and h ∈ W(r, w′ ) then f h ∈ W(r, w + w′ ). If r ≤ r′ then W(r, w) ⊂ W(r′ , w).
 (W2) If f ∈ W(r, w) and the series ai have no constant term and ai ∈ W(r′ , ri ), then f (a) ∈
      W(r′ , w). (A monomial fn an lies in W(r′ , w − n · r + n · r).)
 (W3) If Y has no constant term and Y ∈ W(r, ℓ−1
                                              1
                                                 ), then exp(Y ) − 1 and log(1 + Y ) lie in
      W(r, ℓ−1 ).
            1


 (W4) The coefficient of X κ in f h, in exp(Y ) and in log(1 + Y ) depends only on the coefficients of
      X n with n ≤ κ; so (W1) and (W3) hold with “for all n” replaced by “for all n ≤ κ”.

                                                                   82
For (W3): Y k ∈ W(r, ℓ−1  k
                            ), and v(k) ≤ v(k!) = (k − σℓ (k))/(ℓ − 1) ≤ (k − 1)/(ℓ − 1), where
σℓ (k) ≥ 1 is the sum of the digits of k in base ℓ. So Y k /k! and Y k /k lie in W(r, ℓ−1
                                                                                       1
                                                                                          ). (Lean:
MulWtLe, RadMono, SubstWt, LogStep. The Lean statement ExpWtLe is not (W3): it gives weight
0 for the exponential of a series, truncated at κ, which is what Proposition 5 uses.)
   Lemma 1 (inverse of the logarithm). Let Y be a finite set and π : Y → Y a map. For
y ∈ Y put
                                                2e
                              Ly (X) =   ℓ−e Xπℓ e (y) .                       (III.84)
                                       X

                                                     e≥0

There is a unique family g = (gy ) of power series without constant term with Ly (g) = Xy for all y.
It satisfies gy ∈ W(ρ, ρ), where the radius is ρ for every variable:
                             v coefficient of X n in gy        ≥ − |n| − 1 ρ.                    (III.85)
                                                                         

                                                2e
Proof. The equation is gy = Xy − e≥1 ℓ−e gπℓe (y) . It determines the coefficients by induction on |n|,
                                   P

since the terms with e ≥ 1 involve only coefficients of lower degree. The bound holds for |n| = 1.
Let |n| ≥ 2 and r = ℓ2e , e ≥ 1. The coefficient of X n in an rth power is a sum of products of r
coefficients, of degrees |n1 |, . . . , |nr | ≥ 1 with sum |n|. By induction such a product has valuation
at least −(|n| − r)ρ. Division by ℓe costs e, and e ≤ 1 + ℓ2 + · · · + ℓ2(e−1) = (r − 1)ρ.
This is Part II, §2, in several variables. (Lean: GenLogWeight, generalized_log_weight; the
series (III.84) is genLog. Also g(L(X)) = X: GenLogInverse.) In one variable (π the identity) a
computation confirms (III.85) for ℓ = 3, 5, 7 and all degrees up to 801, 1201, 1201: the minimum of
v(gn ) + (n − 1)ρ is 0, attained exactly at n = ℓ2e . The bound cannot be improved.


3.   From integrality to a bound for β

In (III.80) the factor U (g(Z)) lies in W(ρ, 0), by Lemma 1 and (W2). After multiplication by κ!
this gives the exponent |κ| ( ℓ−1
                               1
                                  − ρ) = |κ|γ of §I.3. For the exponential factor to do as well, by
(W3) the series β should have weight ℓ−1 1
                                             at radii ρ. Integrality gives directly only the weight ρ.
The gain from ρ to ℓ−1 is the content of this paragraph. It uses that the degrees of the monomials
                      1

are divisible by ℓ + 1. The proof given below also uses that the coefficients lie in a field with value
group Z (an unramified field); the source carries this hypothesis (Unram, in BHyp), and it is kept as
(B1). The statements do not need it: see the Remarks after Theorem J.
    Hypothesis (B). Let K0 ⊂ K be a subfield with v(K0× ) = Z. Let ζ ∈ K be a primitive root
of unity of order ℓd − 1. For a site s, represented by 0 ≤ s < d, put lw(s) = (−1)s ℓs (the letters
are those of the Lean). The series β, in variables Xs (s ∈ Z/d), satisfies: (B1) its coefficients lie in
K0 ; (B2) β (ζ lw(s) Xs )s = β(X); (B3) every monomial of β has degree ≥ 1 in the variables at even
sites, degree ≥ 1 in the variables at odd sites, and total degree ≥ 3; (B4) for the two series
                        ∆1 β = β(xev + yev , xod ) − β(xev , xod ) − β(yev , xod ),
                                                                                                 (III.86)
                        ∆2 β = β(xev , xod + yod ) − β(xev , xod ) − β(xev , yod ),
in which the variables at the even (resp. odd) sites are doubled, the series exp(∆i β) ◦ L has all
coefficients of valuation ≥ 0. Here L is (III.84) for the doubled set of variables, with π the shift
s 7→ s − 2 of the site. (Lean: BHyp; ∆1 , ∆2 are de1, de2; L is letterLog.) Hypothesis (B4) says
that exp(∆i β) becomes integral when written in the coordinates T = g(X) of the formal group. It
is assumed here, from the source.
    Lemma 2 (quantization). Let c ∈ K0 and let N be an integer divisible by ℓ + 1. If
                             1
v(c) ≥ ρ − N ρ, then v(c) ≥ ℓ−1 − N ρ.

    Lemma 3. Assume (B). Then ∆1 β and ∆2 β lie in W(ρ, ℓ−1
                                                         1
                                                            ).
    Proof. Let ∆ be one of them and E = exp(∆) ◦ L. By (B3) ∆ has no constant term, so E
has constant term 1. Since L ◦ g is the identity, exp(∆) − 1 = (E − 1) ◦ g. By (B4), Lemma 1
and (W2), a monomial of degree j ≥ 1 of E − 1 contributes a series in W(ρ, jρ) ⊂ W(ρ, ρ). So
exp(∆) − 1 ∈ W(ρ, ρ). Its coefficients lie in K0 , by (B1). By (B2), ∆ and exp(∆) are unchanged
when each variable at the site s is multiplied by ζ lw(s) . So a monomial X n with nonzero coefficient
has nx lw(sx ) ≡ 0 modulo ℓd − 1, where sx is the site of the variable x. Now ℓ + 1 divides ℓd − 1,
    P

because d is even, and lw(s) ≡ (−1)s (−1)s = 1 modulo ℓ + 1. Hence ℓ + 1 divides |n|. Lemma 2
gives exp(∆) − 1 ∈ W(ρ, ℓ−1
                          1
                             ), and (W3) gives the same for ∆ = log(1 + (exp(∆) − 1)).
(Lean: asmS_diff in ConstantCoeffEvenDerivativeEqZero.lean, with ExpDeInv for the divisi-
bility.) A computation tests the chain of Lemmas 1–3 in one variable: for Y = φ(g(X)) with φ
an integral series in T ℓ+1 , and ∆ = log(1 + Y ), the minimum over n ≤ 240 of v(∆n ) − ( ℓ−1

In the first case the factorial contributes κ/(ℓ − 1) = κ(γ + ρ) and the function costs κρ. In the
second the exceptional terms can cost a further κ/(ℓ + 1). (Lean, for the second: OneLetterRadius.
The Lean statement OneLetterR is for several variables through an integral matrix, with exponent
(sgn/2) β(Z, ep · Z).) A computation tests (III.91) with exact rational arithmetic for ℓ = 3, 5, 7

and all κ ≤ 240, with U a random series with integer coefficients that are units at ℓ, and Bn of the
least valuation allowed. The minimum over κ of the left side minus the right side is 14 , 16 , 18 in the

                                                       85
first case and 34 , 65 , 87 in the second. In the second case the first bound fails: at κ = 240 the left side
is below κγ − λ(κ) by 57, 37, 28. So the cost κ/(ℓ + 1) is real in this model (240/(ℓ + 1) = 60, 40, 30).

Proof. Let j0 be the least odd j < J with cj ≥ A/ℓj , and j0 = J if there is none. Take ω = ℓ−j0 .

Here ω is the radius of the variables of a top row of length A, and cj bounds the degree in those
variables at the site s + j which are linked to the top row by an exceptional monomial. Such a
variable needs the radius (1 − ℓj ω)+ , by (III.89). The right side of (III.93), with both of its sums,
is top(s) of (III.82) for A = ν1 (s), cj = cap(s + j) and J = dtop (s): the second sum of (III.93),
over odd j > J, is the second sum in the definition of top(s). The lemma asserts that a suitable ω
exists, not that (III.93) holds for every ω ≥ ℓ−J . If dtop (s) = ∞ and only the j with ℓj ≤ A occur
on the left, the same choice (with ω = 0 if there is no j0 ) gives j odd (A/ℓj − cj )+ on the right.

    Theorem J. Assume (B), with ℓ an odd prime and d ≥ 2 even. Let a shape, a set TT , the
polynomial P and the integer ā be as in §1. Call a row (s, i) ordinary if it is not a top row. Call a

and ℓ−j ≤ size(s) if j < 0. Let spec be an injective map from a set of ordinary rows to pairs (s0 , j),
such that s0 , j, (s, i) are linked whenever spec(s, i) = (s0 , j); the pair is the label of the row. Here
j is an integer, not a class modulo d: (s0 , j) and (s0 , j + d) are different labels for the same site.
Suppose that whenever s0 , j, (s, i) are linked and (s0 , j) is not the label of (s, i), the frame satisfies

    Proof. Since P has integer coefficients, it suffices to prove v(κ!Θκ ) ≥ B ′ for each monomial X κ
of P . A determinant is linear in each row. So the degree of X κ in the variables of the row (s, i)
is the number of c with len(s, c) > i. It is ν1 (s) for i = 0, and at most ν2 (s) for i ≥ 1. Hence the
degree is ν1 (s) in a top row, at most cap(s) in an ordinary row, and D(s) in all ordinary rows at s
together; and each κy ≤ ā.
    Radii. Let σ be an optimal dual for D, as above. For s ∈ TT let ω(s) be given by Lemma 6 with

ordinary rows before the minors are formed. That generality is not proved here. The inequality for
the sum was tested on 3000 random shapes.)
   Remarks. The following are observations of the test of this section. They are recorded without
proof, and nothing above depends on them.
    (1) The field K0 . The field K0 , hypothesis (B1) and Lemma 2 are not used in Lemmas 3–4,
Proposition 5 and Theorem J, except in the proof of Lemma 3 as written. The reason is this. In
that proof the series E = exp(∆) ◦ L is invariant under the torus, because L commutes with it.
Hence every monomial of E − 1 has degree j divisible by ℓ + 1, so j ≥ ℓ + 1, and it contributes a
series in W(ρ, jρ) ⊂ W(ρ, (ℓ + 1)ρ) = W(ρ, ℓ−11
                                                 ). This is the conclusion of Lemma 3. The Lean
statement BinomExtract has no K0 either.
    (2) Strong duality. The strong duality cited from the source is the duality theorem of linear
programming together with two elementary reductions. First, replacing σ by min(σ, 1) keeps the
constraints and does not increase s σ(s)D(s). Second, for odd i ≥ d write i = i′ + kd with i′ odd
                                    P
                                            ′
and 1 ≤ i′ < d; then ℓi σ(s) + σ(s + i) ≥ ℓi σ(s) + σ(s + i′ ) ≥ 1, so every dual satisfies the inequalities
for all odd i ≥ 1. The equality was confirmed in every instance computed (d = 2, . . . , 10).
    (3) The prime 2. That ℓ is odd is not used in the proofs of this section, and Lemma 1 holds
for ℓ = 2. No series with (B) was constructed for ℓ = 2, so Lemmas 3–4 and Theorem J were not
tested for ℓ = 2.
    An example. The test of this section constructed an explicit series β for orbits of length two
                                                                                       2
(d = 2). Let ℓ ≡ 3 mod 4. Let Ec be the curve Y 2 = X 3 − cX, with c in Z[i] and c(ℓ −1)/4 ≡ −1
modulo ℓ (c = −i for ℓ = 3 and 11, c = −1 − i for ℓ = 7). It is a twist of the lemniscate curve,
the curve of the lemniscate sine of §I.3. Let σc be the Weierstrass σ-function of Ec (with g2 = 4c,


                                                                 88
g3 = 0), let t = −X/Y , and put m(z) = log σc (z)/t(z) and β̃(z, w) = m(z + w) − m(z) − m(w).
                                                           

The series β(X0 , X1 ) is the part of β̃ that is invariant under (X0 , X1 ) 7→ (ζX0 , ζ −ℓ X1 ); the field
is K0 = Qℓ (i). Hypothesis (B4) was checked in exact arithmetic: the series exp(∆1 β) ◦ L has
coefficients integral at ℓ up to total degree 84 for ℓ = 3, 72 for ℓ = 7 and 144 for ℓ = 11. That (B)
holds in all degrees was not proved; the test has a sketch of an argument, which was not written
out as a proof. On this β, Lemmas 3 and 4 were checked up to degree 800, and equality holds in
(III.88) at exceptional monomials: there the valuation of the coefficient is exactly ℓ−1   1
                                                                                              − |n|ρ − 1.
So the tested coefficients attain the loss of one unit. This shows that the term ex(n) is sharp for
the finite part of the series that was verified; sharpness under (B) in all degrees would follow if (B4)
were proved for this example. Theorem J was checked on this β in exact arithmetic, for minors
of size one, with ℓ = 3, 7, 11, 19: the bound holds, both with B and with B ′ , in each of 1 041 934
comparisons, of which 147 296 have B > 0 and 266 970 have B ′ > 0. It also holds in every case
computed with minors of size two at one site. These checks use β in degrees beyond those to which
(B4) was checked.
    Comparison with §I.4.3. The description there is (III.83). The weight wh is cap(s0 + h):
the number of minors at the place reached by the hth power of the Frobenius composed with
conjugation. It enters in two steps. First, an exceptional monomial of β links the top row at s0
to a row at s0 + h, by (III.88); only such a link forces a radius, by (III.89). Second, a monomial
of a product of minors has degree at most cap in one row; this is the statement of §I.4.3 that a
minor absorbs one derivative in a linked direction. Lemma 6 then balances the radius of the top
row against the radii of the linked rows, and the result is (A/ℓh − wh )+ at each odd h. The labels
with (III.98) express that at each h only one row at the place s0 + h is linked. The congruence of
Part II, §6.3 is a statement of the same kind as (III.98). The source does not use it by name at
this point: see §6 (ii).


6.   Assumed here, from the source

  (i) The form (III.80) of the theta kernels, and hypothesis (B). In the Lean this is the statement
      Loc.AdmB, of the file ProductZerothFirstParts.lean. It says that there are a unit e and a
      series β such that the kernel, in the coordinates e · L, equals U · exp(± β) ◦ L with U integral
                                                                               P

      (clause B0; also OrbitThetaExpansion), with coefficients in the fraction field of the Witt ring
      (B1), torus invariance (B2), the condition on degrees (B3), and the integrality (B4) (B4). The
      hypothesis hServ.pdv (SelectionServed) of the final theorem does not contain Loc.AdmB
      literally: it gives BeOK (B1–B4 for each orbit representative) and ThetaExpansionAllPlaces.
      How these give Loc.AdmB was not read. Nothing of it is proved here. The passage from
      Loc.AdmB to BHyp (tieB) was not read.
 (ii) The frames. Theorem J needs a labelling and (III.98). In the Lean the theorem for all
      frames at once is ThmAGauss: every coefficient of the polynomial frameJet in the frame
      variables has valuation ≥ B, if 2 logℓ ā + 2 ≤ N0 − 1. Its proof (thmAGauss_of) names
      residuallyGenericExists: there are frames whose entries are residually independent units
      (IsGeneric). Three further inputs are supplied to it as hypotheses, in thmAGauss: a trans-
      fer of the hypotheses between fields (admissibleTransfer, of type TransferB), the eval-
      uation of the frame polynomial (FrameJetEval, GaussPow), and the existence of labels
      (labelsExist_statement, of type LabelsExist). The statement TransferB contains no
      frame: it transports Unram, BHyp, the inverse g of the logarithm and the integrality of U along
      a norm-preserving homomorphism of fields. The proofs of these inputs were not read. The
      congruence key_minor_dvd of Part II, §6.3 is not used by name in this step: no file under


                                                   89
      Formalism/GaussJetBounds mentions it. Its only use by name is in its own file; that file
      is imported by Loc4ResOfLine and LiftSubstStart, in the directory CoarseMultiplicity,
      which were not read.
(iii) From Taylor coefficients to the statement used. The comparison of F (D) and top(s) with the
      weights of the construction (normCor7; split_bound_eleven_one, conditional on Loc.AdmB);
      the factorial count StarArith and the inequality BCore, which is of the form (6.1) of
      Part II; the identification of sites with places and of ν1 with the weights. Then the loss
      terms loG , flG of (III.77); the lattice clauses of LinkG_at; stLoss7; placesF_min’. For
      stLoss7 and placesF_min’ only the statements were read. On the route from LinkG to
      the final theorem that was read, the clauses about valuations of kernels are proved for
      the zero chain (linkG_without_kernels); how ThmAOp enters the final theorem was not
      read. The dependency computation shows that thmAOp is among the declarations the fi-
      nal theorem depends on. By occurrences of names it reaches the final theorem through
      thmAGauss, thmA_of, split_bound_eleven_one and sum_over_primes_service, that is,
      through SelectionServed.
 (iv) The other primes. The primes of class inertClass are at least 13 for large x (thirteen_le,
      which has a hypothesis that x is large and uses the assumed splitting of the small primes,
      split_small). The primes of the other classes, and the primes above X ′′ , are treated by
      other clauses, not discussed here.

     What this section proves, and what it assumes. Proved outright: Lemma 1 (the inverse
of the logarithm), Lemma 2 (integrality), Lemma 6 (the optimisation for the top row), weak duality
(III.95), and the closed form (III.97) for orbits of length two. Proved conditionally on hypothesis
(B): Lemmas 3 and 4, Proposition 5, and Theorem J for d = 2. Conditional on (B) and on strong
duality, which is cited from the source (strongDuality): Theorem J for d ≥ 4; this case was not
tested end to end. Taken from the source without proof: the transcription of the top-level inequality
LinkG_at in §1; that the theta kernels satisfy (B) (item (i)); the dictionary between sites and
places; the frames and labels (item (ii)); the passage from jets to lattices (item (iii)); the remaining
primes (item (iv)). Theorem J is proved without the operator on the ordinary rows that the Lean
statement ThmAOp allows.
    Relation to the declarations the final theorem depends on. By the dependency computa-
tion, the following declarations named or used in this section are among them: strongDuality,
weightQuantization, lemmaS_of, thmAOp, binomial_coefficient_extraction, lpF_eq_Fnum,
residuallyGenericExists, theorem_A_generic and gauss_sum_powers. The four statements
TopRadius, top_radius_holds, LemmaR and ThmABound are in the archive but are not used by the
final theorem. So Lemma 6 and the weighted bound for one Taylor coefficient (Proposition 5) are
proved here independently of the route of the final theorem, and the declarations the final theorem
uses instead are asmA_top and lemma_R_log_loss. Their statements are consistent with Lemma 6
and Proposition 5: for a finite cut-off, and for the set of all odd j < J, asmA_top is (III.93) with
top(s) moved to the other side, and its proof takes the same radius (for an infinite cut-off it is the
remark after Lemma 6; see §III.6, §4), and Proposition 5 is the stronger of the two bounds for a
coefficient.
   What remains to be written. The passage from the estimate of this section to lattices is
taken up in §III.6; some of the items below are treated there, as that section states.
  (i) A proof of strong duality for d ≥ 4 (in the Lean: compactness and a separation theorem); see
      Remark (2) after Theorem J.


                                                  90
 (ii) Theorem J with the operator on the ordinary rows, and the deduction of the statement for all
      frames (ThmAGauss); the role of the congruence of Part II, §6.3.
(iii) Hypothesis (B) for the theta kernels of the construction: the height-two formal group at an
      inert prime, the series β, and the integrality (B4). This is the arithmetic input of the whole
      section.
 (iv) The passage from Theorem J to (III.79): items (iii) and (iv) above, with the loss terms.
 (v) A proof that the series β of the example after Theorem J satisfies (B) in all degrees, and an
     example for d ≥ 4 with an exceptional monomial; none is known to us. For d ≥ 4 Lemmas
     3–4 and Theorem J were tested only on a series without exceptional monomials and on the
     combinatorial steps.
 (vi) To follow, in the source, how the statement for one Taylor coefficient on the route of the final
      theorem, lemma_R_log_loss, is used in place of Proposition 5. (The statements TopRadius,
      top_radius_holds, LemmaR and ThmABound of the archive are not used by it.)


III.6      Divisibility: from derivatives to lattices
This section concerns the passage from the local estimate of §III.5 to the prime sum Pu,t,j of (4.1)
of Part II, the left side of the main inequality (M). In §III.5 one function at one prime is studied.
What (M) needs is a statement about lattices over a number field, at all primes at once, with
the losses added up. The section states that passage, proves its elementary parts, and names the
statements of the source that carry the arithmetic. For the prime sum it does this much: it splits
the sum and bounds several of its terms. The estimates for the eleven loss sums and their total
bound (III.138) are quoted. Theorem III.3.2 alone does not give that bound. The section does not
prove the divisibility side of (M). The divisibility of the period pairing is assumed, as statement
(Val) of §2. Also assumed are a statement on lattices, the inequality (III.79) at the primes of one
of the two classes, the estimates for the eleven loss sums with their total bound, and the slope
inequality for many maps.
    Notation. The notation of §III.5, §1 is kept: the scale x, ε, b, a, b′ , X ′ , X ′′ , p0 , δ1 ; the parameters
m, k, t0 , n, f, Nm ; the set T ; the numbers at , gain, τ , wPart, V (j, t, p), loG , flG , Sc, η, and the
constant C of (III.77). So a is the number xb, as in §III.4 and §III.5, with a ≤ at . And τ (w) is the
number in (III.79); it is not the modulus τ of §III.4. The letter u is the translate of Part II, §4.2,
as in Pu,t,j . The letters introduced here are listed at the end of §1.
    The letters are local. The letters of this section have the meanings given here in this section
only. Other sections of Part III use some of them differently, and some objects have another name
there. The main cases are these. τ is a weight place in §III.7. T is the level in §III.4 and §III.7,
and T (w) in §III.8 is the level prime of a word, which is Lvα here. The members α are the words
w of §III.8, where α is a constant. U is a power series in §III.5 and a space on the target side in
§III.7. γ is ℓ/(ℓ2 − 1) in §III.5 and in the paragraph on asmA_top of §4, and Euler’s constant in §6.
kaj is the number ς of §III.4, and cχd is the cd of §III.4 and §III.7. λw is written wt(y) in §III.7,
and the λw of §III.8 is another number. µ0,α is not the µ0 of §III.7 or of §III.9. Ξ is an operator in
§III.7 and §III.8. L0 is the field called K in §III.9, where L0 is another field.
    Proved outright. Three lemmas on lattices over the ring of integers of a number field: the
slope of a sublattice (Lemma L1), the slope of a lattice multiplied by a fractional ideal, with
the cost of rounding (Lemma L2), and the integrality of a pairing after such a multiplication
(Lemma L3). The bound for the slope when the rank drops (Lemma L5). The identity between the

                                                       91
prime sum (4.1) and sums over single primes (Lemma L6). Three elementary comparisons of losses
(Lemmas L7 to L9). An inequality of the shape of (III.79) at the primes of class signal, for the
explicit formulas of the source and with explicit loss terms (Proposition L10); it gives (III.79) itself
when the constants satisfy the conditions (III.130). The splitting of the prime sum by classes of
primes (Proposition L11) and three of the estimates that bound its terms (Lemmas L12 to L14;
the last uses Theorem III.3.2 and one cited estimate).
    Proved from two statements of the source. Theorem L4. Given the valuation statement (Val) of
§2, the glued lattice pairs integrally with the target. Given the lattice statement (Lat) of §2, its
slope exceeds that of the source lattice by the total gain, minus explicit losses.
   A model, not a statement about the source. Proposition L15 shows how the terms cancel when
there is one injective map between spaces of equal dimension. That hypothesis does not hold in the
source.
    Taken from the source without proof. The statement (Val), that is, the divisibility of the period
pairing of the chain of maps. In the Lean it rests on three assumptions (adm2, the hypotheses Tied
and CutUp of val2AllAtRF, and the field l1b; see §2). It is where Theorem J of §III.5 is used,
and it is conditional on hypothesis (B) there. The statement (Lat). The inequality (III.79) at the
primes of class inertClass. The estimates for the eleven sums over primes of the losses, and their
total bound (III.138). The slope inequality for many maps (Part II, §6.9–6.10). These are listed in
§7.


1.   Lattices over a number field: three lemmas

Places and weights. Let L0 be a number field of degree n0 , totally complex, with ring of integers
o. (In the source L0 is the coefficient field, LL.) A place w is a nonzero prime ideal pw of o; pw is its
residue characteristic, ew its ramification index over pw , vw the valuation with vw (pw ) = 1, and ow
the localisation of o at pw , a discrete valuation ring. For an ow -submodule Λw of an L0 -vector space
and an integer z (which may be negative) we write pzw Λw for (pw ow )z Λw . As in Part II, §6.4, put

               log Norm(pw )
        λw =                             λw ew = log p,      szw (σ) = 2λw ew σ      (σ ∈ R).     (III.99)
                                   X
                             ,
                    n0             w|p

The middle identity holds because the sum over w | p of ew times the residue degree of w is
n0 . So a gain of σ at every place above p, measured in units szw , has total 2σ log p. (Lean: lw,
SizeData2 .sz, FactEtaMean. The Lean states FactEtaMean for a field that is Galois over Q. The
identity holds for every number field, by the argument just given.)
   Lattices and slopes. Let U be an L0 -vector space of finite dimension dim U ≥ 1, and fix a
Haar measure on the real vector space U ⊗Q R. A lattice in U is a finitely generated o-submodule Λ
that spans U over L0 . Its rank is rk Λ = dim U ; it is a discrete subgroup of U ⊗ R of rank n0 dim U ,
with a covolume. The slope divides by the rank. So wherever a slope is taken, in Lemmas L1, L2
and L5 and in Theorem L4, the space has positive dimension and the lattice has rank ≥ 1. Put

                       2                                                      log |disc L0 |
         µ(Λ) = −           log covol(Λ) + cstL0 ,        cstL0 = − log 2 +                  .   (III.100)
                    n0 rk Λ                                                        n0
This is the slope in the normalisation for squared norms: twice the slope of Part II, §6.8, up to
the constant. (Lean: muDef, with the measure given by Hermitian forms at the complex places.
In Lemmas L1–L5 only differences µ(Λ) − µ(Λ′ ) for lattices in the same space occur; they do
not depend on the measure or on cstL0 .) For a finite o-module write lgw of it for the length of

its localisation at w: the number of factors o/pw in a composition series. For a lattice Λ write
Λw = ow Λ ⊂ U . Here Λ always denotes a lattice; the number ΛG of (III.78) is not used in this
section.
    Lemma L1 (a sublattice). Let Λ′ ⊂ Λ be lattices in U , with dim U ≥ 1. Then
                                             ′                             2 X

Proof. The quotient Λ/Λ′ is a finite o-module. It is the direct sum of its localisations, and each
composition factor o/pw has Norm(pw ) elements. This gives the index. Then covol(Λ′ ) = [Λ :
Λ′ ] covol(Λ) and log Norm(pw ) = n0 λw .
(Lean: muDef_index, mu_index_sum.)

    Lemma L3 (integrality after the multiplication). Let U, U ′ be L0 -vector spaces and
⟨ , ⟩ : U × U ′ → L0 a bilinear map. Let Λ ⊂ U and R ⊂ U ′ be lattices. For each place w let

that
                  ⟨y, y ′ ⟩ = 0 or vw ⟨y, y ′ ⟩ ≥ zw   for all y ∈ Λ, y ′ ∈ Genw .         (III.103)

            w . Then ⟨ỹ, ỹ ⟩ ∈ o for all ỹ ∈ IΛ and ỹ ∈ R. If moreover vw0 (⟨y, y ⟩) = zw0 for

β ′ y ′ with β ′ ∈ ow and y ′ ∈ Genw . So ⟨ỹ, ỹ ′ ⟩ is a sum of terms ββ ′ ⟨y, y ′ ⟩, and each term is zero

    Remarks. (1) Places are independent. By Lemma L2 (i) the localisation of IΛ at w depends
only on zw , and by (ii) the gains add over the places. This holds also for two places above the
same prime. It is the form over a number field of the gluing over Q of Part II, §6.8, where the
Chinese remainder theorem was used. (2) Maps compose. Suppose that there are m maps and m + 1
lattices, and that for 1 ≤ j ≤ m the jth map sends the localisation at w of the lattice before it into
 z
pww,j times the localisation of the lattice after it. Then the composite sends the first localisation
       z +···+zw,m
into pww,1          times the last. So the exponents of the m maps of the chain add. This is the
integrality assertion of Part II, §6.2. (3) A sublattice costs. If Λ′w ⊂ pzw Λw with an integer z ≥ 0,
then lgw (Λ/Λ′ ) ≥ z rk Λ, and by Lemma L1 the slope drops by at least 2λw z at w.
                                                                         √
    Checks. The three lemmas were tested in exact arithmetic over Q( −5), whose class number is
2, with the ramified prime above 2, the two primes above 3 and above 7, and the inert prime 11;
lattices of rank 1 to 3, not all free. In 60 cases the two sides of Lemma L2 (ii) agree to 2 · 10−15 ;
with λw zw in place of 2 λw zw they do not. In 25 cases the index formula of Lemma L1 holds
      P                      P

exactly, the lengths being computed place by place. In 12 cases the pairing of Lemma L3 is integral
on IΛ and is not on p−1 w0 IΛ.

2.    The glued lattice: statement and proof

The data. Fix x and one choice of the data of the construction (a “record” of the source). The
record contains one translate u ∈ H (Lean: the field Host.u0). It is the translate u of Part II, §4.2,
and the weights of the record are the weights for this u (§4 below). The maps of the record are
indexed by a finite set. Its elements α are the members (Lean: Word). A member is not a translate.
All members of a record have the same translate, and the prime sum Pu,t,j does not depend on the
member (the Lean statement Loss_at contains no member). A member enters through its lattices,
its level and its error terms. For each member α the source gives:
 • an L0 -vector space Uα of dimension ≥ 1 with a lattice Λsrc α , the source lattice (Lean: MramL;
   it is the preimage of the first lattice of the chain of maps; the positive dimension is the field
   a_pos of the inputs of divClausesR_of, §3);
 • a lattice Λdiv
              α ⊂ Λα (Lean: NuLat);
                   src

   ⟨ , ⟩α : Uα × U ′ → L0 , the period pairing of the whole chain (Lean: perCol; the bilinear form
   is col.B);
 • a prime Lvα , the level of the member (the level T of Part II, §6.7), and an integer rk∗α ≥ rk Λsrc
                                                                                                   α
   (Lean: duS).

     The statement (Val) in the Lean. It is not one Lean statement. It is assumed here; in the Lean
it is obtained from three assumptions.
 (1) LocalQuadraticParts.adm2. Let µw be the twist chosen at w (muAt). The letters of this
     item are those of the Lean: µw , with a place as index, is a twist and not a slope; the element
     F below has nothing to do with the number F0 ; and M    f, V0 have nothing to do with Mu,t,j
     and V . The statement is: for every member α, every place w, every y ∈ Λdiv    α , and every F
     that lies both in the lattice M
                                   f(µw ) (Mtilde) and in the subspace V0 (µw ) (V0) of the ambient


                                                                   95
      space of the twist, there is an element of L0 whose image under the fixed embedding of L0
      in C (embLL) is the period of y against F ; and this element is zero or has valuation at least
      ⌊ew sgα (w)⌋ at w. Both restrictions on F are part of the statement. It says nothing for an
      element of the lattice outside V0 (µw ), and nothing for an element of V0 (µw ) outside the lattice.
      That the period is the embedded value of an element of L0 is also part of the statement; (Val)
      presupposes it.
 (2) The hypotheses of the theorem val2AllAtRF. This theorem turns (1) into the same bound
     for the matrix form of the pairing, on a lattice Nu given in coordinates. Besides (1) it has
     three hypotheses. Two are carried by structures. ColBrF.Tied says that the matrix form is
     tied to the period: the twist at w is the one of the matrix form, each element of the lattice
     of the matrix form maps into V0 and is an integral combination of the generators of M          f,
     and the matrix pairing has the period as its embedded value. CutUp, for every twist that
     is used, says that an element of V0 that is an integral combination of the generators of M     f
     is an integral combination of elements of M ∩ V0 . This is what the two restrictions in (1)
                                                   f
     require. The third hypothesis is the compatibility with the source of (1): every element of Nu
     is mapped by the coordinate map eNu into NuLat, that is, into Λdiv    α . It is not an assumption.
     Where val2AllAtRF is applied (in the files RecBridge.lean and RecBridgeTied.lean) it is
     supplied by the theorem nu_into, from the definition (III.107) (NprimeDef) and two identities
     of lattices: shift, a field of the package, and lat, a field of the structure HLatTie, which the
     package carries for each member as its field hNu.
 (3) The structure field l1b (of type PairingFactorsThroughPeriods). It says that the local
     form at w equals a unit at w times ⟨y, y ′ ⟩α , where y ′ is the image of the element of the local
     lattice under a fixed linear map.
That the sets Genw generate Rw is not an assumption. The set Genw is the image of the local
lattice of (1). The lattice R is defined as the lattice glued from these images (RR, by glueLat),
and the theorem latticeGlue proves that this is a lattice with these localisations (Lemma L2 (i)
is the case of an ideal). The passage to one bilinear pairing is faithful: the Lean theorem intoDual
applies DivIntoDualShift (Lemma L3) with one L0 -bilinear form. The quantifiers of (Val) are
those of the Lean: all members, all places, all y ∈ Λdiv
                                                       α . The final theorem depends on adm2 (the
dependency computation confirms this). It also depends on val2AllAtRF and on latticeGlue: in
the seventh run of the dependency computation both are marked as used. Whether the hypotheses
Tied and CutUp of (2) and the field of (3) are discharged for the construction on the route of the
final theorem was not determined. The dependency computation does not say how hypotheses are
discharged, and the proof terms were not followed that far.
     The statement (Lat) in the Lean. It is the part of the first clause of LinkG_at that is used
here, read with NuDef. That clause is for the places w with pw ̸= Lvα and has three parts. The
first is the inclusion, with the exponent ⌊ew τ (w)⌋. The second is lenMN(α, w) ≤ rk∗α (τ (w) + io(w)).
The third says: if the set VV of the Lean at the prime pw is empty, then τ (w) = 0, io(w) = 0 and
lenMN(α, w) = 0. The third part is not used in Theorem L4. The number lenMN is not the local
length. The last clause of NuDef says that lgw (Λsrc
                                                   α /Λα ) = lenMN(α, w) · ew . With the second part
                                                        div

this gives (III.109), and it is where the factor ew there comes from. For the integrality of τ (w) two
statements are needed. The theorem exists_tau_eq_natCast says that the number given by the
formula tauP is a natural number. It does not mention the τ of the record. That τ (w) equals tauP
at pw is part of the hypothesis fills of §3.

    Checks. Theorem L4 was tested in exact arithmetic over Q( −5) on 200 lattices of rank 2,
with rk∗ from 2 to 5, with the level prime 2, 3, 7 or 11, with a sublattice that is smaller at a place
above the level, and with every hypothesis of (Lat) verified on the data. The identity (III.110)
holds exactly in all cases, and (III.111) holds in all cases. In 60 cases the data were chosen with
equality in (III.109) and with ew sgα (w) integral; then (III.111) is an equality, exactly, in all 60. On
these 60 cases three weaker forms of Lo(α) were tried. With w szw (io(w)) in place of the first two
                                                                P

terms (the loss one would get from (III.109) with rk in place of rk∗ at all places) the inequality
fails in 48. Without the term at the level it fails in 15, which are all the cases where that term is
positive. Without the factor for the rank it fails in 46, which are all the cases where rk∗ > rk and
τ + io is not zero. On the other 140 cases, without the rounding term it fails in 15. Lemma L5 was
also tested on 20000 random data in the form of the Lean statement ClaimLow; without the factor
for the rank the inequality fails in a third of the cases.

3.   What LinkG is, and where the valuation statement lives

The statement LinkG of the source has five clauses (LinkG_at). They are: (1) the lattice clause, of
which (Lat) is the part used in §2; (2) a bound for the auxiliary numbers Sv; (3) the inequality
(III.79) of §III.5, with 0 ≤ gain(j, w) ≤ 4|T |a/pw , at the places of class signal or inertClass
with pw ≤ X ′′ ; (4) a clause that holds by definition; (5) a bound for the mean of certain numbers
κ(Q), used in §5. None of them mentions a theta function, a Taylor coefficient, or a pairing. As
§III.5 already said, (III.79) is an inequality between the numbers gain, τ, wPart. A hypothesis on
the family (the field fills, of type ParametersFill) says that these numbers are given by explicit
formulas (gainP, tauP, fill, partner, TTP); they are recalled in §4. With that hypothesis, clause
(3) is arithmetic about the formulas. Proposition L10 below carries this out at the primes of class
signal, with its own loss terms.
    The zero chain. The proof of LinkG in the source (linkG_without_kernels) goes through an
intermediate statement admG1, one part of which does speak of the valuation of a chain of maps on
a local lattice (vKge). On the route to the final theorem that part is applied to the chain in which
every map is zero (zeroCh). The valuation of 0 is infinite, so the part holds for a trivial reason
(vKge_zero). So LinkG is proved without any information on the theta kernels. This is consistent:
LinkG is the bookkeeping, and the lattice clause (1).
    Where the divisibility is asserted. It is the statement (Val). The final theorem depends on
LocalQuadraticParts.adm2. The route, read downward from the place where it is consumed, is
this.
 (a) divClausesR_of takes a structure of inputs, DivInputsR, and produces four clauses. One
     field of DivInputsR is val2, that is (III.108). The other fields are independent of it.
     From val2 comes the first clause only: the integrality of Theorem L4 (i), by intoDual
     and divIntoDualShift (Lemma L3), which use the field l1b. The second clause bounds
     the squares of the operator norms of the pairing at the complex places by numbers whose
     mean logarithm is the height of the record minus a constant. It comes from the height inputs
     (the fields ch, hn, nP, nKcb, eig6). The third clause is the inequality of sizes, first line of

                                                   99
     (6.5) of Part II, with its term −F0 . It comes from the size inputs (among them L3main, Qb,
     deQ, errB, and the inequality constant_ge_twenty_eight). The fourth clause is the slope
     identity of Lemma L2 (ii), by shiftMu; it uses that Nu is a lattice of positive rank (Nu_lat,
     a_pos). So the valuation statement does not imply the height bound or the inequality of sizes.
     The inequality of sizes is the subject of §III.4.
 (b) val2AllAtRF deduces val2 from adm2, by linearity, under the hypotheses described in §2. In
     the seventh run of the dependency computation val2AllAtRF is marked as used by the final
     theorem. The archive has a second name, DivisionPackage.val2. The query for it returned
     an empty list. This is inconclusive: it does not show which fully qualified name was intended,
     and it does not show that such a declaration is unused.
 (c) admissible_level_two proves adm2, place by place. At the places above Lvα and above
     the two auxiliary level primes g0 , g1 of Part II, §6.7 it uses separate inputs. At all other
     places it uses the statement Conclusion1 of the file WeilJacquetShalikaLaw.lean: for
     every y in the local lattice at w that defines Λdiv    α , the image of y under the composite of
     the m maps of the chain is divisible, in the last lattice            of the chain, by pw to the power
              (gain(j,    +           err(α,        +           (D   )    Here Dα is an integer denominator
                                                                      
                                    −                    ·
         P
     ew     j          w)   ϕ(j, w)          j, w))   sp   v pw    α    .
     of the member, vpw is the pw -adic valuation, and sp is a real parameter of the Lean statement.
     On the route sp is 1: the statement Serv contains Conclusion1 with this value. The term
     with Dα does not reach (III.108). The theorem period_geTU, which passes from the chain to
     the period, lowers the exponent by exactly vpw (Dα ) (vDG), and the two cancel. This is the
     statement about kernels.
 (d) Conclusion1 is part of ServF, which sum_over_primes_service proves from the structure
     SumOverPrimesHypotheses, which has the fields twU and pdv, a bound for the denominators
     Dα (DGamSize), and the theorem split_bound_eleven_one. The latter is the local estimate:
     it rests on thmAOp, the Lean form of Theorem J of §III.5. The hypothesis pdv contains
     hypothesis (B) of §III.5 for the kernels of the construction.
So there are two branches. One (LinkG) says what the numbers gain and τ are and that they
dominate the sums V ; it needs no kernel. The other (ServF, then (Val)) says that the kernels are
divisible by these numbers; it needs Theorem J and hypothesis (B), and it is assumed here. They
meet in Theorem L4 and in §5.


4.   From Theorem J to a gain at a place

Cells, and sums over one prime. Let H be the group of Part II, §4.1, of order n. It is the
group of §III.3, and it is written additively here, as there and as in the Lean; T ⊂ H. A cell is a
pair (0, t) or (1, t) with t ∈ H; the first entry is the row. There are f = 2n cells. For an odd prime
p let hp ∈ H be its class, of order np . The classes of primes are those of the Lean (primeClass): a
prime p ∤ 2qd is splitK if χd (p) = 1; if χd (p) = −1 it is signal when χq (p) = 1 and inertClass
when χq (p) = −1. For an odd prime p ̸= q, t ∈ H and an integer i ≥ 1 put
                                                    1 + χ (p)i            

                                                         2
This is the cell of the prime power pi in the table of Part II, §4.1, for the column t. For odd i the
row is 1 if p is signal and 0 if p is inertClass. (Lean, for odd i: partner.) For each j there are
weights wtj > 0 on the cells (Lean: fill); these are the weights of Part II, §4.2, for the translate u

contains every i with pi ≤ at . In the source the weights that occur are ≥ 1 (§III.5, §1). Without
this hypothesis the two sums can differ; see the example below.

In the running example of Part II, §4.2 (q = 3, d = 23, n = 4, at = 10000) the weights are 0.05
and 10. Computing the right side of (III.121) gives 95461.6156 for the translate [1], the value of
Part II, and the four values for the four translates agree with (4.1) to 10−10 . The example uses
V∞ , not V : its weights 0.05 are below 1, and with V in place of V∞ the right side of (III.121) is
smaller by 0.309064 for the translate [1] (the powers 57 and 115 are lost). With the weights 10 and
1 the difference is 0. The primes of class inertClass contribute 51234.4133 for every translate:
their odd powers lie in row 0, which contains no distinguished cell. The primes of class signal
contribute 42833.7633 for the translate [1]. The distinguished weights carry the information on the
zeros. Among the unramified primes, only signal primes sample a distinguished weight; this is the
reason for the name. The powers of d contribute the separate last term of Lemma L6, and that
term does depend on the translate. Here 232 ≡ 1 modulo 3 and modulo 16, so the cell of 232 for
the column t is (1, t), in row 1, and 10000/232 > 10. The last term is 1393.4390 for the translate
[1] and 1424.6372 for the translates [3], [5], [7]. The difference is (10 − 0.05) log 23.

    Comparison with Theorem J. Theorem J of §III.5 is applied to one orbit: the sites are the
sites of the orbit just defined, the top sites are the sites whose column lies in T (Q) (for a signal
prime: those of even parity among them), the number at −8 Sc plays the role of the length of the first
row at the top site t, and wtj −kaj +4 Sc that of the capacity at a site. The term top of (III.82) then
has the shape of the double sum in (III.122), and the last term of (III.82) has the shape of the first
term of (III.123) (Lemma L8). This dictionary, the treatment of the excluded exponents, the other
terms of (III.82), and the passage from one function to the lattices of the chain are in the source
(normCor7, split_bound_eleven_one, orbit_entry_bounds_hold, lift_bounds_of_essential,
then WeilJacquetShalikaLaw.Proof.stmt_proved); they are assumed here.
    The statement asmA_top. The final theorem uses this statement for the top row (it is marked as

of §III.5. In this paragraph s is a site, ℓ is the prime, and ν1 , cap, top, dtop are as in §III.5, §1; i is

 (a) If dtop (s) is finite, take the set of all odd i < dtop (s). Then (III.125) is (III.93) of that lemma,
     with A = ν1 (s), ci = cap(s + i) and with dtop (s) as the odd bound J of the lemma: the
     same statement. Here top(s) is the whole of the second line of (III.82): the sum over odd

    The statement lemma_R_log_loss. The final theorem also uses this statement (seventh run).
It is the bound for one Taylor coefficient on the route of the final theorem. Its comparison with
Proposition 5 is in §III.5, in the paragraph on the declarations the final theorem depends on:
Proposition 5 is the stronger of the two bounds for a coefficient. The formal statement (LemmaRA,
in the file GaussJetBounds/StatementG.lean) is for a multi-index whose entries are all at most
an integer a ≥ 1, and it assumes a test on the pure monomials below the multi-index. Its exponent
has the explicit loss |ι| (logℓ a + 1), where ι is the finite index set of the variables and ℓ is the prime.
(In this sentence a is the bound of the Lean statement.)
   We now prove what is elementary: three comparisons of losses, and then an inequality of the
shape of (III.79) at the signal primes.
   Lemma L7 (shifted positive parts). Let p ≥ 2, A ≥ 0, c ≥ 0, and for each odd i ≥ 1 let
ψi ≥ 1 and ci ≥ 0 be real numbers. Let oddp (A) be the number of odd i ≥ 1 with pi ≤ A. Then
           X  A − 2c                               X A                               2
                         − ψi + ci − c           ≥                 − ψi           −c 1+      oddp (A).   (III.126)
          i odd
                    pi                       +

The left side may be +∞. If A ≥ 1, then oddp (A) ≤ 21 (logp A + 1).
    Proof. For each i, A−2c
                          pi
                             − ψi + ci − c ≥ pAi − ψi − c(1 + p2i ). The positive part is increasing,
and subtracting a number ≥ 0 lowers a positive part by at most that number. If pi > A then
A/pi − ψi < 0, so the term on the right is 0 and nothing is lost. The odd i with pi ≤ A are the odd
i with 1 ≤ i ≤ logp A; for A ≥ 1 their number is at most 12 (logp A + 1).
The bound for oddp (A) needs A ≥ 1: for 0 < A < 1/p the count is 0 and 12 (logp A + 1) is negative.
In the use below A = at ≥ 1. The left side of (III.126) is finite if ci ≤ c + ψi for all large i; this
holds in the use below.
    Lemma L8 (three digit losses, one bound). Let p be a prime and ā ≥ p an integer. Then
digp (ā) ≤ logp ā + 1 ≤ 2 logp ā, and:

The paper has three digit losses: one digit count per variable (§III.5); one per factorial, with a
square for the top sites (Part II, (6.1a); Lean: StarArith, and the loss in gainA); and the closed


                                                         103
form (III.123) per orbit. They are three different quantities. The lemma shows only that (III.123)
is an upper bound for the first two. For example, with p = 3, ā = 80, two sites, N0 = N1 = 2 and
r0 = r1 = 1, the largest value of the first loss is 32 and that of the second, when both sites are in
the subset, is 48.

Proof. The orbits partition H. For inertClass the orbit has np points and n(Q) = np , so the sum

their length, so the sum is 2n = f .

(which holds when a ≥ 2), since |T | ≤ n = f /2. This is why flG is subtracted once for each of the
m maps in (III.79), and not once for each t.

The proposition does not show that the constants of the source satisfy (III.130). In the Lean the
comparison of the losses is lo_le_loG and fl_le_flG. They say: for every real number C∗ , the


                                                     104
Lean’s own losses lo and fl with the constant C∗ are at most loG and flG with the constant
4|C∗ | + 1. This is the source’s version of the conditions (III.130). (The sums in (III.122) are finite
because the capacities are ≥ 0. Proposition L10 was tested on 3000 random toy data, with H
cyclic of order 8, 16, 32, and again on 30170 exact cases: no violation, and the lower bound is
attained; without the loss terms the inequality fails in nearly all cases.) For the primes of class
inertClass the same computation applies to the orbits that are not served, but there Ex(p, t) is
not empty and the labels on these orbits do not belong to Tw . On the served orbits the source
uses gainsrv − Pr and pays τ (w); these orbits satisfy the conditions (G1)–(G3) of §III.3. That case
is assumed (AuxBlock8.Pf.stmtF, linkG_at_core). The terms pr(p) and or(p) of (III.78) occur
only there.

Proof. By (III.131) only the places above the primes of Ξ contribute to Gtop , and by (III.99),

The hypothesis (III.131) is needed: Gtop is a sum over all places, and Ξ contains only primes of
the two classes. In the source it holds at the places above the split primes, above 2, q, d, g0 , g1
and above the levels. The Lean field not_inert gives the vanishing at every place whose prime is
of neither of the two classes. That the level primes, among them g0 and g1 , are of class splitK
is another field (level_split). At g0 , g1 the gain vanishes also by the field gain_g. Without it
(III.132) fails: a gain of 1 at one place above a split prime changes the left side and not the right.
(Lean: SplitEq, SplitClasses. Tested on 15000 random data with (III.131): no failure. Without
it (III.132) fails in all 15000. If the labels outside Tp are forgotten, (III.133) fails.) So the prime
sum exceeds Gtop by four kinds of terms: the losses loG , flG at the primes where (III.79) is used;
the labels not served at those primes; the whole of V at the other primes; and the prime d.
   Lemma L12 (one prime). For p ≥ 2, V (j, t, p) ≤ at p/(p2 − 1) ≤ 34 at /p. Proof.                                            −i =

p/(p2 − 1), and p2 /(p2 − 1) ≤ 43 .

                                                           105
    The classes of primes in the sum. The source sorts the primes of class signal or inertClass
as follows (clAt, NoGain). Say that a prime p is in a band if p ≥ p0 and some power pi lies within
a factor 1 ± bw of X ′ , for a small number bw > 0 of the source. Below, Cr is a further constant of
the source (Lean: Cr; it is not the constant C of (III.77)). A candidate is an inertClass prime
p < X ′ whose class hp has order np ≥ xε/4 and which is not in a band. For a candidate put
Sig(p) = m κ(Q), the sum over the good orbits Q, those on which T satisfies (G1)–(G3) of §III.3
            P

with the parameters there; κ(Q) ≥ 0 is a number attached to the orbit (kapv). The set of served
orbits Srv(p) of a candidate is the set of good orbits if Sig(p) ≤ x−ε , and is empty otherwise; for all
other primes Srv(p) = ∅. The set of served labels is

   Tp = T if p is signal or p ≥ X ′ ,         Tp = {t ∈ T : the orbit of t is in Srv(p)} otherwise.
                                                                                               (III.134)
There are four classes. A prime belongs to the first class in the following list whose condition it
satisfies; read in this order the list is a partition. (As conditions, “high” and “first” overlap.)
  1. high: p > X ′′ . The gain is 0. All weights are at least a/X ′′ (Lean: the field off_thin of
     the structure Inst, with a/X ′′ = w′′ ; the other cells have larger weights, by off_top and
     off_thick). Since moreover at ≤ a(1 + 1/(kx13 )), V vanishes unless p ≤ X ′′ (1 + 1/(kx13 )): a
     short interval.
  2. second: candidates with Sig(p) > x−ε . The gain is set to 0.
  3. first: inertClass, p < X ′ (1 + Cr bw), with np < xε/4 or p in a band; and inertClass with
     np odd and np > 1. The gain is set to 0.
  4. served: all others. There (III.79) holds with Tp as in (III.134).
The class “served” contains the inertClass primes with hp = 0 and p ≥ X ′ (1 + Cr bw). Their gain
is 0 by (III.124), and V vanishes there, because at /p is below the weights of the cells (0, t), t ∈ T .
These weights exceed b′ = a/X ′ (Lean: the field bT_ge of SizeData2 for the label, and off_top of
Inst for the rest of the weight, which is at least b′ /x). The primes of the classes high, second and
first are the “dropped” primes of §2. The other primes need no gain: split primes have coefficient 0
in (4.1), and so do 2 and q. Every odd prime up to xε/(5Co ) splits, where Co is the constant of the
source that appears after Theorem III.3.2 (Lean: split_small, an assumed property of d, with
three_le_split; the Lean writes the exponent as ez/5, and ez is ε divided by a constant J of the
scale, so Co is J). The paragraph after Theorem III.3.2 writes Co ≥ 2. The Lean structure of the
scale has only 1 ≤ J (one_le_J). Whether J ≥ 2 on the route was not determined; the statement
that these primes split does not depend on it. So the sum starts above that bound. At the split
primes, at g0 , g1 and at the levels the numbers gain, τ , io are 0, and only ϕ and err occur. The
cut-offs have this meaning: X ′ = a/b′ is where a/p falls below the weights of the cells (0, t), t ∈ T ;
X ′′ is where it falls below the smallest weights; below p0 the source uses a different local argument.
    Lemma L13 (a label that is not served lies on a bad orbit). Let p be a served prime
and t ∈ T ∖ Tp . Then p is of class inertClass, p < X ′ , np ≥ xε/4 , and the orbit of t under hp is
not good.
    Proof. By (III.134), p is inertClass and p < X ′ . Since p is neither first nor second, np ≥ xε/4 ,
p is not in a band, and Sig(p) ≤ x−ε . So p is a candidate and Srv(p) is the set of good orbits.
(Lean: unserved_bad, which the final theorem uses by the seventh run of the dependency compu-
tation.) This is where the conditions (G1)–(G3) are used in the sum over primes. Since served
orbits are good by definition, the conditions are also available in the proof of (III.79) on the served
orbits (§4); that proof was not read.

6.   A model computation: how the terms would cancel

This subsection does not prove the prime side of (M), for the source or for one member. It proves
an inequality under a hypothesis, called (a) below, that does not hold in the source. Its purpose is
to show which term of the earlier subsections meets which term of Part II, §6.7.
    Proposition L15 (a model; hypothesis (a) does not hold in the source). Fix a member

 (a) (model) the pairing gives an injective map Perα : Uα → HomL0 (U ′ , L0 ), and dim Uα = dim U ′ ;
     the measures on Uα ⊗ R and on HomL0 (U ′ , L0 ) ⊗ R come from Euclidean norms that are
     orthogonal sums over the n0 /2 complex places of L0 ; and htα is the mean over these places

     does not hold in the source. There the rank of Λsrc   α is smaller than the rank of R, there
     are many members, and no inequality for one member is available: the source has only an
     inequality for a mean over the members of the record (Lean: Frame_at), and that inequality
     has further error terms (the logarithm of the number of members, a term for the spread of the
     slopes, two error terms from second moments multiplied by a range of slopes, and a factor
     close to 1);

lets the size of the map bound the slope of Λgl α ; (III.111) turns that into a bound for Gtop ; (III.138)
turns Gtop into the prime sum. (The arithmetic was tested on 25000 random data satisfying the
hypotheses: no violation, equality when all hypotheses are equalities, and failure with a wrong sign
in three places.)


7.   Assumed here, from the source

  (i) The valuation statement (Val).           In the Lean it rests on three assumptions:

      the route of the final theorem. That the local images generate Rw is a definition (RR) with

      by admissible_level_two from: the statement Conclusion1 on the composite of the chain

   What this section proves, and what it assumes. Proved outright: Lemmas L1, L2, L3 and
L5 (lattices); Lemma L6 (the prime sum as a sum over single primes); Lemmas L7, L8, L9 (losses);
Proposition L10 (an inequality of the shape of (III.79) at the signal primes, for the formulas of
the source, with explicit loss terms; it is (III.79) under (III.130)); Proposition L11, Lemmas L12


                                                  110
and L13, and Lemma L14 given Theorem III.3.2 and one cited estimate. For the prime sum this
amounts to the following: the section splits the sum and bounds several of its terms. The estimates
for the eleven loss sums and their total bound (III.138) are quoted; Theorem III.3.2 alone does
not give that bound. Proved from statements of the source: Theorem L4: part (i) from (Val), part
(iii) from (Lat); part (ii) needs neither. A model: Proposition L15, whose hypothesis (a) does not
hold in the source. Assumed: the items of §7. The section does not prove that any theta kernel is
divisible by anything: that is (Val), which rests on Theorem J and on hypothesis (B). It does not
prove the prime side of (M).
    What remains.
  (i) The passage from Theorem J to Conclusion1: frames and labels, the dictionary of §4, the
      chain of m maps, and the local lattice that defines Λdiv
                                                           α . Then the passage from adm2 to (Val):
      the hypotheses Tied and CutUp of val2AllAtRF and the field l1b, that is, the comparison of
      the local pairings at different twists.
 (ii) The inequality (III.79) on the served orbits of the inertClass primes, which are good orbits
      in the sense of (G1)–(G3), with the terms pr(p), or(p) and τ ; the bound for the mean of κ(Q);
      and the comparison of constants (III.130) at the signal primes.
(iii) The lattice statement (Lat), and the four statements of §2 (a)–(d) that lead from (III.111) to
      (III.117).
 (iv) The eleven sums over primes behind (III.138), and their total bound.
 (v) The mean-slope argument for all members of a record, which the source has in the place of
     hypothesis (a) of Proposition L15, and the mean over the translates in (M).


III.7      The size bound: the arithmetic inputs
This section concerns the passage from the analytic estimates of §III.4 to the inequality (III.75) at
the end of §III.4, which is the first line of (6.5) of Part II. In the source this passage is one theorem
(divClausesR_of) with a long list of hypotheses.
    Proved here. First: a finite list of numerical inputs, named (S1)–(S8), (W1), (W2) and (G)
below, implies the inequality, with explicit constants (Theorem III.7.1). The proof includes a
lemma on the norm of a map that factors through a quotient, the averaging of the bounds over the
archimedean places, and the cancellation of the period. Second: a formula for a mean of logarithms
of periods, defined by the integral lines of the field E (the Proposition of subsection 4). It is deduced
from the class mean formula for the elliptic curve (inputs (I2), (I3) of §III.4), from four statements
about ideals, three of which are proved and one cited, and from one fact about the field E. Third:
(W1) and (W2) follow from the conclusions of the paragraph “Row by row” of §III.4 and three
facts taken from the source (the remark after the list of inputs).
   Assumed here, from the source. All the inputs (S1)–(S8), (W1), (W2), (G). For each we give
the statement and say what kind of theorem it is. In particular the period input (S4) is assumed.
The Proposition of subsection 4 does not prove it. (S4) follows from that Proposition only together
with three further statements, (P1)–(P3), listed at the end of subsection 4. (P1) and (P3) are taken
from the source. (P2) is proved in subsection 4. Nothing in this section proves that the lattices and
maps of the construction exist or have the stated properties.

     Letters that need a remark.
 • K is the number field over which the matrices of the maps are defined, as in the paragraph
   “Sums over√classes” of §III.4. In the paragraph “The reference mean square” §III.4 writes K
   also for Q( −d); here that field is kd .
 • Kj (1 ≤ j ≤ m) are the maps of the chain. Steps are numbered from 1, as in “Row by row”
   and in the source; “Sums over classes” numbers them from 0.
 • T is the set of distinguished places and T is the level, as in (III.75). The other uses of T in
   §III.4 (for |θ(0)| and for the theta lift) do not occur here. In §III.5 the letter T is an index set.
 • τ always denotes a weight place, that is, an element of P; t denotes an element of T ; and ρ
   denotes an embedding of the field M into C, as in “The reference mean square”. The letter ρ
   occurs only in subsection 4 and in quotations from §III.4. §III.4 writes τ for the weight place in
   “Several blocks” and ρ in “The eigenvalues of a lift”, “Row by row” and “The pairing at the end
   of the chain”; what is A±ρ , Jρ , dρ there is Aτ , Jτ , dτ here.
                                                  ±

 • h is the function h(u) = u log u − u, and, in the last display of Theorem III.7.1 only, the term h
   of (III.75); §III.4 does the same. Heights of maps are written ht. The numbers hB,j are not the
   hB of Part II, §6.7.
 • Nm is the rank of the last space of the chain; in the source Nm = k + m(t0 + 1). The letters N ,
   N ′ are left to §III.4, where they are the ranks of a pair. t0 is the parameter called t in §III.4. ϱ
   and L are those of “The eigenvalues of a lift” (Lean: nV, Lp).
 • η is the bound for the additional rows, as in input (e) of “Row by row”; it is not Dedekind’s
   function. π is the number. κv is not the ratio κ of §III.4. Θ1 (v) is not the Θ0 of §III.4; see (S1)
   and subsection 5.
 • The labels (W1), (W2) are not the rules (W1)–(W4) of §III.5, and (G) is a label, not a letter.
   Parts (a), (b) of Theorem III.7.1 are not the inputs (a)–(e) of “Row by row”.
 • §III.5 uses x, a, ε, λ, p, d, β and N with meanings of its own, as it says. Here they follow §III.4.
 • Some letters have several roles inside this section. The index tells them apart.
     – λ: λ0 is the bound for la in (S7). λ1,τ is the first weight at τ in (S2), the λ1 of §III.4; it is
       not related to λ0 . λj,p (v) are the eigenvalue factors of (S3). λ without index is the scalar of
       the source’s evaluation, in subsection 5 only.

    – p: pj is a number. p without index is the index of the subspaces Ūp , in (S3) and in Step 1
      only.
    – l: ℓτ is the exponent of the level in (S2). l3,j and l6,j are the real numbers of (S5), (S6) and
      of (S3). lτ is the integral line of subsection 4.
    – H: H(·) is the hook product of a diagram, and log Hjτ is a number of the record. Hmax
      and H∗ are bounds for slopes in (S7); they are not hook products. Hρ occurs once, in a
      quotation from §III.4 in subsection 5.
    – E: E is the field. E3 , Eb , EK , EX , Eloc and E1 , . . . , E8 are error terms. E0 , in subsection 5
      only, is the Euler product of §III.4.
    – d: d is the prime. d0,τ , d1,τ are the degrees in (S2). dτ , in subsection 5 only, is the dρ of

    – w: wjτ is the weight shift, written w in remark (1) for fixed j, τ . w′′ is a parameter. wE , in

      primes.

      rank. r = k + m − 1; rν is the number of kernel degrees. B0 is the scale above; B occurs in
      remark (1) only. Σj (v) is a number, not a sum sign.
    Letters and the other sections. The letters introduced here are local to this section. The sections
of Part III do not share one notation, and we did not rename across sections. Each section declares
its own letters. The clashes most likely to confuse are these. C0 and CH are other constants in
§III.8. R is another object in §III.6 and in §III.9. M, H, I, m, κ and Θ1 have other meanings
in §III.8. U is a unitary matrix in §III.4 and another space in §III.6. Y has other meanings
in §III.4–III.6, III.8 and III.9. Φ is a test function in §III.8. ι is an index set in §III.5 and an
isomorphism in §III.9. Π and d have other meanings in §III.9. C2 is another constant in §III.6,
and µ0 another number in §III.9. No statement of this section uses a letter in the sense of another
section, except the letters listed above as kept from §III.4 and §III.5.
    Scales. x > 1 and ε > 0 are real numbers; Lsc = xε , b = eLsc , a = x b, La = log(a rdE ),
B0 = f m b xε , and Nc ≥ 0. (In the application f = 2n and Nc = |T2 | k b x2ε /n2 ; the proofs below
use only Nc ≥ 0.) As in §III.4, h(u) = u log u − u for u > 0 and h(0) = 0. So at log at − at means 0
when at = 0 (the convention 0 · log 0 = 0).
    Index sets. J = {1, . . . , m} indexes the lifts. P is the set of weight places; it has at most f
elements. T ⊆ P is the set of distinguished places, with a real number at ≥ 0 for each t ∈ T . When
an element of T is called τ this number is written aτ . For a statement S the symbol [S] is 1 if S
holds and 0 otherwise.
    The field and its places. K is the number field of the first remark above. In the source it is
totally complex. v runs over its archimedean places, s is their number, and ⟨u⟩ = s−1 v u(v) for a
                                                                                     P

real function u of v.
    The chain and its end. A Hermitian norm at v on a K-vector space W means a positive definite
Hermitian norm on the complex space W ⊗K,v C. V0 , . . . , Vm are finite-dimensional K-vector
spaces with a Hermitian norm at each v, and Kj : Vj−1 → Vj (j ∈ J ) are K-linear maps. U is a
finite-dimensional K-vector space with a positive definite Hermitian norm at each v, and U0 ⊆ U
is a K-subspace. Ū = U/U0 , and ū ∈ Ū is the class of u ∈ U . Ū carries at each v the quotient
norm, ∥x∥ = min{∥u∥ : ū = x}, where x is in Ū ⊗K,v C and u runs over U ⊗K,v C (the minimum
is attained there). Y is a K-vector space with a Hermitian norm at each v, and ι : Ū → Y is a
K-linear bijection. Π : Vm → U ∗ is a K-linear map into the dual space (the pairing at the end

                                                   113
of the chain). Dual spaces carry the dual norms. The map of the inequality is the K-linear map
Φ : V0 → Y ∗ determined by

                         Φ(φ) ι ū = Π Km · · · K1 φ (u)                    (φ ∈ V0 , u ∈ U ).             (III.141)
                                                                  


It exists as soon as the right side vanishes for u ∈ U0 ; this is part of input (S3). If Φ ̸= 0, its height
is ht(Φ) = ⟨log ∥Φ∥2v ⟩; §III.4 writes h(P ) for the height of a map P . In the source the chain is a
chain of matrix steps, and one step may contain several lifts (Lean: a map lf from the lifts to the
steps); the bound (S1) is then a bound for the matrix of a step. We state everything for one lift in
each step. The argument is the same.
    Lattices and slopes. A lattice L is a finitely generated OK -module that spans a K-vector space
with norms at every v. µ(L) denotes the normalised slope of the source (muDef): twice the slope of
L for these norms, plus a constant depending only on K. It is the normalisation for squared norms.
L∨ is the dual lattice in the dual space, with the dual norms, and µmax (L) is the largest normalised
slope of a sublattice. R ⊂ Y is the lattice whose dual receives the values of Φ, and µCC = µ(R∨ ).
There is a second lattice R0 , in a space Y0 of the same kind, built from the same data at a lower
level; µ0 = µ(R∨0 ).

                                                  120
4.    The mean of the periods of the integral lines

This subsection proves a formula for a mean of periods (the Proposition below). It does not prove
(S4); what is missing is listed at the end. In this subsection ρ denotes an embedding of a field M
into C, as in the paragraph “The reference mean square” of §III.4, and τ , τ ′ denote weight places.
                  √
    Let kd = Q( −d), embedded in C. Its discriminant is −d, because d is squarefree and
d ≡ 3 mod 4 (the d of the paper is a prime with d ≡ 7 mod 8); Lemma A uses this. Let E ⊃ kd
be the field of the construction, of degree [E : kd ] = f . The weight places are the f embeddings
τ : E → C that extend the given embedding of kd . So here P has exactly f elements; subsection 3
used only |P| ≤ f . Let D be the different of E over kd , and let the bar be complex conjugation on
E.
    A fact about the field E. There is√
                                      an integral ideal b of E with D = b b̄. This holds for the field
                             −1 √
of the paper, E = Q(ζN + ζN , −q, −d), where N = 4n, n is a power of 2, and q is a prime with
q ≡ 3 mod 4 that divides d + 1. The proof has two steps.
   First step: every prime of E that divides D lies over 2 or over q. (This is (P2) below.) Put
              −1 √                                                          √
F = Q(ζN + ζN    , −q). Then E = kd F . Since q ≡ 3 mod 4, the number −q lies in Q(ζq ) (the
quadratic Gauss sum). So F ⊆ Q(ζN q ), and F is unramified over Q at every prime number that
does not divide 2q. Let Q be a prime of E that does not lie over 2 or q. An extension that is
unramified at a prime stays unramified when the ground field is enlarged; so E = kd F is unramified
over kd at Q. A prime that is unramified does not divide the different. (For n = 1 the field F is
  √
Q( −q), and only primes over q can divide D.) The Lean has this step as diffSupport.
     Second step. 2 and q split in kd , because d ≡ 7 mod 8 and −d ≡ 1 mod q. Complex conjugation
exchanges the two primes of kd over 2, and the two over q. So a prime Q of E that divides D and
its conjugate Q̄ lie over different primes of kd , and Q̄ =
                                                          ̸ Q. Conjugation maps E to E and kd to kd ;
so D̄ = D, and Q, Q̄ occur in D with the same exponent. Take for b the product of one prime
power from each pair. (Lean: principal in KcDiff.lean, from diffSupport and kc_below_ne;
it is used through Arith.bb_ex to fill the field b_spec of the record Host.)
    This b is coprime to b̄, as is the ideal of the source. There are other integral choices when an
exponent e is larger than 1: one may take Qi Q̄e−i with 0 ≤ i ≤ e from a pair. The Proposition
below holds for every b with D = b b̄; its proof uses nothing else. The coprimality is not used in
this section.
    The splitting of 2 and q in kd is sufficient for the existence of b. It is not necessary. For a
field E with complex multiplication over an imaginary quadratic field, an integral b with D = b b̄
exists if and only if every prime of D that is equal to its conjugate has an even exponent. (If
Q̄ = Q, the exponent of Q in b b̄ is twice its exponent in b. Conversely, halve the exponents of
these primes√ and√treat the other
                                √ primes in pairs as above.) So the statement fails for some fields:
for E = Q( −7, −3) over Q( −7) the different is the prime over 3, with exponent 1, and it is
its own conjugate.
   Fix such a b and put a0 = b−1 . Let M ⊂ C be a number field that contains τ (E) for every τ
and a field of definition of the elliptic curve of §III.4, with lattice Λ normalised by g23 − 27g32 = 1.
For an embedding ρ of M , Aρ is the area of the lattice of the conjugate curve, as in §III.4.
     Let M ⊂ M f be the OM -module spanned by the vectors (τ (α))τ , α ∈ a0 , and let

                                     lτ = {z ∈ M : z eτ ∈ M},                                 (III.202)

where eτ is the τ th coordinate vector. This is the integral line at the place τ (Lean: ellT). For a
nonzero z ∈ lτ the local squared period at (τ, ρ) is Aρ /(π|ρ(z)|2 ), and the mean of its logarithm is

                                                 121
defined, with a correction for the index, by
                                      1 X           Aρ                        
                         log Ωτ =             log           + 2 log     :                                (III.203)
                                                                    
                                                                     lτ   OM z    .
                                    [M : Q] ρ     π |ρ(z)|2

(Lean: meanLogOm, logMet.)          If lτ = OM zτ is principal, this is the plain mean over ρ of
log Aρ /(π|ρ(zτ )|2 ) .
   Proposition (the mean period). The number (III.203) does not depend on z, on τ , or on
M , and
                                   1           Aρ       rdE
                        log Ωτ =           log    − log √ .                     (III.204)
                                        X
                                 [M : Q] ρ     π          d
Under the inputs (I2), (I3) of §III.4, it equals γ + log(2π/rdE ) − cd = log ΩE .
    The proof uses four statements about ideals. N is the absolute norm of ideals of E.
    Lemma A (the tower formula). |disc E| = d f N(D). Consequently [E:Q]
                                                                     1
                                                                         log N(D) =
             √
log rdE − log d.
     Proof. Cited: the different is multiplicative in towers, DE/Q = D · Dkd /Q OE , and the absolute
norm of the different of a number field is the absolute value of its discriminant (J. Neukirch, Algebraic
Number Theory, 1999, Ch. III, (2.2)(i) and (2.9)). The norm of Dkd /Q OE is N(Dkd /Q )[E:kd ] = df ,
since the discriminant of kd is −d. For the second statement divide the logarithm by [E : Q] =
2f .
   Lemma B (the integral line). For a nonzero fractional ideal c of E let Mc be the OM -module
spanned by the vectors (τ ′ (α))τ ′ , α ∈ c. Then the line {z ∈ M : z eτ ∈ Mc } is the OM -module
generated by τ (c D). In particular lτ = τ (a0 D)OM = τ (b̄)OM .
      Proof. Let c∗ = {α∗ ∈ E : TrE/kd (α∗ c) ⊆ Okd } = c−1 D−1 (the definition of the different),
and let M∗ be the OM -module spanned by the vectors (τ ′ (α∗ ))τ ′ , α∗ ∈ c∗ . Use the bilinear
form ⟨m, m′ ⟩ = τ ′ mτ ′ m′τ ′ on M f . For α ∈ c and α∗ ∈ c∗ the pairing of their vectors is
                         P

   τ ′ τ (αα ) = TrE/kd (αα ) ∈ Okd ⊆ OM . (Every τ restricts to the given embedding of kd ; so the
P        ′     ∗                ∗                                 ′

map α 7→ (τ ′ (α))τ ′ is kd -linear, and the sum over τ ′ is the trace to kd .) We claim that Mc is exactly
the set of m with ⟨m, m′ ⟩ ∈ OM for all m′ ∈ M∗ . It is enough to check this after localising at each
prime of OM . Above a prime p of kd the module cp is free over the local ring of kd , with a basis
α1 , . . . , αf , and c∗p has the dual basis α1∗ , . . . , αf∗ for the trace form. The corresponding vectors ai ,
ai∗ of M f satisfy ⟨ai , ai∗′ ⟩ = δii′ , so they are two bases of M f , dual to each other, and they generate
the localisations of Mc and M∗ . This proves the claim. Now z eτ pairs with the vector of α∗ to
z τ (α∗ ). So z eτ ∈ Mc if and only if z τ (α∗ ) ∈ OM for all α∗ ∈ c∗ , that is, if and only if z lies in
the inverse of the ideal τ (c∗ )OM , which is τ (c D)OM .
    Lemma C (the index). For nonzero z ∈ lτ ,               [lτ : OM z] = |NM/Q (z)| · N(b̄)−[M :Q]/[E:Q] .
