    What the Proposition proves, and what it does not. It proves that the number (III.203), which
is defined by the ideal a0 and the areas Aρ , equals log ΩE , under (I2), (I3) and the fact about
E. Input (S4) is a different statement. It concerns the numbers Ωτ (v) of (S1), averaged over the
archimedean places v of K. To deduce (S4) from the Proposition one needs three further statements.
(P1) and (P3) are assumed, from the source, and not proved here. (P2) was proved above:
 (P1) the period that occurs in the block estimate of §III.4 for the kernel conjugated by ρ, at the
      weight place τ , is Aρ /(π|ρ(zτ )|2 ) for a generator zτ of lτ (Lean: logOmAt, ExtraTie.om_eq).
      Generators need not exist over a given M . An example: M = E, q = 3, d = 23, N = 4, 8 or
      16 (the class number of E is 3, 6, 60). There the ideal τ (b̄)OE of Lemma B is not principal
      for any of the choices of b coprime to b̄. This was computed with Sage 10.9, with class
      groups computed in its mode proof=False; so the example is correct under GRH and was
      not checked without it. Nothing in this section depends on the example. It depends on the
      choice of b: for N = 8 two of the eight integral b, which are not coprime to their conjugates,
      give a principal line. The existence of generators over a suitable larger field is the hypothesis
      IsDgen of mean_logMet_eq;
 (P2) every prime of E that divides D lies over 2 or over q (Lean: diffSupport). This is the first
      step of the fact about E above; it is proved there for the field E of the paper. With it the
      ideal b exists;
 (P3) the mean over the embeddings ρ of M can be replaced by the mean over the places v
      of K: the source takes M ⊇ K and for log Ωτ (v) an average, with weights of sum 1, of
      the numbers log Aρ /(π|ρ(zτ )|2 ) , the weights having the same total for every ρ (Lean:
      kerSide_of_meansS, exists_weights).

5.   The remaining inputs: statements and status

Each statement below is assumed in this section. We give its form in the source and say what a
proof needs. The Lean names are collected in subsection 6.
    (S1), the chain bound. The analytic content is proved in §III.4: the bound for one block
with the period (“Several blocks, and the period”), the bound for one map (“Sums over classes”),
and the bound for the Gaussian constant by a mean square. Compare with the formula for log bj (v)
and with the constant S of (III.51) there. |P| gj is the number g = 2nN N ′ of variables, with N ,
                                                                             ′
N ′ the ranks of the pair as in §III.4. βj∗ (v) is the prefactor β c2[E:Q]N N , and clj , cl′j count classes.
Θ1 (v) is a mean square per variable. In §III.4 the reference mean square at an embedding ρ is
                ′
Θ0,ρ = Hρf N N , with Hρ the mean square of one elliptic theta function. It enters S 2 once, with
⟨log Θ0,ρ ⟩ ≤ f N N ′ Lsc . In (S1) the same term is written |P| gj log Θ1 (v), with ⟨log Θ1 ⟩ ≤ Cth Lsc .
So Θ1 corresponds to Hρ , not to Θ0,ρ . What is assumed: that the maps of the construction are
instances (item (vi) of the list in §III.4), and the eight numerical bounds listed under (S1). Of
these, the bound for ⟨log Θ1 ⟩ is the conclusion of the paragraph “The reference mean square” of
§III.4 (conditional on (I1)–(I5)), divided by the number of variables; the bounds for βj∗ and for the
numbers of classes are statements about the finite data of the construction (coefficients, modulus,
class numbers of Hermitian lattices of bounded level). A proof needs a bound for class numbers of
definite Hermitian lattices in terms of the mass and the level. The source’s statements were not
read for this section, with one exception, the sign of vh . In the record of the source vh is a real
number with no condition on its sign (Lean: the field vv of HtNums, and the same in KerSide); the
logarithm of the Lean is defined for every real number, so the record needs none. Where the source
builds the record, vh = rdE /2 (Lean: NumDatum.v), and vh ≥ 0 is proved from rdE ≥ 1 inside the
proof of the bound with c5 (log_eight_leS). The source’s statement of the analytic bound for
one map (LiftedMapEmbeddingLogBound) has vh > 0 among its hypotheses. So vh ≥ 0 is not a
hypothesis of the record, and it is a consequence of the construction.
     (S2), the pairing at the end. The inequality ∥Π∥2v ≤ ehΠ , with hΠ bounded as in the
first line of (S2) with eΠ = 0, is the conclusion of the paragraph “The pairing at the end of the
chain” of §III.4, with its own inputs; there the two terms are bounded by log |ϑ| ≤ ψ d1 log Γ and
log κ ≤ Ψ(Ψ−1) 2  log Γ. The source does not name log |ϑ| and log κ: its numbers mτ , m′τ are these
two majorants with ψ = Nm − 1, Ψ = Nm and 2a in the place of Γ. The identification of d0,τ , d1,τ ,
ℓτ , λ1,τ , bτ with the d0 , d1 , ℓ, λ1 , bt of §III.4 was confirmed in the Lean (lastSide_of_logT_le).
The remaining conditions of (S2) express the exponent ℓτ = λ1,τ − d0,τ and the degrees d0,τ , d1,τ
through the weights of the construction: at a distinguished place the first weight is aτ up to the
shift (Nm − k)/2. They are statements about the weights only; this is the “substitution of the
actual weights” of item (ii) of the list in §III.4. A proof needs the weights of the last two spaces of
the chain as explicit functions of the data. In the source eΠ = 0 and CΠ = 0.
   (S3), the eigenvalues. This is the paragraph “The eigenvalues of a lift” of §III.4, with its

This is the deduction written in §III.4, with one difference. The mass bound in (III.63) is
            −1                                 −1
 (8f 2 )ϱ ϱ! ; here and in the Lean it is wEϱ ϱ! . The second implies the first, because wE ≤ 8f 2 .
The reason: write eul(u) for Euler’s function of an integer u ≥ 1. The field E contains Q(ζwE ),
which has degree eul(wE ) over Q. So eul(wE ) divides [E : Q] = 2f , and eul(wE ) ≤ 2f . Next,
u ≤ 2 eul(u)2 for every u ≥ 1: the quotient eul(u)2 /u is the product of the numbers pe−2 (p − 1)2
over the prime powers pe that divide u exactly, and such a number is at least 43 for p ≥ 3 and
at least 12 for p = 2. Hence wE ≤ 2(2f )2 = 8f 2 . The Lean has this inequality as a theorem,
for the field E of its record Host: host_torsionOrder_le, which states Units.torsionOrder
H.E ≤ 8f 2 . The inner-product formula itself is used in the following form (Lean: the hypothesis
InnerProductFormulaC of term_eval_lowC): for theta lifts θ(ϕi , fi ), to the definite group, of forms
fi on the group G lifted from,

the integral being written as a sum over finitely many cosets at the finite places and an integral
over the real points, absolutely convergent. This is the inner-product formula in the convergent
range, as an integral of matrix coefficients; it is not the form with a special value of an L-function,
and no Siegel–Weil formula appears in the statement. For the classical statements see S. Rallis,
L-functions and the oscillator representation, Lecture Notes in Math. 1245 (1987) [Ral87] and
Injectivity properties of liftings associated to Weil representations, Compositio Math. 52 (1984);
J.-S. Li, Non-vanishing theorems for the cohomology of certain arithmetic quotients, J. reine
angew. Math. 428 (1992) (the formula as an integral of matrix coefficients, in the stable range);
W. T. Gan, Y. Qiu, S. Takeda, The regularized Siegel–Weil formula (the second term identity)
and the Rallis inner product formula, Invent. Math. 198 (2014); S. Yamana, L-functions and
theta correspondence for classical groups, Invent. Math. 196 (2014). All from memory; none was
consulted. A proof of (S3) needs: this formula for the actual test functions; the unramified
computation of the local integrals (the factors of E0 ); the mass formula for definite unitary
groups (for instance [GHY01]; its formula is for maximal lattices, and local comparisons would
be needed for the levels used here); the formal degree of the discrete series at the real places
(Harish-Chandra); the transport to the other embeddings (E7); and the description of the target
by Hecke components (E6). The source has a theorem that derives the inner-product formula
(innerProductFormula_of_hypotheses) from four hypotheses. Three are statements named
Published.CuspRapidDecay, WeilSiegelFormulaUnitary and SiegelEisensteinContinuation.
The fourth is the existence of a choice of parameters (Lean: a ParameterChoice’ exists for some
SizeData2 ). Each of the three named statements has a closed theorem in the source. Their
proofs were not read. The dependency computation shows that the final theorem depends on
innerProductFormula_of_hypotheses; the three named statements were not put to it.

III.8       The images in general position: the trace-formula estimates
This section concerns the two smallness statements that the linear algebra of Part II, §6.9–6.10
consumes, and the identity that connects them with the pairings of the chain of lifts. In the Lean
development these are three statements: LinkS (bounds for the Gram blocks of a family of maps;
the trace statement), LinkIRR (a count; the rank statement), and BesselId (the pairing at the
end of the chain is an inner product with the image of such a map; the pairing statement). The
title needs a word of explanation. The source does not prove that the images of the maps are
nearly orthogonal. The images cannot all be mutually orthogonal: by the trace statement their
dimensions add up to more than the dimension of the target (Remark (1) of subsection 2). What
the source bounds is this: every block Bw† Bw′ with w ̸= w′ has at most (1 + η0 )(1 + η1 ) times
the mean size that it has for subspaces in general position, where η0 and η1 are the numbers of
Statement (S) of subsection 1, at most exp(−xε/8 ). So the operator Q = w Bw Bw† is close to its
                                                                          P

mean eigenvalue in Hilbert–Schmidt norm (Remark (1) of subsection 2). The operator Q is not a
sum of projections. Proved here: Lemma T1 and Corollary T2. This section deduces the dimension
bound from the three trace, rank and pairing statements and the size and subspace conditions in
T2, with explicit constants. It also proves Lemma T3, a statement about finite sets and weights
which is the mechanism of a second moment in the simplest case (a sum over twists of Bχ† Bχ is
a count of pairs of representations, and its main term is a mass). Quoted from the source: the
three statements themselves, transcribed in subsection 1, and the theorems from which the source
deduces them, listed in subsection 4 with their Lean names. The construction of the spaces and
maps, the arithmetic inputs and the assembly of (M) are quoted. None of these is proved here.
The error term that Part II, §6.10 takes from this section is recalled at the end of subsection 2.
Below, §1, §2, . . . without a part number mean the subsections of this section.

1.     The three statements in the source

The data. At a scale x the source works with a record (Lean: D7) which contains, among many

       (Dstar). The positivity of D∗ is not a field of the record. It follows from two fields of other

Both sides are linear in φ and in F . For the right side and F : replace F by iF . Then ΨF is
multiplied by i, since Ψ is linear; sΨF is multiplied by −i, since s is conjugate-linear; and the inner
product is multiplied by i, since it is conjugate-linear in its first variable. The map s cannot be
omitted: for a complex subspace E of H, a pairing that is linear on Mw × E cannot be equal to

in the source as a Bessel period of F ; in the form (III.222) no integral appears.

family, tr(Q2 ) ≥ (tr Q)2 /D, by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality for the eigenvalues of Q. If all Bw
are isometries, then Kww = 1, and this and (a) give

The sum of the bounds dw dw′ /D over the same pairs is (S 2 − w d2w )/D, which exceeds the right
                                                                     P

side by at most S, that is, by the relative amount D/S at most. By (III.220), 1/D∗ ≤ (1 + η1 )/D.
So (III.218) and (III.220) assert that the squared norm of every off-diagonal block is at most
(1 + η0 )(1 + η1 ) times dw dw′ /D. Up to this factor and up to the relative error D/S, that is the least
size that the blocks can have on average. Statement (S) bounds D by D∗ from above only; the bound
in the other direction is in (R3) of §4 and is not used in this section. The number dw dw′ /D is also the
mean size for subspaces in general position: if Ξ is a random unitary map of H for Haar measure and
ζ, ζ ′ are unit vectors, the mean of |⟨ζ, Ξζ ′ ⟩|2 is 1/D, so the mean of ∥B † ΞB ′ ∥2HS for isometries B, B ′
of ranks ν, ν ′ is νν ′ /D. Orthogonal images would mean Kww′ = 0. Mutually orthogonal images
have dimensions that add up to at most D. For injective maps this is impossible when S > D. The
maps of the source are not known to be injective, but (III.219) bounds their ranks from below. The
operator Kww − 1 is −1 on the kernel of Bw , so ∥Kww − 1∥2HS ≥ dw − rank Bw , and (III.219) gives
rank Bw ≥ (1 − e0 ) dw . Under the hypotheses of Corollary T2 below, e0 ≤ 64          1
                                                                                        , S ≥ xCH D∗ ≥ 4D∗
and D ≤ 98 D∗ . So the ranks add up to at least 63     64 S > D, which is more than the dimension of the
target, and the images cannot all be mutually orthogonal. The Gram block estimates control their
total squared overlap and put Q close to its mean eigenvalue in Hilbert–Schmidt norm. Precisely,
for a Hermitian operator Q on H one has tr(Q2 ) − (tr Q)2 /D = ∥Q − (tr Q/D) · 1∥2HS , so under the
hypotheses of Lemma T1(c) the bound (III.225) is

the projections onto the images: Bw Bw† is a projection only if Bw is a partial isometry. (2) The
                           √
constant 5. The term (1 + e0 )2 S in (III.223) comes from the diagonal blocks. It is the reason why
(III.225) needs D∗ ≤ κS: the total dimension S of the sources must exceed the dimension of the
target by the factor 1/κ. A term of this size is present on average for maps in general position:
for isometries Bw composed with independent random unitary maps of H, the mean of tr(Q2 ) is

do not bound tr(Q2 )D/(tr Q)2 − 1 from below. For example, if every Bw is a unitary map of H,
then (III.218)–(III.220) hold with D∗ = D and η0 = e0 = η1 = 0, and Q = |W| · 1, so this number
is 0, while D∗ /S = 1/|W|. (Another example, with dw = 1: the p(p + 1) lines spanned by the

    The estimate in H. The space E = s(E0 ) is a complex subspace of H, because c s(F ) = s(c̄F ). Its

which have the dimensions of the Uw , with αram + α′ in place of α, and with δE = δp . Since Pw and

E. This step uses only the subspace E of H, and neither s nor the scalars of E0 .
    The passage to the dual. Let Λ : H → E0∨ send h to the form

                                Λ(h) : F 7−→ ⟨sΨF, h⟩H              (F ∈ E0 ).                      (III.236)

The form Λ(h) is C-linear in F , by the computation after (III.222); so it lies in E0∨ . The map Λ is
C-linear in h. Its kernel is the set of h orthogonal to sΨ(E0 ) = s(E0 ) = E, that is, E ⊥ . So Λ = Λ ◦ πE ,
the restriction of Λ to E is injective, and since dim E = Dr = dim E0∨ it maps E isomorphically onto
E0∨ . Hence Λ maps H onto E0∨ , and Λ(Y) has in E0∨ the codimension of Y in E. By (III.222), the


                                                   135
map φ 7→ Bw (φ, ·) from Mw to E0∨ is λw ΛPw Ψw , a C-linear map. The image of Uw under it is
ΛπE Pw Ψw Uw , because λw ̸= 0 and the image is a complex subspace. So the sum of these images is
Λ(Y).
    The algebraic model. The map EL → E0 is linear over L and maps an L-basis to a C-basis. So
restriction of forms along it is a C-linear isomorphism of E0∨ with HomL (EL , L) ⊗L C. Under it and
under Mw,L ⊗L C = Mw , the map φ 7→ Bw (φ, ·) is βw : both are C-linear, and by (P1) they agree
on the image of Mw,L , which spans Mw .
     On the two scalar actions. Every space in this proof is a complex space, and every map in
it is C-linear, except s. The conjugate-linear map s occurs only inside the inner product, in its
conjugate-linear variable. The constants are those of Lemma T1(d).

that occur are complexifications of L-subspaces of Mw,L .

3.     The mechanism in a finite model

Statement (S) is proved in the source by expanding each block Kww′ as a sum of terms and
computing its squared norm as a sum over pairs of terms (see §4). The simplest instance of this kind
of computation is the following, for theta lifts between two definite groups as in Part II, §6.1–6.2.
It is a statement about finite sets.
    The setting. Let I and I ′ be nonempty finite sets with positive weights ω and ω ′ (classes
of lattices Li and L′i′ , with ω(i) = 1/|Aut Li |). For i ∈ I let Gi be a finite group of order
1/ω(i). For i ∈ I, i′ ∈ I ′ let Rep(i, i′ ) be a finite set on which Gi acts freely (the isometric
embeddings ρ : Li → L′i′ , with σ ∈ Aut Li acting by ρ 7→ ρσ). Let A be a finite abelian group and
inv : Rep(i, i′ ) → A maps with inv(ρσ) = inv(ρ) for σ ∈ Gi . For a character χ of A put
             Θχ (i, i′ ) =                                            (Bχ f )(i′ ) =             ω(i) Θχ (i, i′ ) f (i).   (III.241)

    Proof. (a) The adjoint of Bχ is (Bχ† g)(i) = i′ ω ′ (i′ )Θχ (i, i′ )g(i′ ), as one sees by writing
                                                          P

out ⟨Bχ f, g⟩ = ⟨f, Bχ† g⟩. So Bχ† Bχ has the kernel i′ ω ′ (i′ )Θχ (i1 , i′ )Θχ (i2 , i′ ). Insert (III.241)
                                                       P

and sum over χ: by the orthogonality of the characters of A, χ χ(inv(ρ1 ))χ(inv(ρ2 )) is |A| if
                                                                   P

inv(ρ1 ) = inv(ρ2 ) and 0 otherwise. This gives |A| N (i1 , i2 ).
     (b) Let i1 = i2 = i. The pairs (ρ, ρσ) with ρ ∈ Rep(i, i′ ) and σ ∈ Gi are distinct for distinct
(ρ, σ), because the action is free; they satisfy inv(ρ) = inv(ρσ); and their number is |Rep(i, i′ )|/ω(i).
By (III.243) their contribution to N (i, i) is m/ω(i), which is the kernel of m · 1. The rest is
N ◦ , by definition, and N ◦ ≥ 0 because it is a weighted count. (III.244) is the formula for the
Hilbert–Schmidt norm of an operator with a kernel.
    (c) For a Hermitian operator Q on a space of dimension ν and any real y, tr(Q2 ) − (tr Q)2 /ν =
∥Q − trνQ ∥2HS ≤ ∥Q − y∥2HS . Take ν = |I| and y = |A| m and use (III.244). Also tr Q = |A|(m|I| +
tr N◦ ) ≥ |A| m|I|, because tr N◦ = i ω(i)N ◦ (i, i) ≥ 0. Divide.
                                    P

    What the lemma shows. The sum over the twists is a count of pairs of representations subject to
one condition, inv(ρ1 ) = inv(ρ2 ): this is the form a relative trace formula takes when both groups
are definite. The pairs that are forced, (ρ, ρσ), give a multiple of the identity, and the multiple
is the mean number of representations (III.243). The other pairs are coincidences, and η is their
normalised mean square. Part (b) uses three hypotheses, and each is needed. The first is that the
action of Gi is free: with stabilisers of order 2 the identity of (b) fails. The second is the invariance
inv(ρσ) = inv(ρ): without it the forced pairs do not all satisfy the condition, and the identity of (b)
and (III.244) fail. (The number N ◦ is a restricted count and stays nonnegative; it is the kernel
N − m/ω on the diagonal that can become negative.) The third is (III.243), which is not formal.
For lattices it is Siegel’s theorem: if I ′ is a full genus and the Li belong to one genus, the left side
of (III.243) is the mass of the genus I ′ times a product of local densities, which depend only on the
genus of Li (C. L. Siegel, Über die analytische Theorie der quadratischen Formen, Ann. of Math. 36
(1935); in the adelic form, A. Weil, Sur la formule de Siegel dans la théorie des groupes classiques,
Acta Math. 113 (1965); both cited from memory).
     An example. Let L1 , L2 be the binary lattices with Gram matrices 21 12          1  and 41 16 (twice
                                                                                                   

the forms X1 + X1 X2 + 6X2 and 2X1 + X1 X2 + 3X2 of discriminant −23, which form one genus
              2               2             2               2

and represent the two classes under GL2 (Z)), with automorphism groups of orders 4 and 2, and
let I ′ consist of the lattice Z5 , which is alone in its genus, with ω ′ = 1/(25 5!). A count gives
|Rep(L1 , Z5 )| = |Rep(L2 , Z5 )| = 1920, so (III.243) holds with m = 12 ; for Z 6 both numbers are
19200. (For two forms of determinant 60 in different genera, 20 30      0  and 60 10
                                                                                   0 , the numbers for Z5

are 4480 and 13440.) Let ς0 , . . . , ς9 be the ten 2 × 2 minors of the pair of vectors ρ, in lexicographic
order. Take for inv(ρ) the 55 products ςι ςι′ modulo        a prime ℓ0 ; they are invariant under Gi , and
A = (Z/ℓ0 )55 . For ℓ0 = 3 one finds N ◦ = 20 03 . Then the true value tr(Q2 )|I|/(tr Q)2 − 1 is 19 , and
                                                    

the bound (III.245) gives η = 5. For ℓ0 = 5 and ℓ0 = 11 there are no coincidences, N ◦ = 0, and
   χ Bχ Bχ = |A| m exactly. With the single invariant inv(ρ) = ς0 + 2ς9 + 3ς1 ς4 modulo 5 (so |A| = 5)
P      †                                                              2     2

the coincidences dominate: N ≈ 242◦
                                          173 209 . So the family of twists must be large compared with
                                              173

the number of representations for the count N ◦ to be small. The identities (III.244), (III.245) were
also checked numerically on random finite models.

With the upper bound in (III.217), G d2w /D∗ ≤ (C0 /G) dw : this is (III.219), and it shows where
e0 comes from. The diagonal bound is small because each Cw is a small part of H, of relative
dimension about G−2 , while the constant in the second bound of (III.246) is only G. This is an
expansion of the kind of Petersson’s formula for the inner products of Poincaré series, here for the
Bessel periods of a unitary group in 2( m 2 − 1) + k + 1 variables along a unitary group in k variables;
this number is k + m − 1, the r of §III.4 (the periods of W. T. Gan, B. Gross and D. Prasad,
Astérisque 346 (2012)); it is a relative trace formula in the sense of Jacquet. In the source the
squared norm of a part of the sum is expressed as a sum, over pairs of terms, of inner products of
vectors attached to the terms (pairSum; compare (III.242)), and the pairs are divided by a rank
s0 ≥ 1 and by being “scalar” or not. The pairs with s0 = 1 that are scalar and generic give the
main term. The statements read are bounds for the classes of pairs: RowNGP (non-generic terms: at
most 2−(k−m−3) exp(Cm5 f log b) dw dw′ /D∗ ) and RowMainEqP (the main pairs of a diagonal block:
at most exp(Cm5 f log b) d2w /D∗ ). Both are deduced in the source from (R2). The saving in η0 = b−6
comes from k and from the level primes: the non-generic and the higher-rank pairs carry factors
2−k and exp(−ckf log k) with k ≥ C ′ m5 f xε , and the factor v (1 + 3/|Fv |) over the places above
                                                                 Q

g0 , g1 is 1 + O(b−7 ) when g0 , g1 ≥ (kb)8 (NumPrice). How these are combined in the proof of the
first bound of (III.246) was not read.
    (R2) The Siegel–Weil formula in the convergent range (SiegelWeilUnitaryConvergent;
the theorem siegelWeilUnitaryConvergent is among the declarations the final theorem depends
on). Let E/E + be a CM extension, J a nondegenerate Hermitian matrix of size M over E, and ν
an integer with 2ν < M . The quotient of the adelic points of the unitary group of J by its rational
points has finite volume, and for every nondegenerate Hermitian Z of size ν and every factorisable
Schwartz function Φ = Φ∞ ⊗ v Φv , the mean over this quotient of X Φ(gX), the sum being over
                                N                                    P

the ν-tuples X of rational vectors with Gram matrix Z, equals ϱ∞ (Z) v ϱv (Z), where ϱv is the
                                                                           Q

density at Z of the image of the measure Φv dX under the Gram map. (The description of the
theta sum is our reading of the names; its definition was not read.) This is Siegel’s theorem in
Weil’s form, for a unitary group, in a range of absolute convergence; for unitary groups beyond that
range see A. Ichino, A regularized Siegel–Weil formula for unitary groups, Math. Z. 247 (2004), and
S. Yamana, On the Siegel–Weil formula: the case of singular forms, Compos. Math. 147 (2011). It
plays the role of (III.243): it evaluates the mean number of representations, and so the main pairs,
by local densities. It contains no saving by itself.
    (R3) The count of H (CountAllPointsClass; it gives (III.220)).                 |D − D∗ | ≤
exp(−c rf log r) D∗ , where D is the multiplicity of a discrete series representation, at a fixed
level, in the automorphic spectrum of the unitary group in r variables, and D∗ is the product of


                                                    140
its formal degree, the volume of the quotient and the value at 1 of the idempotent defining the
level, provided the Harish-Chandra parameter has all gaps at least Cr19 f 5 log(b rdE ), and under
two conditions on the sizes of f and b. (The description of D and D∗ is our reading of the
definitions Dcount and Dstar.) This is a limit multiplicity statement with a rate (in the tradition
of D. L. de George and N. R. Wallach, Ann. of Math. 107 (1978), and L. Clozel, Invent. Math. 83
(1986)); D∗ is the identity term of the trace formula. The saving comes from the gaps of the weights.
The volume in D∗ is a mass. The mass formula of the source is siegel_mass_ratio: the quotient
of the masses in ranks M and M − 1 is ((M − 1)! (v/π)M )f (2v)−f /2 times a further factor (here
v is a positive real number, not a place, and π is the number), which is the recurrence displayed
before (6.4) in Part II, §6.6 (H. Braun, Abh. Math. Sem. Hamburg 14 (1941); W. T. Gan, J. Hanke
and J.-K. Yu, Duke Math. J. 107 (2001)). Here we read the source’s vInv, fDeg, lValEta as v, f
and that factor; their definitions, and the place where the formula is applied to D∗ , were not read.
    (R4) The count behind (I) (closed_linkIRR_of_join_count, delp_of_allRows). The
space H has a basis each of whose D elements carries, at each of the places above g0 and above g1 ,
a label: a monic polynomial of degree r over Fgι (ι = 0, 1) with nonzero constant term. A tuple
of labels at the places above one gι is good if the polynomials are squarefree, their roots satisfy
two conditions of non-coincidence, for every j with 1 ≤ j ≤ r/2 some place has a label with no
factor of degree j, and some place has a label with an irreducible factor of prime degree j ′ with
r/2 < j ′ ≤ r − 3 (IsGood). Dr is the number of basis elements whose tuples are good at both
primes. The proof of delp_of_allRows contains the step
                           1 + εC
                                  Wg(g0 ) + Wg(g1 ) ,      εC = exp(−c rf log r),             (III.247)
                                                   
                    δp ≤
                           1 − εC
            3f   23f log g            1         1 + log r  r  7 f           f 
  Wg(g) =      +   √       + 2f m              +            +         + exp −         , (III.248)
             g        g            4 min(q, d)     g−1        2 8             5 log r
where q, d are the auxiliary primes of §III.1. The statement delp_of_allRows has as a hypothesis
that the right side of (III.247) is at most a number of the source (E7), and as its conclusion
0 ≤ δp ≤ exp(−xεz /7 ); (III.247) itself is not a statement of the source. Two inputs of that proof:
the count (R3), applied to the part of H with prescribed labels, whose expected dimension is D∗
times a product over the places of local weights; and the fact that the local weight of a squarefree
label is g −r (1 + O(r/g)), the labels that are not squarefree having total weight at most 3/g (admLq,
lawAt), so that the labels at each place are distributed, up to these errors, like uniformly random
monic polynomials of degree r with nonzero constant term. The latter rests on the parametrisation
of the irreducible characters of GLr (Fg ) by J. A. Green, The characters of the finite general linear
groups, Trans. Amer. Math. Soc. 80 (1955), which the source takes as a hypothesis Green (the
cuspidal characters of GLj (Fg ) correspond to the monic irreducible polynomials of degree j with
nonzero constant term and have degree j−1       i=1 (g − 1); for j = 1 these are the g − 1 polynomials
                                                      i

X − a with a =   ̸ 0, and the polynomial X is excluded; Lean: IsPrimeLab). The terms of Wg(g)
are then statements about the factorisation of uniformly random monic polynomials with nonzero
constant term, taken at f independent places. The bound (III.221) follows from (III.247) and the
sizes g0 , g1 ≥ exp(8xεz ), f ≥ x2 , f, m, r ≤ xC and min(q, d) ≥ exp(xεz /6 ) (SizesAt). The term that
limits the bound is f m/(2 min(q, d)); the others are much smaller. The good tuples are those for
which the source can prove that an attached residual Galois representation is irreducible; this use
was not read. By the dependency computation the final theorem depends on delp_of_allRows
and on Green.
   (R5) Uniqueness of Bessel models, and decay (besselMapUniqGap, wuDecayOfGap,

whose Harish-Chandra parameter has all gaps at least 120r3 , a representation of the unitary group in

proportional. (The source states this for k + 2j + 1 variables with any j ≥ 0; here j = m
                                                                                        2 − 1, which
gives r variables.) Compare Theorem A of D. Jiang, B. Sun and C.-B. Zhu, Uniqueness of Bessel
models: the archimedean case, Geom. Funct. Anal. 20 (2010): it gives multiplicity at most one for
Bessel models at an archimedean place. The statement of the source has in addition a hypothesis
on the gaps. Theorem A does not give the quantitative decay used here. The decay is a further
statement of the source: from the uniqueness it deduces an integrability statement for a function

the convergence of the expansion in (R1). Statement (P2) is proved by adm13_of_kernel_join,
from hypotheses that were not read.

5.    The interlacing of the weights

At each weight place the source fixes rational numbers

     ξ0 > ψ0 > ξ1 > ψ1 > · · · > ψk−2 > ξk−1 > −θ0 ,         θ0 < e′0 < θ1 < e′1 < · · · < θm−1 < e′m−1 ,
                                                                                                  (III.249)
with ξi ∈ Z+ 12 and ψi , θj , e′j ∈ Z (HCParam; the condition is named Interlace; we write ξi , ψi , θj , e′j
for the numbers called u, v, b, e there; e′0 is not the e0 of (S)). The first chain is the condition
under which the representation of the unitary group in k − 1 variables with parameter ψ occurs in
the restriction of the one in k variables with parameter ξ: the classical branching rule, here for
parameters shifted by half the sum of the positive roots. In this section it is used in one place,
the definition of the family: the words are the weights ξ that interlace one fixed ψ. Precisely,
consecutive ψl differ by 4∆ for a fixed ∆, the numbers ξ0 and ξk−1 are the same for all words, and
for 0 ≤ l ≤ k − 3
                             ξl+1 = ψl − 12 − ∆ · (the lth letter of the word),                   (III.250)
and for both values of the letter this number lies between ψl+1 and ψl , since (III.249) is part of the
data for every word (cloud_gap, u_letter, hcW_v, hcW_first, hcW_last). So two words differ in

                                                    142
the weight ξ at the positions where their letters differ. That the bounds (III.246) for w ̸= w′ use
this (through the archimedean factors of the terms) is our reading of the definitions; the proofs were
not read. The second chain of (III.249), with gaps at least (t0 + 3)/2 (gap_be, gap_eb), concerns
the weights along the chain of lifts and belongs to the divisibility and size estimates (§III.4, §III.5).

     What this section proves, and what it assumes. This section deduces the dimension
bound from the three trace, rank and pairing statements (S), (I), (P) and the size and subspace
conditions (III.233), (III.234) in T2. Proved outright: Lemma T1, including the polynomial
inequality (III.230); Corollary T2, for arbitrary subspaces, as that deduction; Lemma T3; and
the arithmetic of the example of §3 (by computation). Quoted from the source without proof: (S),
(I), (P) and everything in §4 and §6. So the construction of the spaces and maps, the arithmetic
inputs and the assembly of (M) are quoted. Lemma T3 is a model. It is not a step of the
source’s proof: there the family is the set of words, the terms are attached to a Bessel subgroup,
and the larger group is, by our reading, not definite (the parameter sigY0 of the source is the
signature (k + m2 − 1, 2 ); it is the signature of the space Y 0 of the record at every embedding, by
                       m

the field baseSpace_signature_eq). The statements FrameLA, frame_linear_algebra_bound,

III.9     The bound for Galois cohomology
This section concerns the bound (II.46) of Part II, §6.8 (Part I, §I.7, “Denominators”): a bound for
the lengths of certain Galois cohomology groups of the field E, which the announcement [Alp26]
calls a weakening of Vandiver’s conjecture. It is the part of the proof that uses the most mathematics
from outside analytic number theory, and no proof of it is given here. The section states it exactly,
shows how it is used, describes the structure of its proof in the Lean development with the published
theorems that correspond to each step, and works out the simplest case.
   Proved outright. Lemma G1 (lengths, orders and indices); Proposition G2 (how the bound gives
the estimate for the denominators, given the inequality LinkN_at of the source).
   Proved from theorems of books that we consulted. Proposition G3: for the field Q the groups
vanish when a class group vanishes; with its corollaries for odd twists (Herbrand’s theorem), for the
twists with (ℓ − 1) | 2e, and under Vandiver’s conjecture. The theorems used are in Washington’s
book [Was82]; their statements
                         √      and numbers were checked in a copy of the first edition. Lemma G4:
the class number of Q( ℓ) is less than ℓ; it is proved from Minkowski’s theorem on lattice points
[Neu99, Ch. I, (4.4)]. The numerical statements of §5 are computed exactly.
   Proved from the standard statements of class field theory. The vanishing of Sha5 (Q(i), 4), at
the end of §5. The proof is for this one field and this one twist.
   Taken from the source without proof. Theorem G (the bound itself, Lean: VWinAt for the trivial
character); the inequality LinkN_at; and every statement of §4.
    Citations. They are of three kinds. (1) The books [Was82], [Neu99] and [NSW00] were read in
local copies ([Was82] and [NSW00] in the first edition); the test group G19 compared each statement
cited from them with the page. (2) The articles [Sou03], [Kat04], [Kur92], [BC04], [Car12], [Car14],
[CH13], [BS23] and the book [Rub00] were not available to us. Where a theorem or a section of

                                                 144
one of them is named, the wording was compared with the publication by an outside reviewer, and
we say no more than his report says. (3) Everything else is cited from memory and is marked so:
[Sou79], the theorem of Voevodsky and Rost, [MW84], [FW79], the work of Harris and Taylor and
of Shin, and the last column of the table of §4.
      Letters introduced in this section: ℓ (a prime, as in §III.5), e (the twist; Part II, §6.8 writes a),
t (a number of twists; in the construction it is the parameter written t0 in §III.5, and t in §III.4;
Part II, §6.8 does not name it and writes the bound ⌊x400 ⌋), V0 , Sℓ , GE,ℓ , Gv , We , Shaℓ (E, e), hℓ,e ,
h±ℓ,e , ȷ (complex conjugation), ψ (a Dirichlet character), B, ηj (w), Hj , y = x , V , eO , fO , κ, cψ , x ;
                                                                                     ε                     ′

in §4: K, R, R, R , c0 , ϑ, ϱ; and in §5: A, A , X∞ , H∞ , Γ, Π, ω, F , h, d, a, α, T , L, j, N , c, δ,
                      ′                             (i)

λ, z, ϕ, a1 , a2 , rec, u, ϵ, β. The letters x, ε, b, m, f, q, d, n have their meaning from Parts I and II;
in particular b = exp(xε ).
    The letters are local. The letters of this section have the meanings given here in this section
only. The clashes most likely to confuse are these. n is the power of 2 with [E : Q] = 4n; §III.5
takes its n from the record of the construction (ord), and §1 says how the order of the frame is
related to the n of Theorem G; Soulé’s twist, which he writes n, is written n′ in §4. t is a number of
twists, the t0 of §III.5; in §III.5 the letter t is an index. e is the twist; e(w) and eO are ramification
indices (the ew of §III.6); e is never the base of the natural logarithm here. L0 is the base field of a
tower, in §4 only; the coefficient field, which is L0 in §III.6, is called K here, and K is an imaginary
quadratic field. ψ is a Dirichlet character and Ψ = ψχeℓ a character; in §III.2 ψ(u, χ) is a sum over
prime powers, in §III.4 and §III.7 ψ and Ψ are ranks, and in §III.8 they are weights and unitary
maps. C is the constant of (III.257), or an exponent in the table of §4; it is not the C of another
section, and it is not one of c (a homomorphism, in §5), c0 , cψ . Inside the section four letters have
two or more uses. T is a finite group in Lemma G1 and a real number in Lemma G4. N is a level
or a conductor in §3 and §4, the norm N (w), N (a) of a prime or an ideal, and the norm map of
class groups in the proof of the corollaries. j numbers the maps of the chain in §2 and is the map
that extends ideals in §5. ϵ = ±1 is a sign in Step 4 of §5; it is not ε.


1.   The statement in the source

The groups. Let E be a number field, ℓ a prime and e an integer. Let Sℓ be the set of primes of
E above ℓ, and GE,ℓ the Galois group over E of the largest extension of E in which no finite prime
outside Sℓ ramifies. Let χℓ : GE,ℓ → Z×
                                      ℓ be the cyclotomic character, and let We = Qℓ /Zℓ (−e): the
discrete group Qℓ /Zℓ on which σ acts by multiplication by χℓ (σ)−e . For v ∈ Sℓ let Gv ⊂ GE be a
decomposition group at v, mapped to GE,ℓ . Put
                                                                                  
                       Shaℓ (E, e) = ker H 1 (GE,ℓ , We ) −→          H 1 (Gv , We ) ,
                                                                Y

                                                               v∈Sℓ                                (III.251)
                              hℓ,e = lengthZℓ Shaℓ (E, e) ∈ {0, 1, 2, . . . } ∪ {∞}.

Cohomology is that of continuous cochains: H 1 is the group of continuous maps ξ with
ξ(σσ ′ ) = ξ(σ) + σ · ξ(σ ′ ), modulo the maps σ 7→ σ · w − w. So Shaℓ (E, e) consists of the
classes that are unramified outside ℓ and trivial at every prime above ℓ, as in Part II, §6.8.
The length of a Zℓ -module is the length of a longest chain of submodules; a finite module of
length h has ℓh elements, and the length is ∞ if there is no finite bound. In the Lean: LData
(a choice of a place of E above each v ∈ Sℓ and of the character χℓ on GE,ℓ ), Sha and shaLen
(Formalism/GaloisCohomology/Statement.lean, lines 54, 75, 78); the value ∞ is written ⊤. The
length does not depend on the choices; this is proved in the source (shaLen_eq_shaInv).


                                                    145
   The statement VVle(E, t, V0 ), for an integer t ≥ 1 and a real number V0 (Lean: VVle, in the
namespace UpperTheory.Top; ExistsPageN.lean:12). For every algebraic closure of E and every
choice of the data above, for every prime ℓ:

             (1 ≤ e < t),      and             log ℓ                       for every finite set S of primes.
                                         X              X
  hℓ,e < ∞                                                     hℓ,e ≤ V0
                                         ℓ∈S           1≤e<t
                                                                                         (III.252)
(The first clause is needed: in the Lean sum an infinite length is counted as 0.) If VVle(E, t, V0 )
holds, t′ ≤ t and V0 ≤ V0′ , then VVle(E, t′ , V0′ ) holds (VVle.mono).
    The field. For √ n ≥ 1 a power of 2 and primes q ≡ 3 (mod 8), d ≡ 7 (mod 16), let E =
           −1 √
Q(ζ4n + ζ4n  , −q, −d). It is the subfield of a cyclotomic field fixed by the automorphisms ζ 7→ ζ α
with α ≡ ±1 (mod 4n) and q = d = 1 (Lean: IsChiField for the trivial character, line 19).
                             α      α

It is a field with complex multiplication, of degree 4n over Q, and its Galois group over Q is
Z/n × (Z/2)2 .
    Theorem G [quoted]. (Lean: VWinAt for the trivial character and the exponent 401, lines
44–49 of the same file; proved in the source as vwin_of_row.) Let 0 < ε < 1/52, and for x ≥ 1
let n(x) be a power of 2 with x2+8ε ≤ n(x) ≤ 2x2+8ε . There are J ≥ 1 and X0 with the following
property. Let X ≥ X0 , put Y0 = X 1/J , and let q ≡ 3 (mod 8) and d ≡ 7 (mod 16) be primes with

       1 ε/5             ε/5         1 ε/5                     ε/5
       2 Y0  < log q ≤ Y0 ,          2 Y0  log2 Y0 < log d ≤ Y0 log2 Y0 ,            q | d + 1,      (III.253)
                                        ε/5
and such that every odd prime p ≤ Y0 divides d + 1. Then there is Y with Y0 ≤ Y and Y 2 ≤ X
such that, for every x ∈ [Y, Y 2 ] and for the field E with n = n(x),
                                               √ 
                           VVle E, ⌊x401 ⌋, b holds,           b = exp(xε ).        (III.254)

The congruences and sizes required of (q, d) are those of the primes of Estimate B (Lean: WinPair).
The interval [Y, Y 2 ] is the “window”; its origin is explained in §4.
     From Theorem G to the bound of Part II. The bound (II.46) of Part II has ⌊x400 ⌋ where
(III.254) has ⌊x401 ⌋. The source passes from one to the other by a change of scale (Lean: winPairs,
WinPairs.lean:5–81). Let cψ ≥ 1 be the constant of the source such that the order of the frame
of the construction is cψ times the power of 2 of Theorem G (the constant of fieldChi; we did
not evaluate it for the trivial character). Let κ ≥ 1 be the number with κ2+8ε = cψ . Theorem G is
applied at a point x′ , and the construction works at the scale x = κx′ . Then x2+8ε = cψ x′ 2+8ε , so
the bounds for n(x′ ) become x2+8ε ≤ cψ n(x′ ) ≤ 2x2+8ε . The proof takes x′ so large that κ400 ≤ x′ .
Hence
                        ⌊x400 ⌋ = ⌊κ400 x′ 400 ⌋ ≤ ⌊x′ 401 ⌋, exp(x′ ε ) ≤ exp(xε ),        (III.255)
                                                √
and (III.254) at x′ gives VVle(E, ⌊x400 ⌋, b) with b = exp(xε ), by VVle.mono. This is the field
bound of the record WinDatum (TwistForm.lean:25). The window [Y, Y 2 ] for x′ becomes a window
[Z, Z 3/2 ] for x, with Z = κY ; this uses κ3 ≤ Y . So the remark of Part II, §6.8, that the source
states the bound for twists below x401 at a slightly different scale, is accurate.
    The number of twists of the construction (Lean: R.t) is not a free parameter. The condition
RankVal (PairRowIffQdRows.lean:28–32) fixes it by an equation in the other parameters, and
eventually_rank_le_rpow_of_lt_one_div_fiftytwo proves that it is at most x400 for x ≥ 2100 .
The theorem supplyOfAdmOf then applies VVle.mono once        √more. The family of the construction
receives the result in the form HypV: for large x, VVle(E, t, b) holds for its fields E and its number
of twists t (DivinNOfHypV.lean:10). The declarations named in this paragraph and in the preceding
one are among those the final theorem depends on.

                                                       146
2.   From lengths to indices of lattices

Lemma G1. (i) Let T be a finite abelian group and hℓ the length of its ℓ-primary part as a
Zℓ -module. Then log |T | = ℓ hℓ log ℓ. (ii) Let O be the ring of integers of a finite extension of
                               P

Qℓ , with ramification index eO and residue degree fO . If T is an O-module of finite length, then
lengthZℓ T = fO lengthO T and |T | = ℓfO lengthO T . If T0 is a finite Zℓ -module, then lengthO (O ⊗Zℓ
T0 ) = eO lengthZℓ T0 . (iii) Let M ′ ⊂ M be lattices of the same rank over the ring of integers OK
of a number field K. Then log[M : M ′ ] = w lengthOK,w (M/M ′ )w log N (w), the sum over the
                                             P

primes w of K; here N (w) is the norm and the subscript w is the completion.
    Proof. (i) A finite Zℓ -module of length h has a chain of submodules with h successive quotients
Z/ℓ, so it has ℓh elements; and T is the product of its primary parts. (ii) The simple O-module is
the residue field, which has ℓfO elements and length fO over Zℓ . For T0 = Z/ℓs the module O/ℓs O
has length eO s, because ℓO is the eO th power of the maximal ideal; a general T0 is a product of
such groups. (iii) M/M ′ is the product of its completions, by the Chinese remainder theorem, and
a module of length h over OK,w has N (w)h elements.
    By (ii), lengthO (O ⊗ T0 )/eO does not depend on O. The Lean uses this normalisation when it
enlarges the coefficients to treat a group character by character (Chr.nrm: the length divided by
the length of O/ℓO, which is eO ; Lean: eO).
   The inequality of the source. The construction has a coefficient field, a number field which
we call K here (Lean: LL), and for each map of the chain, numbered j = 1, . . . , m, and each prime
w of K a rational number ηj (w) ≥ 0 (Lean: etaJ). A prime w is called twisted if the character
twist chosen at w is not the base one (Lean: µw ̸= µ0 ). Put

                                                  log N (w)
                                 Hj =                       e(w) ηj (w),                       (III.256)
                                          X

                                        w twisted
                                                   [K : Q]

where e(w) is the ramification index of w over Q and the sum is over a finite set (Lean: etaSum,
GlobalTheoremInputs.lean:36). The sum runs over twisted w only.
     What is known about the meaning of ηj (w). The number ηj (w) of (III.256) is the field etaJ of
the record of data of the final theorem (DataRows.lean:162). The source also has a number with the
same name for a family of lattices (DefFam.etaJ, LiftLat.lean:147). For that number the meaning
is clear. Times e(w) it is the length of a quotient of two lattices at w: the definition is BlockDat.etaW
(LiftLat.lean:116), the length over the localisation at w of A/(A ∩ A′ ) for two lattices A, A′
(locLen), and the relation η · e(w) = etaW is eta_units (LiftLatLemmas.lean:46). The final
theorem depends on etaW, locLen and eta_units. It is not established here that the field etaJ of
the record equals the number of the family at the twisted primes. An earlier version of this section
cited the declarations TieEta (Statement.lean:90) and etaN_eq_etaJ (CoverAct.lean:108) for
this. By the dependency computation of the test group G19, both are in the archive and the final
theorem depends on neither. So they establish nothing about the ηj (w) that the final theorem uses.
The statement that is missing is this: for the record of data of the final theorem and every twisted
w, e(w) ηj (w) equals etaW at w, a length of A/(A ∩ A′ ). The archive contains statements of this
form (the field etaJ of the record NumTie, RowSideTie.lean:244, and the theorem etaJ_join);
whether the final theorem depends on them was not computed. The record itself only says that
ηj (w) is a sum of local numbers (its field etaJ_tie). To decide the question one has to read the
proof of LinkN (linkN_of_twGood_galoisPreconditions, which the final theorem depends on).
Until this is done, the following is an expectation and not a statement of this paper: that e(w)ηj (w)
is the length at w of a quotient M/M ′ of two lattices over OK ; that, by Lemma G1(iii), [K : Q] Hj
is the part of log[M : M ′ ] that comes from the twisted primes; and that ηj (w) is the defect of the

only the inequality LinkN_at.

3.    Why the statement is of Vandiver type

Let ℓ be odd and let ȷ be complex conjugation on E. It acts on Shaℓ (E, e) as an involution, and the
Lean splits the group into the part where ȷ = (−1)e and the part where ȷ = (−1)e+1 , of lengths h+   ℓ,e
and h−ℓ,e ; hℓ,e = h+
                    ℓ,e + h−
                           ℓ,e (shaLen_eq_add, proved there by linear algebra). Let E  be abelian  over
Q and ℓ ∤ [E : Q]. In the classical theory the group then splits, after enlarging the coefficients to a
ring O, into parts indexed by the Dirichlet characters ψ of Gal(E/Q); the part of ψ lies in the plus
part if ψ(−1) = (−1)e and in the minus part otherwise. The source does not state a decomposition.
It proves an inequality (HV.redPrime): for ℓ ̸= 2 and ℓ ∤ [E : Q],

                                                      lengthO the group of ψ ,                  (III.259)
                                           X
                          eO h+
                                                                                   
                              ℓ,e ≤
                                       ψ(−1)=(−1)e

the sum over the characters ψ of Gal(E/Q) with values in O. The group of ψ is defined over Q,
with coefficients twisted by ψ (Lean: Vlen, the length of Chr.V’ at the level ℓN , N the conductor
of E). For the other ℓ there is a similar inequality with a loss term (HV.redAll). For the field E of
Theorem G every odd ℓ is prime to [E : Q] = 4n.
   By the duality theorem of Poitou and Tate, Shaℓ (E, e) has the same length as the kernel of
H 2 (GE,ℓ , Zℓ (e + 1)) →  v|ℓ H (Gv , Zℓ (e + 1)). The duality is [NSW00, §8.6, Thm. 8.6.8], and
                                2
                          Q

the long exact sequence that contains it is Theorem 8.6.13 there; these numbers are those of the

                                                      148
first edition, which was read. Theorem 8.6.8 is stated there for finitely generated modules and
Theorem 8.6.13 for finite modules. For the statement used here, with the coefficients Qℓ /Zℓ (−e)
and Zℓ (e + 1), one passes to limits, and we did not write this out. In the Lean this equality of
lengths is the statement PTShaAt (Statement.lean:101); it is stated under the condition that
ℓ ̸= 2 or E has no real place. For e ≥ 1 the group H 2 (GE,ℓ , Zℓ (e + 1)) is finite (Soulé [Sou79],
from memory) and, for odd ℓ, isomorphic to K2e (OE ) ⊗ Zℓ , where K2e is Quillen’s K-group (the
conjecture of Quillen and Lichtenbaum, a consequence of the theorem of Voevodsky and Rost; from
memory). Take E = Q. For odd e the order of K2e (Z) ⊗ Zℓ is the power of ℓ in the numerator of
Be+1 /(e + 1): this is the minus part, and it is governed by a value of ζ (Mazur and Wiles [MW84],
from memory). For even e the group is the plus part. Vandiver’s conjecture says that ℓ does not
divide the class number of Q(ζℓ + ζℓ−1 ). Kurihara [Kur92] relates it to the vanishing of the groups
K4s (Z), s ≥ 1: his Proposition 3.7 proves one implication, and his Remark 3.11 gives the converse
under the comparison of K-theory with cohomology that is used there (compared with the article
by a reviewer, not by us). An equivalence for the integral groups also needs that comparison and a
treatment of the 2-primary component. We do not use an equivalence. Corollary (c) of §5 proves
the sufficient implication that is used here: under Vandiver’s conjecture, hℓ,e (Q) = 0 for all odd ℓ
and all even e ≥ 2.
    Theorem G is weaker√than a statement of vanishing in two ways: it bounds a sum weighted
by log ℓ, and the bound b = exp(xε /2) is very large. It is stronger in one way: the field is E, of
degree about 4x2+8ε , and the twists go up to x401 . Theorem G asserts no vanishing for the plus
part of E. For the field Q some plus parts do vanish: those with (ℓ − 1) | 2e (Corollary (b) of §5;
this is the statement PropZ of the source under the identification discussed in §5; for example ℓ = 5,
e = 2), and all of them under Vandiver’s conjecture (Corollary (c)).


4.   The structure of the proof in the source

What follows was obtained by reading statements, and the proof terms that connect them, downward
from vwin_of_row. The long proofs were not read. Sources in the literature are cited as said in
the paragraph “Citations” at the head of the section: from memory, unless a theorem or a section
is named.
    Two dependency computations were made for this section, by the test groups
G12 and G19 (files outputs/depcheckG12.out and outputs/depcheckG19.out in
zeta-paper_tests/displays/G12 and G19). They give the same number of declarations
that the final theorem depends on (1 478 198) and the same three axioms. The version of this
section that was tested prints 133 Lean names. G12’s run covers 89 of them: 84 are among the
declarations the final theorem depends on, and five are not. G19’s run covers 35 of the other 44: 33
are among them, and two are not. The remaining nine are not names of declarations (local names,
fields of records and the three axioms). The seven exceptions are these. OneChar, Adm3, Adm2,
Sp4In and KatoUse are in the archive but the final theorem does not depend on them. It uses
variants: OneCharU_s (NumberU3US.lean:49) in place of OneChar, with the conditions Adm3U_s
and Adm2A in place of Adm3 and Adm2; and SymplecticFourImageDense in place of Sp4In. That
the variants are used is in G19’s run and in a run of the authors, not in G12’s. The other two
exceptions are TieEta and etaN_eq_etaJ; see §2. Names first printed in the present version
(NumTie, etaJ_join, etaJ_tie, DefFam.etaJ) were not tested.
    The top. vwin_of_row takes one hypothesis, Row0b (WeightsConjBis.lean:69): for every
field E with complex multiplication, every s ≥ 1, every regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic
representation π of GLs over E that is conjugate self-dual up to a twist, every ℓ and every


                                                 149
                                                                                              s
isomorphism ι : Qℓ ≃ C, there is a continuous semisimple representation of Gal(E/E) on Qℓ , with
predicted weights and the local comparisons in the record PropsB (lines 54–67). The record says
the following. The representation is continuous and semisimple. At the primes not above ℓ it is
compared with π in a weakened sense (AwayB). For each embedding τ of E a multiset of integers
is given, and it equals the multiset computed from the infinitesimal character of π; this equality,
and a symmetry under complex conjugation, are stated without a condition. They prescribe the
multiset. They do not say that it is the multiset of the jumps of the representation. The assertions
about the actual jumps are in the part AboveB (lines 39–52). They are made with respect to a
local comparison datum (Lean: LocBridge, Pst.Induces), and in two cases only: where the local
component of π is spherical (the representation is then crystalline and de Rham there, with the
predicted jumps), and in a specified local case (the conditions TmW and Sqf with IsQpBig, or Tor,
together with mono0; we did not transcribe them). The classical comparison is with the theorem
stated in the introduction of Chenevier and Harris [CH13] and with the compatibility of local and
global correspondences of Caraiani [Car12], [Car14, Thm. 1.1] (these by the reviewer’s report); they
build on the work of Harris and Taylor and of Shin (from memory). The normalisations and the
hypotheses on the two sides have to be specified before an exact dictionary can be claimed. We have
not done this. In the Lean Row0b is a theorem proved in the archive (galoisLocalGlobalStrong_
recParameter). The theorem vwin_of_row depends only on the three standard axioms of Lean
(propext, Classical.choice, Quot.sound), by the dependency computation. So no statement on
this route is left as an assumption other than Row0b.
    All other inputs named “literature” on this route are theorems proved in the archive. They are
passed through eight structures whose fields are hypotheses. Each structure is built by a term, as
follows.
 (1) OpenImageCondition: one field, Row0b, filled in vwin_of_row. The field is never read: the
     declarations the final theorem depends on contain no projection and no case analysis of this
     structure. In its file 62 theorems take it as an argument and ignore it.
 (2) LiteratureRankNHypothesis: four fields (local, automorphic, cohomological, complex multi-
     plication), filled by the next four structures. The last field is not read.
 (3) LitLocAlt: 18 fields, filled by the term local_inputs_from_literature, each field by a
     theorem of the archive; 11 are read.
 (4) LiteratureAutomorphyHypothesis: 15 fields; 13 are filled by theorems of the archive, one
     by the theorem baseChangeN, and one by the hypothesis Row0b; 10 are read.
 (5) LitCoh: 12 fields, filled by galois_cohomology_from_literature; all are read.
 (6) LiteratureCMHypothesis: 11 fields, filled by cm_abelian_inputs_from_literature; none
     is read.
 (7) HypothesesH’: 15 fields with those of its parent; all but one are read. It is filled by the term
     published_hypotheses_from_patching_alt.
 (8) U3.CohInp: 5 fields, filled by Core0.cohInp_alt; all are read.
The propositions passed as arguments further down (LemmaK, CountI to CountV, CountZ, KatoTwoW,
ThmBab, ThmBcFree, PsiBound, FerreroWashington, SymplecticFourImageDense and others) are
each supplied by a theorem that the final theorem depends on.
    The structure OpenLiteratureStatements is not on this route. It belongs to the other half
of the final theorem, which proves LinkN. It has three fields, and all three are read. Two
of them are filled with the hypothesis Row0b; their names are caraianiLocalGlobalDual and


                                                150
galoisRepOfPolarizedPairLit. The third is filled with the theorem recTExists; its name is

   The last column names related work. It does not say that the statement of the Lean is a
published theorem; for that question see the three lists at the end of the section. CountV has the
shape of CountII, for Case V with a further condition that we did not transcribe.
    The row for the minus part, with its hypotheses (PsiBound, ZeroMemUnrHom.lean:97)
[quoted]. Let E be an abelian field with complex multiplication, and N ≥ 1 a level with a homo-
morphism (Z/N )× → Gal(E/Q) compatible with the action on roots of unity (Lean: IsCycAct).
Let ℓ be an odd prime with ℓ ∤ [E : Q]. Let O be a discrete valuation ring, finite over Zℓ , inside an
algebraic closure of Qℓ , and let vO be its additive valuation, which is 1 on a generator of the maximal
ideal. Let e ≥ 1 and let ψ be a character of Gal(E/Q) with values in O× and ψ(−1) = (−1)e+1 .


                                                      151
Then                                                                              
                       lengthO the group of ψ                                                  (III.260)
                                                                 Y
                                                     ≤ vO N              p · Be+1,ψ ,
                                                                 p≤e+2

with the Bernoulli number of the primitive character. Both sides are in units of length over O; on
Zℓ we have vO = eO vℓ . So PsiBound is an upper bound for a cohomology length in terms of the
valuation of a Bernoulli number with explicit integral factors. It is not a bound for the valuation.
For ψ = 1 the field E is still a field with complex multiplication, so the statement does not apply
to E = Q. To use it over Q one needs a descent, for example from the part of the trivial character
over Q(i); we did not carry this out. Rubin [Rub00, §2.2] gives general bounds from Euler systems
under explicit hypotheses (by the reviewer’s report). The specialisation to the bound by a Bernoulli
number is not supplied here.
    The row for Case I, with its hypotheses (OneCharU_s, NumberU3US.lean:49) [quoted]. Let
K be an imaginary quadratic field, ℓ a prime, e an integer, N a level and ψ a Dirichlet character
modulo N with values in the coefficient ring. Assume the twelve conditions of the record GD
(Counts.lean:9); among them: ℓ =   ̸ 2; e ≥ 1; N is even; ℓ does not divide the order of ψ; ℓ
does not divide the discriminant of K times its class number; the discriminant of K is prime to
N ; ψ(−1) = (−1)e ; and (ℓ − 1) ∤ 2e. Fix an isomorphism ι : Qℓ ≃ C. Call a three-dimensional
representation ρ of Gal(K/K) over Qℓ admissible if it satisfies

                                               or                                              (III.261)
                                                                                   
                       Adm3U_s(ι, e, N, ρ)                Adm2A ι, e, N |disc K|, ρ .

The same ι, e and levels are used in the hypothesis and in the conclusion. Let σ ∈ Gal(K/K) be
such that tr ρ(σ) ̸= Ψ(σ) + 1 + Ψ(σ)−1 for every admissible ρ. Then the length of the group of ψ is
finite, and there is a finite set R of admissible representations, no two of them isomorphic, such
that the normalised length is at most 2 ρ∈R vℓ tr ρ(σ) − Ψ(σ) − 1 − Ψ(σ)−1 . The condition on σ
                                         P                                    

makes every term of the sum finite. For the statement OneChar, which has the same shape, the
conditions Adm3 and Adm2 say in classical terms: ρ is irreducible, automorphic, conjugate self-dual,
of weights (e, 0, −e); or ρ = W ⊕ 1 with W attached to a newform of weight 2e + 1. Whether
Adm3U_s and Adm2A differ from them in substance was not examined. Bellaïche and Chenevier
[BC04, Thm. 1.1] construct an extension in a Selmer group for their characters (by the reviewer’s
report). This illustrates the method of congruences. A uniform upper bound for a length is a
further statement.
    Further inputs, all proved in the archive. LemmaK (LemmaK.lean:13): for every prime
ℓ, every totally complex field L containing the ℓth roots of unity (the fourth roots if ℓ = 2) and
every e ≥ 1, the group H 2 (GL,ℓ , Zℓ (e + 1)) is finite and the logarithm of its order is at most
exp (2e[L : Q])C log(2|disc L|) . So LemmaK bounds the whole group, for every ℓ, and it includes
ℓ = 2. Finiteness is due to Soulé ([Sou79], from memory). A quantitative bound away from small
primes is in [Sou03]: Theorem 2 there discards the torsion at small primes, and Theorem 3 bounds
the groups H 2 (OL [1/p], Zp (n′ )) in a product over the primes p ≥ 4n′ − 1, p ≥ [L : Q] + 2 only. Here
n′ is the twist, which Soulé writes n; it is not the n of this section. These two statements of [Sou03]
were compared with the article by a reviewer, not by us. We have not identified a published estimate
for LemmaK in its full small-prime range. The proof of LemmaK in the Lean names a statement
on the rank of the homology of GLs (OL ) (BOdd; Borel’s theorem). FerreroWashington: for an
abelian field L containing those roots of unity, the ℓ-rank of the class group of L(ζℓr ) is bounded
independently of r; it is proved in the archive. In the cyclotomic Zℓ -tower, a bounded ℓ-rank of the
class groups is equivalent to µ = 0 [Was82, Prop. 13.23] (first edition, p. 284); so this is the theorem
of [FW79] (from memory) in its form with bounded rank. The theorems of Poitou and Tate, Tate’s
local duality and Euler characteristic, the formula of Greenberg and Wiles, and the local theorems


                                                    152
of Bloch and Kato are collected in the structure LitCoh. KatoTwoW (KatoTwoW.lean:10–14): let
W be a two-dimensional representation of Gal(Q/Q) over Qℓ and θ a character, such that the twist
of W by χ−eℓ is attached to a newform of weight 2e + 1 (the attachment hypothesis), and assume
the hypotheses Used and WitAQ of the source, which we did not transcribe. Then the two rational
Selmer spaces of Bloch and Kato, of W and of W ⊗ θ, vanish: every cocycle with values in the
vector space over Qℓ that satisfies the local conditions is a coboundary. Kato [Kat04, §14.1 and
Thm. 14.2(1)] proves that the discrete Selmer groups are finite at the critical twists other than
the central one (by the reviewer’s report). The two statements are of different kinds: the Lean
statement is about vector spaces, and it bounds no integral length. In our reading the relevant
point is s = e + 1, on the edge of the critical strip.
   The window. The statement ABound (SecondBound.lean:19–25; line 25 is its second clause)
explains it. Let ℓ = 2 or ℓ ∈ B, let L0 be a fixed base field and R ≥ 0 an integer. Put

                                       j0 = 3 + ⌊logℓ max(1, R)⌋                                 (III.262)

(Lean: jZero; the logarithm of the Lean is 0 at R = 0). The first clause of ABound says: there are
an integer c0 ≥ 0 such that L0 contains a primitive root of unity of order ℓc0 , an integer R′ with
0 ≤ R′ ≤ R, and points s1 , . . . , sR′ of a field containing Qℓ , with a non-archimedean absolute value
| · |ℓ extending that of Qℓ , with this property. We call the si the exceptional centres. Let e ≥ 1, let
ϑ ≥ 0 be an integer, and let L1 be a layer of degree ℓϑ over L0 in the sense of the Lean (IsLayer,
StmtFields.lean:47): a number field L1 = L0 (ζℓk ) for some k ≥ 0, with [L1 : L0 ] = ℓϑ . Let hℓ,e
be the length (III.251) for the field L1 . Then for every integer ϱ ≥ 0

          hℓ,e ≤ R′ max(ϑ, j0 ) + c0 + ϱ           whenever |si − e|ℓ ≥ ℓ−ϱ for all i.           (III.263)
                                             


If L0 contains the ℓth roots of unity (the fourth roots if ℓ = 2), a layer in this sense is the field
of degree ℓϑ over L0 in the cyclotomic Zℓ -extension of L0 ; this is the case in the uses TwoIn and
FixedIn. The statement ABound itself does not assume it, since c0 may be 0. Without it a layer
need not lie in the cyclotomic Zℓ -extension: for ℓ = 2 and L0 = Q the field Q(i) is a layer with
ϑ = 1, and it is not in the cyclotomic Z2 -extension of Q. A second clause (SecondBound) compares
a layer of degree ℓϑ with a layer of degree ℓj0 , with a loss R · max(ϑ − j0 , 0); we do not transcribe it.
By (III.263) the lengths are small unless e is ℓ-adically very close to one of at most R exceptional
centres. The statement defines its conclusion by the distances from e to the centres. It does not
say what the centres are. Iwasawa’s theory gives a statement of this shape when µ = 0: a module
with λ ≤ R, and the zeros of its characteristic power series in the place of the centres. The Lean
statement does not identify the centres with such zeros in the coordinate e, and we did not check
this in the proof. For the exceptional e only LemmaK is available, and it is useful only if e is at most
a small power of x. The window is an interval of x in which no exceptional e lies between that
small power and x401 ; the number J of Theorem G grows with R. This paragraph is our reading of
the statements ABound, FirstBound and ThmBab; the proof was not read.
    Three claims of earlier notes, compared with the Lean statements. (1) “The minus
part is a Stickelberger index bound.” The statement is a bound of a length by the valuation of a gener-
alised Bernoulli number, as in the table; summed over ℓ it is bounded by [E : Q] log 16N e+2 (e + 1)!
                                                                                                     

(PrimeBound). Its proof passes through a reciprocity statement (Rec) deduced from the duality of
Poitou and Tate, and the files that prove it are named after Kolyvagin’s derivative construction
and Jacobi sums. A cruder bound of Stickelberger type (StickBound) is used only for ℓ | [E : Q].
(2) “The plus part uses congruences on U (3) in the manner of Ribet.” Confirmed for Case I at
the level of the statement OneCharU_s. The inequality bounds a cohomology group from above
by a congruence number. Ribet’s method produces classes; an upper bound of this kind needs in

                                                   153
addition a comparison of a deformation ring with a Hecke algebra, and the Lean names such
inputs (adjoint_lifting_of_cohomology, published_hypotheses_from_patching_alt). (3)
“Prismatic F -crystals are needed because the local fields at 2 have unbounded degree.” The archive
contains the statement BhattScholzeIntegral: every lattice in a crystalline representation of the
Galois group of any finite extension of Qℓ comes from a prismatic F -crystal. Bhatt and Scholze
[BS23, Thms. 1.2 and 5.6] give the equivalence of prismatic F -crystals with the finite free Zp -lattices
whose rationalisations are crystalline (by the reviewer’s report). By the dependency computation
it belongs to the half that proves LinkN, not to the proof of Theorem G. The completion of E
at a prime above 2 has ramification index n and residue degree 2, so its degree 2n is unbounded;
the theory of Fontaine and Laffaille needs an unramified field and small weights. So the claim is
consistent with the statements. That the result is needed cannot be read off from statements.

     Minkowski’s bound: every ideal class of F contains an integral ideal of norm at most 12 d. This
is [Neu99, Ch. I, §6, Exercise 3] for a field of degree 2 with two real places. It is an exercise there,

                                                  154
so we give the proof from two statements of the text, (5.2) and √     (4.4). Let a be a nonzero integral
ideal. Its image under α 7→ (α, α′ ) is a lattice in R2 of covolume d N (a) [Neu99, Ch. I, (5.2)]. For
T > 0 the set |x| + |y| ≤ T is convex and symmetric, of area 2T 2 . Minkowski’s lattice √  point theorem
[Neu99, Ch. I, (4.4)] says that it contains a nonzero point of the lattice if 2T 2 > 4 d N (a). The
inequality is strict there. (The case of equality for compact √ sets is the last sentence of Exercise 2 of
§4 there, without proof; we do not use it.) Take T = 2 d N (a). For every T > T the theorem
                                                        2                             ′

gives a nonzero lattice point with |x| + |y| ≤ T ′ . The lattice has only finitely many points with
|x| + |y| ≤ 2T . So one of them, nonzero, serves for a sequence of numbers T ′ decreasing to T . We
get α ∈ a, α ̸= 0, with |α| + |α′ | ≤ T , hence
                                                    2             √
                             |αα′ | ≤ 14 |α| + |α′ | ≤ 14 T 2 = 12 d N (a).                      (III.265)

Now let an ideal class be given, and let a be an integral ideal in the inverse class. The√ ideal (α)a
                                                                                                     −1

is integral, because α ∈ a. It lies in the given class, and its norm is |αα |/N (a) ≤ 2 d.
                                                                           ′           1

    Counting: for j ≥ 1, the number of integral ideals of F of norm j is at most j. An ideal of
norm j is in one way a product, over the primes p dividing j, of ideals whose norms are the powers
of p in j. So let j = pk . If p splits, (p) = pp′ , the ideals of norm pk are the k + 1 ideals pk1 p′ k2
with k1 + k2 = k. If p is inert, there is one such ideal for even k and none for odd k. If p ramifies,
(p) = p2 , there is one, pk . In each case the number is at most k + 1, the number of divisors of pk .
Hence the number of ideals of norm j is at most the number of positive divisors of j, which is at
most j.

    Corollaries. (a) Let e ≥ 1 be odd. If ℓ does not divide the numerator of Be+1 /(e + 1), then
Shaℓ (Q, e) = 0. (b) Let e ≥ 2 be even and (ℓ − 1) | 2e. Then Shaℓ (Q, e) = 0. (c) Let e ≥ 2 be even.
If ℓ does not divide the class number of Q(ζℓ + ζℓ−1 ), then Shaℓ (Q, e) = 0.
   Proof. We use this fact: if F ⊂ Q(ζℓ ) is a subfield, then the ℓ-part of the class group of F is the
part of A fixed by Gal(Q(ζℓ )/F ). Indeed, write L = Q(ζℓ ) in this proof, let j : Cl(F ) → Cl(L) be

                     N ◦ j = [L : F ] on Cl(F ),     j◦N =                 σ on Cl(L).          (III.267)

The degree [L : F ] is prime to ℓ. By the first identity j is injective on the ℓ-part. By the second, a
class z of A fixed by Gal(L/F ) satisfies j(N (z)) = [L : F ] z, so it lies in the image of j; and the
image of j is fixed by Gal(L/F ). (a) Here i ≡ −e is odd. If (ℓ − 1) | (e + 1) then i ≡ 1 and A(1) = 0
[Was82, Prop. 6.16]. Otherwise take 3 ≤ i ≤ ℓ − 2. By Herbrand’s theorem [Was82, Thm. 6.17],
A(i) = 0 unless ℓ | Bℓ−i ; and Bℓ−i /(ℓ − i) ≡ Be+1 /(e + 1) (mod ℓ) by Kummer’s congruence [Was82,

                                                   155

the class group of Q, by the fact above. Otherwise (ℓ − 1)/2 divides e and is even, so ℓ ≡ 1 (mod 4)
and i ≡ (ℓ − 1)/2. The

    Kummer’s congruence, as used in (a), was also tested numerically by the test group G12:
Br /r ≡ Br′ /r′ (mod ℓ) for the 2543 triples (ℓ, r, r′ ) with ℓ prime, 5 ≤ ℓ < 200, r and r′ even,
2 ≤ r ≤ ℓ − 3, r < r′ ≤ 220 and r ≡ r′ (mod ℓ − 1); in 14 of them ℓ divides Br (script
t03_bernoulli.py, part G, in zeta-paper_tests/displays/G12). The test group G19 dropped
the condition r ≤ ℓ − 3 and required r ̸≡ 0 (mod ℓ − 1) instead: the congruence holds for all 9738
such triples.
     What is proved here, and what the Lean states. Everything proved in this subsection is about
the groups Shaℓ (E, e) of (III.251). Two identifications with the Lean are assumed and not proved.
The first is the transcription of §1: that Sha and shaLen of the Lean are the group and the length of
(III.251). It was compared at the level of the definitions (the same set Sℓ , the same twist χ−e
                                                                                              ℓ , discrete
coefficients Qℓ /Zℓ , continuous cocycles, the kernel of the restrictions to chosen decomposition groups;
shaLen_eq_shaInv removes the choices). Under it, the vanishing of Sha5 (Q(i), 4) proved below says
that shaLen is 0 for Q(i), ℓ = 5, e = 4. The second concerns PropZ, which says: for ℓ ≥ 5 and even
e ≥ 2 with (ℓ − 1) | 2e, the number Chr.V of the trivial character is 0. Corollary (b) is, for ℓ ≥ 5,
this statement, if the group Chr.V of the Lean is identified with Shaℓ (Q, e) with enlarged coefficients.
At the level of the definitions this is so: Chr.V is the length of the group of classes that vanish
on the decomposition groups above ℓ and on all inertia groups (ShaFamMono.lean:30–32). The
cohomology of the Lean is continuous cohomology: H1U is defined by restricting the representation
to the subgroup (RestrictionMemInvariantH1.lean:35), and the group H1 is the quotient of the
continuous cocycles by the coboundaries (SeparatedDvrSecondCohomologyMem.lean:42–66). A
full identification of the coefficients and of the local conditions of the Lean with those of (III.251)
was not checked. So Corollary (b) is a proof of the classical statement, and it is a proof of PropZ
only under this identification. The source proves PropZ independently (X.propZ). Corollary (a) is
proved from Herbrand’s theorem and does not use PsiBound. As said in §4, PsiBound with ψ = 1
needs a field with complex multiplication, so it gives a statement over Q only after a descent. The
precise length over Q, vℓ (Be+1 /(e + 1)) for (ℓ − 1) ∤ (e + 1), is the theorem of Mazur and Wiles
([MW84], from memory).
    The field Q(i). For odd ℓ, Shaℓ (Q(i), e) is the sum of the part on which complex conjugation
ȷ acts as +1 and the part on which it acts as −1. The first is Shaℓ (Q, e), and the second is the part
of the character χ4 modulo 4 (restriction and corestriction; not proved here). For odd e the first is
the minus part and the second the plus part; for even e it is the other way round. The minus part

    A prime that divides the Bernoulli number while the group is zero. Take e = 4 and
ℓ = 5. The prime 5 divides the numerator of B5,χ4 /5 = − 52 . The reason is an Euler factor. Let
L5 (s, χ4 ω) be the 5-adic L-function. For r ≡ 1 (mod 4) its value at 1 − r is −(1 − χ4 (5)5r−1 )Br,χ4 /r
[Was82, Thm. 5.11]. At r = 1 this is 0, because χ4 (5) = 1. All its values at integers are congruent
modulo 5 [Was82, Cor. 5.13]. So 5 divides the numerator of Be+1,χ4 /(e + 1) for every e ≡ 0 (mod 4),
e ≥ 4. Consistently, the computation finds the factor 5 for every e ≡ 0 (mod 4) with e ≤ 24 and
for no e ≡ 2 (mod 4) in that range.
   The group Sha5 (Q(i), 4) is nevertheless 0; in particular its part on which ȷ acts as −1 is 0.
We prove this. The proof uses local and global class field theory in their standard form and
one computation modulo 25. It is for this field and this twist only. Put E = Q(i), G = GE,5 ,
W = W4 = Q5 /Z5 (−4) and χ = χ5 . For a subgroup H write Hom(H, F5 ) for the continuous
homomorphisms.
   Step 1: the invariants. The prime 5 splits in E. So S5 = {v, v ′ } and Ev = Ev′ = Q5 . The
character χ maps G onto Z× 5 , because E is ramified only at 2 and every subfield ̸= Q of the field of
5-power roots of unity is ramified at 5. It maps each of Gv , Gv′ onto Z×5 , because the cyclotomic
character of Q5 is onto. An element w ∈ W is fixed if and only if (u − 1)w = 0 for all u ∈ Z×
